**Counterevidence/rival:** The authors themselves call for more comprehensive model validation; contemporary works modify flows.

**Inference limit:** Numerical experiment is not an ancient performance series or a causal explanation of two millennia.

## ANC-K19

Dholavira's excavated water system combined storage, diversion and drainage; the ICOMOS phase narrative locates major reservoirs in an expansion episode rather than one timeless configuration.

**Cases:** ANC-C06. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ANC-S13: Dholavira: A Harappan City (India), No 1645—Advisory Body Evaluation](https://whc.unesco.org/document/189324), Description and history, printed pp. 14–15. Supports: Structures and staged construction.
- [ANC-S12: Archaeological studies at Dholavira using GPR](https://doi.org/10.18520/cs/v114/i04/879-887), pp. 879–880, 884–886. Supports: Existing reservoirs distinguish excavated evidence from new GPR hypotheses.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Material configuration and attributed phasing, not identification of a cause of urban growth.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Detailed construction chronology requires the excavation archive; some proposed structures remain unexcavated.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish precise population served, ownership or water reliability.

## ANC-K20

The Dholavira GPR study proposes reservoirs or bunds and possible flood damage, explicitly requiring excavation to confirm those interpretations.

**Cases:** ANC-C06. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [ANC-S12: Archaeological studies at Dholavira using GPR](https://doi.org/10.18520/cs/v114/i04/879-887), Archaeological interpretations and Conclusion, pp. 884–886; Fig. 9. Supports: Alternative feature identifications and explicit excavation requirement.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Flood damages water-control walls -> walls disappear and rubble accumulates -> particular radar signature.
- **strongest rival:** Other collapse, dismantling or fill processes could create comparable reflections.
- **discriminating observation:** Excavated structural relationships, dated flood sediments and identifiable displaced masonry.
- **evidence sought found:** ANC-S12 reports geophysical features and alternative interpretations, not excavation confirmation.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Radar does not directly determine function, age or destruction mechanism.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Similar reflections and rubble can have different structural or depositional explanations.

**Inference limit:** No dated ancient flood or causal link to city-wide decline is established.

## ANC-K21

ICOMOS reports unrestored water-structure damage and occupation gaps at Dholavira; this limits a narrative of continuous urban service but does not identify the cause of declining use.

**Cases:** ANC-C06. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ANC-S13: Dholavira: A Harappan City (India), No 1645—Advisory Body Evaluation](https://whc.unesco.org/document/189324), Description and history, Stages V–VII, printed p. 15. Supports: Unrestored damage, contraction and gaps in the phase narrative.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Attributes phased observations without converting association into a causal explanation.

**Counterevidence/rival:** A service may continue at reduced scale, and alternative water arrangements may leave little evidence.

**Inference limit:** No warranted inference that water failure caused rather than followed settlement change.

## ANC-K22

These cases show why cessation of an installation, substitution of its service, and loss of technical knowledge must be tested separately; survival of a named system likewise does not establish unchanged operation.

**Cases:** ANC-C02; ANC-C03; ANC-C04; ANC-C05; ANC-C06. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [ANC-S04: Operation and decline of the Barbegal mill complex, the largest industrial complex of antiquity](https://doi.org/10.1002/gea.22016), §6. Supports: Staged local cessation.
- [ANC-S07: Wars, Book V (Gothic War I), chapter 19](https://penelope.uchicago.edu/Thayer/E/Roman/Texts/Procopius/Wars/5C%2A.html), V.19.19–28. Supports: Reported service substitution.
- [ANC-S08: Qanāt and Falaj: Polycentric and Multi-Period Innovations: Iran and the United Arab Emirates as Case Studies](https://www.academia.edu/124000172/Boucharlat_R_2017_Ancient_qan%C4%81t_and_falaj_polycentric_and_multi_period_innovations_Iran_and_the_United_Arab_Emirates_as_case_studies_in_A_N_Angelakis_E_Chiotis_S_Eslamian_H_Weingartner_eds_Underground_Aqueducts_Handbook_CRC_Press_Boca_Raton_FL_279_301), §17.2.2. Supports: Adaptation and abandonment.
- [ANC-S10: Dujiangyan's Annual Repair System in Ancient Times: From Jianpengyan Inscription by Shu Defender Li Bing in Qin Dynasty (古代都江堰岁修制度——从《秦蜀守李冰湔堋堰官碑》说起)](https://www.sohu.com/a/255708893_523159), §§1–2. Supports: Chronological uncertainty in maintenance.
- [ANC-S13: Dholavira: A Harappan City (India), No 1645—Advisory Body Evaluation](https://whc.unesco.org/document/189324), printed pp. 14–15. Supports: Changing settlement/service context.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Loss or change of a local complement -> installation ceases or service relocates -> material disappearance without necessary erasure of know-how.
- **strongest rival:** Skilled communities or transmission may themselves disappear, causing installation failure.
- **discriminating observation:** Trace practitioners, texts and equivalent working installations alongside independently dated service cessation.
- **evidence sought found:** Selected ANC cases contain differing terminal sequences and alternatives; no complete knowledge-transmission series was assembled.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Material and knowledge losses may reinforce each other; bounded alternatives disprove inevitability, not all loss.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some episodes may genuinely include skill loss; archaeological disappearance can also coincide with institutional collapse.

**Inference limit:** Purposive cases do not estimate the frequency of any mechanism or demonstrate universal continuity.

## CTX-K01

Eyferth’s post-1949 investigation records continued household textile work in three Guanzhong villages: Gedatou in Zhouzhi, and Danbei and Zhangli in Xingping. Its fieldwork comprised about 200 interviews with more than eighty villagers, mostly men and women born in 1920–1945, over fourteen weeks during five visits in 2005–2011.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), pp.379–381; pp.384–389 for recalled collective-era work. Supports: Names sites, interview scope and population, then reports work histories.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Records the population and observed practice; assigns no causal share to persistence.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The author explicitly acknowledges that one village cannot represent China and presents extension to other cotton regions as a hypothesis. Later photographs distinguish a spinning demonstration and niche weaving.

**Inference limit:** No probability sampling frame, sex-specific interview count, national prevalence or annual adoption series. This is retrospective local evidence; 2011–2012 photographs are not a measurement of collective-era diffusion.

## CTX-K02

In Eyferth’s Zhouzhi account, cloth-ration entitlement did not ensure purchased factory cloth: informants attributed coupon resale and continued home production to scarce disposable cash, with village cadres and newly married couples described as exceptions to the prevailing practice.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), p.377 for 1954 procurement/rationing; pp.386–387 for Zhouzhi use and cash. Supports: Distinguishes coupon access from buying cloth and reports resale, household resources and exceptions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Procurement/income arrangements and essential purchases leave little cash -> households sell coupons and avoid cloth spending -> home production supplies clothing despite industrial availability.
- **strongest rival:** Durability, ritual use or custom could sustain homespun even with adequate purchasing power; low physical rations could also cause home production.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare redemption and home labour across similar households or within the same household after cash changes, holding cloth entitlement, service quality and family needs as constant as possible.
- **evidence sought found:** Read CTX-S01 pp.377 and 386–387: explicit cash explanation and coupon resale, with named social exceptions. No matched household panel or affordability intervention found in the article.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Interviews favour constrained access over preference alone, but prices, quantities, cloth qualities and obligations vary together; relative contributions remain unidentified.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis responding to P2-SP03

**Counterevidence/rival:** Rations could themselves fall below needs; homespun had reported durability and social uses. Better-dressed cadres and newlyweds are not matched households and had different roles and needs.

**Inference limit:** Supports an actor-level access pathway, not a national preference claim, a measured price elasticity or a counterfactual effect of abolishing the policy. The numerical household budget is an author’s illustrative reconstruction, not a sampled mean; it is not reproduced as a CTX estimate.

## CTX-K03

The Guanzhong accounts describe women meeting household clothing duties through unpaid textile work alongside collective agricultural obligations, often at the expense of rest. This supports a pathway in which continued household production was sustained by shifting time costs onto its women operators.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), pp.385–389, especially pp.388–389 and notes79–82. Supports: Connects collective work, family expectations, blame, night work and recalled exhaustion.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Collective labour demands plus household clothing obligations -> women reconcile competing claims with additional domestic work -> clothing is supplied while operators bear unremunerated time and rest costs.
- **strongest rival:** Poverty or clothing quality might motivate extra work independently of gendered authority; rewarding skilled activity could explain some time spent at the loom.
- **discriminating observation:** Separate household time diaries and bargaining accounts, including households with shared textile duties or purchasable substitutes, would test who could shift costs and refuse them.
- **evidence sought found:** Read CTX-S01 pp.388–389: differentiated household duties, late textile work, reported exhaustion and blame, alongside accounts of negotiation and sale.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Accounts directly locate burdens but do not isolate gendered authority from scarcity, family size, skill or preferences; prevalence and causal magnitude are not measured.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis responding to P2-SP03

**Counterevidence/rival:** Women negotiated, traded and sometimes held leadership positions; both textile skill and public work could bring recognition. The source does not show every household bargain or compare all male and female tasks.

**Inference limit:** No quantified aggregate labour transfer, national time budget, coercion rate or net welfare balance. This is not evidence of a state mandate to hand-weave: official policy opposed that activity while other obligations narrowed alternatives.

## CTX-K04

Reported durability and the social uses of textiles complicate a comparison based on metres of cloth alone. These observations leave room for useful task advantages of homespun, but do not establish that ritual textiles had to be handmade or that operators freely preferred the work.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), pp.383–384 for exchanges and grave clothes; p.386 and note76 for interview-based cloth needs; p.382 captions for later niche. Supports: Documents differentiated cloth services and an author estimate reflecting reported wear.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cloth attributes and local social uses -> differing value for specific services -> possible persistence of selected hand-production tasks.
- **strongest rival:** Restricted cash and household authority can produce the same observed persistence even when a factory substitute could perform the task adequately.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare equivalent warmth, wear, repair and social acceptability at affordable prices, allowing the operator meaningful refusal; persistence after those constraints relax would strengthen the task-advantage account.
- **evidence sought found:** Read CTX-S01 pp.382–386: reported product/service distinctions, but no matched product trial or claim that all gifts necessarily require handmade cloth.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Useful service and constrained access are compatible; the source cannot allocate their weights.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis responding to P2-SP03

**Counterevidence/rival:** Factory cloth was used by some people and ration coupons were sold for cash. Social duties could impose obligations rather than represent autonomous preference. Material quality was not measured experimentally.

**Inference limit:** No controlled wear test, all-purpose technical ranking or causal contribution of ritual demand. The local subsistence estimates cannot be treated as objective universal clothing requirements.

## CTX-K05

Retained and diverted cotton, local distributions and some black-market cloth production offered routes around formal procurement constraints in the Guanzhong account. Reports of commercial weaving coexist with reports that its risk and required absence from collective work made it inaccessible to many interviewees.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), pp.387–388; p.377 for self-retained cotton. Supports: Describes cotton sources, concealed distributions, trading and different assessments of commercial risk.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cotton retained, diverted or exchanged outside formal channels -> inputs for home production and occasional sales -> clothing or cash despite official restrictions.
- **strongest rival:** Commercial reports may be selected exceptional accounts; formal rations or gifts might have supplied more clothing than narrators recall.
- **discriminating observation:** Link dated team cotton accounts and household output/sales to independently recorded access and enforcement, comparing sellers and nonsellers.
- **evidence sought found:** Read CTX-S01 pp.387–388: several distinct input routes and admissions of sales, but also danger/absence constraints and disagreement between farmers and former leaders about pilfering.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The channels are documented narratively; their size, selection and effect on bargaining remain unknown.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis responding to P2-SP03

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some interviewees, including a former local Women’s Federation chairwoman, sold cloth. Self-provision was not the only use of household output, and local leaders were not uniformly enforcers of central restrictions.

**Inference limit:** No fraction of all households able to trade, audited profitability or proof of women’s control of proceeds. Commercial anecdotes cannot establish a generally accessible independent occupation.

## CTX-K06

The evidence does not support a uniform loss-of-agency account: Eyferth records appreciation of changed marital status and some women’s public prominence, while Hershatter describes broadened rural social worlds and labour-model visibility alongside continued unequal burdens.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), pp.384, 386, 388. Supports: Reports attraction to marital reform, differentiated activism and interviewees’ gratitude about status.
- [CTX-S03: Women and China’s Socialist Construction, 1949–78](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/D3FF69916AB8B12A27073722AF080C6F/S1557466019015365a.pdf/women-and-chinas-socialist-construction-1949-78.pdf), pp.13–14; notes41/43 at p.27. Supports: Describes social encounters and public roles during mobilisation while retaining domestic and remuneration inequalities.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Records counterevidence to an undifferentiated characterisation of actors, without estimating the causal or welfare balance of political change.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Public recognition was selective and could require exceptional effort; gratitude about marriage is not evidence of adequate material provision or free consent to every work demand.

**Inference limit:** Different outcomes and populations cannot be combined into a net welfare verdict. Hershatter’s broader discussion is not a new matched study of the three Eyferth villages; shared scholarship is disclosed.

## CTX-K07

Compared with IND-C07’s pre-1949 hybrid-yarn configuration, this post-1949 episode adds observed access and unpaid-labour pathways to persistence. Continued task performance alone cannot establish technical preference, voluntary participation or a desirable paid occupation.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [CTX-S01: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/womens-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980.pdf), pp.373–374 compared with pp.386–389. Supports: Contrasts historical background on hybrid inputs with original local findings on access and competing labour demands.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Different feasible choices and different control of labour -> similar visible persistence -> an outcome label alone leaves mechanism and livelihood quality unidentified.
- **strongest rival:** A task-fit explanation could remain sufficient for some earlier households or later niches; the later access evidence does not show it was absent.
- **discriminating observation:** Observe comparable households with equivalent accessible substitutes and operator control, then compare continuing production, compensation and exits.
- **evidence sought found:** Reopened CTX-S01 pp.373–374 and 386–389; read IND-C07/K20–K22/X03. The later account contains concrete cash, coupon and work-allocation evidence absent from the earlier case’s selected period.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The countercase defeats an inference from persistence alone to desirable work; it does not estimate the share of persistence caused by each pathway or invalidate the earlier hybrid configuration.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis responding to P2-SP03

**Counterevidence/rival:** Useful product properties, skills and commercial niches remain possible contributors. IND-C07 already limits claims about voluntary preference; the new evidence extends selection rather than refuting its hybrid observation.

**Inference limit:** Same article as IND-S15, different temporal episodes and evidence bases. No independent replication, pooled prevalence or projection of post-1949 institutions backward. This is an inference about what persistence can establish, not a forecast of present-day repair employment.

## CTX-K08

Hershatter’s account distinguishes early PRC support for some women’s textile cooperatives from the narrowing of household selling opportunities and the Great Leap’s attempted collective provision of domestic services. Policy towards women’s work was not one unchanging programme across 1949–1980.

**Cases:** CTX-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [CTX-S03: Women and China’s Socialist Construction, 1949–78](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/D3FF69916AB8B12A27073722AF080C6F/S1557466019015365a.pdf/women-and-chinas-socialist-construction-1949-78.pdf), p.12; pp.16–18; notes40/41/50 at p.27. Supports: Dates distinct institutional arrangements and attempted reorganisation of domestic work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Distinguishes named policy episodes and limits a uniform-period interpretation; does not infer implementation success.

**Counterevidence/rival:** An initiative’s stated aim does not establish reliable services or release from domestic duties. Later accounts still describe unpaid household work, and the Great Leap episode is not a successful alternative established by this supplement.

**Inference limit:** A periodisation check from a broader synthesis, not an adoption-rate or implementation-effect estimate in Eyferth’s three villages. Note40 shares Eyferth2015; no claim of independent corroboration of household cloth access.

## DIG-K01

The 1989 CERN proposal specified remote access, heterogeneous computers, decentralised linking and access to existing information; text on 24-by-80 screens was initially sufficient and elaborate security/accounting was not a primary local requirement.

**Cases:** DIG-C01. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S01: Information Management: A Proposal](https://www.w3.org/History/1989/proposal.html), CERN Requirements; Non requirements. Supports: Author’s explicit design priorities.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Later consumer uses could require very different interfaces and protection.

**Inference limit:** A proposal establishes intent, not successful deployment or the causes of mass diffusion.

## DIG-K02

CERN released specified Web components to the public domain in April 1993 and later used permissive royalty-free licensing; this removed a reuse-permission barrier, but its contribution to later diffusion is not identified here.

**Cases:** DIG-C01. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [DIG-S02: Licensing the Web](https://home.cern/science/computing/the-birth-of-the-web/licensing-web/), Public releases of Web software; Open Source release; The first CERN Open Source software licence. Supports: Dates and terms of releases, with retrospective rationale.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Reuse permission -> implementers can copy/modify code without licensing payment -> potentially lower entry cost -> wider deployment.
- **strongest rival:** Network growth and browser/interface improvement could drive the same observed expansion.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare otherwise similar implementers facing different effective licensing barriers before/after release.
- **evidence sought found:** Read DIG-S01 design requirements and DIG-S02 release history; found legal change but no controlled uptake comparison.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Cannot rank licence change against technical or demand complements.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Working networks, usable interfaces and content could explain diffusion despite permissive licensing being present.

**Inference limit:** Original signed release not separately acquired; no matched diffusion counterfactual.

## DIG-K03

The Census historical CPS series reports computers in about 8% of US households in 1984, 51% in 2000 and 79% in 2015; household internet use was 18% in 1997 and 73% in 2015.

**Cases:** DIG-C02. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S03: Computer and Internet Use in the United States: 2015 (ACS-37)](https://www.census.gov/content/dam/Census/library/publications/2017/acs/acs-37.pdf), p.2 Computer and Internet Use over Time; Figure 1 p.3. Supports: CPS series and cross-survey warning.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** ACS gives different levels because questions, weighting and collection differ.

**Inference limit:** Rounded household estimates; no individual-use rate or causal speed comparison across technologies.

## DIG-K04

In the 2015 ACS, 78% of US households had desktop/laptop access, 75% handheld-computer access and 77% broadband; 62% had all three, showing household-level coexistence.

**Cases:** DIG-C02. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S03: Computer and Internet Use in the United States: 2015 (ACS-37)](https://www.census.gov/content/dam/Census/library/publications/2017/acs/acs-37.pdf), p.2 Highlights; Tables 1–2; footnote 1. Supports: Device/subscription categories and joint high-connectivity indicator.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Household coexistence could include disused equipment or different people rather than task complementarity for one person.

**Inference limit:** Broadband includes mobile; handheld includes more than smartphones. This is access/ownership, not active usage.

## DIG-K05

In early 2015, 33% of UK internet users aged 16+ named smartphones and 30% laptops as their most important internet-access device.

**Cases:** DIG-C03. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S04: Communications Market Report 2015: Internet and online content](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/cmr/cmr15/uk_5.pdf?v=322209), pp.346–347, Figure 5.20, QE 11/QE 40; n=3095. Supports: Question, denominator and levels.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Older respondents selected computers more often.

**Inference limit:** Self-reported importance is not ownership, share of tasks or minutes, and does not isolate pocketability.

## DIG-K06

Ofcom reported tablet availability in 54% of UK households in 2015; among internet users aged 55+, laptops (36%), desktops (27%) and tablets (22%) were the leading most-important-device choices.

**Cases:** DIG-C03. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S04: Communications Market Report 2015: Internet and online content](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/cmr/cmr15/uk_5.pdf?v=322209), pp.341–344 Figures 5.14, 5.17; pp.346–347 Figure 5.20. Supports: Household tablet measure and age-specific preferences.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Device choice differs by age, resources and tasks; broad smartphone growth did not imply no use of alternatives.

**Inference limit:** The household tablet denominator differs from the age-specific internet-user denominator; no common device price comparison.

## DIG-K07

Pew’s November 2014 surveys found smartphone ownership among 64% of US adults, against 35% in 2011; 7% of adults had both no home broadband and few alternative ways to get online.

**Cases:** DIG-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S05: The Smartphone Difference / U.S. Smartphone Use in 2015](https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/wp-content/uploads/sites/9/2015/03/PI_Smartphones_0401151.pdf), pp.2–3; Smartphone dependence section pp.16–18. Supports: Ownership history and conjunctive definition.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** A broader measure gives 19% experiencing at least one constraint, and should not be called the same dependent group.

**Inference limit:** Late-2014 observations published in 2015; adult measure, not households or every form of smartphone-only access.

## DIG-K08

Among Pew’s narrowly defined smartphone-dependent users in its 2014 research, 48% reported having previously suspended service because of financial hardship.

**Cases:** DIG-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S05: The Smartphone Difference / U.S. Smartphone Use in 2015](https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/wp-content/uploads/sites/9/2015/03/PI_Smartphones_0401151.pdf), p.4 summary and p.19. Supports: Financial-suspension response and dependence definition.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Dependent users still used smartphones; this does not show they would prefer computers if unconstrained.

**Inference limit:** Past episode with no fixed recent window; observational self-report, not current disconnected share.

## DIG-K09

In the 2023 Ofcom Core Survey, 68% of UK smartphone users aged 16+ agreed that completing forms and working on documents was harder on their phone than on a laptop or desktop.

**Cases:** DIG-C03. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S15: Adults’ Media Literacy Core Survey 2023 Data Tables](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/data/statistics/2024/adults-media-literacy-tracker/adults-media-literacy-core-survey-2023-data-tables?v=330749), Table 70 M 6A, pp.292–294; unweighted base 3351. Supports: Combined agreement percentage and question.
- [DIG-S16: Adults’ Media Literacy Research 2023 Technical Report](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/data/statistics/2024/adults-media-literacy-tracker/adults-media-literacy-tracker-2023-technical-report?v=330751), pp.7–8. Supports: Adult weighting, sample and interview methods.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Difficulty can coexist with useful portable access; the table does not measure objective task time or ownership of alternatives.

**Inference limit:** Perceived task difficulty in a mixed quota sample. Cannot be used as a whole-market ergonomic ranking.

## DIG-K10

India’s official compilation reports nominal average wireless-data revenue realisation falling from ₹268.97/GB in calendar 2014 to ₹9.12/GB in FY 2023–24, while monthly data revenue per data subscriber rose from ₹71.25 to ₹211.36.

**Cases:** DIG-C04. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S06: Telecom Statistics India-2024](https://www.dot.gov.in/static/uploads/2025/07/f44e3a674c85aa98cd6fa881e4462b57.pdf), Table 6.2 p.61 and period/source footnote. Supports: Both series and calendar/financial-year distinction.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Higher usage and changing customer composition can accompany lower per-unit revenue.

**Inference limit:** Not list prices, household bills or a causal effect of competition; no inflation or income adjustment.

## DIG-K11

At March 2024 the official Indian compilation recorded 111.75 urban and 44.16 rural internet subscriptions per 100 inhabitants.

**Cases:** DIG-C04. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S06: Telecom Statistics India-2024](https://www.dot.gov.in/static/uploads/2025/07/f44e3a674c85aa98cd6fa881e4462b57.pdf), Table 1.10 p.14. Supports: Urban/rural subscription-density observations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Multiple subscriptions can make the ratio exceed 100.

**Inference limit:** Not unique internet users, physical population coverage, connection quality or literacy.

## DIG-K12

ASER’s rural enrolled-child comparison reports a smartphone at home for 36.5% in 2018 and 67.6% in 2021; the 2018 comparison excludes households without mobile phones to match telephone-survey coverage more closely.

**Cases:** DIG-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S07: Annual Status of Education Report (Rural) 2021](https://img.asercentre.org/docs/aser2021forweb.pdf), Sample Design pp.15–16; Tables 8–11 pp.28–29 and footnotes. Supports: Levels and sample restriction.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Changed reachability and nonresponse can remain even after excluding no-phone households from 2018.

**Inference limit:** Not all Indian children or personally owned phones; not a causal effect of school closures.

## DIG-K13

Among ASER 2021 enrolled children with a smartphone at home, 26.1% had no access to it for studies and 27.0% had access at all times.

**Cases:** DIG-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S07: Annual Status of Education Report (Rural) 2021](https://img.asercentre.org/docs/aser2021forweb.pdf), Table 10 p.28; Table 12 p.31. Supports: Conditional access shares.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** A phone’s presence could still help some tasks or times; lack of study access does not uniquely identify why access was constrained.

**Inference limit:** Household device availability and child control are distinct outcomes; no measured learning effect.

## DIG-K14

M-PESA’s early Kenyan configuration combined SMS-capable phones with a carrier network, registered users, cash/e-money agents and bank-held funds; smartphone internet was not a prerequisite for this implementation.

**Cases:** DIG-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [DIG-S08: Mobile Money: The Economics of M-PESA (NBER Working Paper 16721)](https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w16721/w16721.pdf), Section II, PDFpp.6–11. Supports: Operational architecture, identification, agent liquidity and bank links.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** SMS instruction and registered balance -> transfer between accounts -> local agent cash redemption -> usable remittance.
- **strongest rival:** A handset-only account of adoption omits cash conversion and institutional acceptance.
- **discriminating observation:** If phones alone sufficed for cash redemption, withdrawals would not depend on agent cash or network functioning.
- **evidence sought found:** DIG-S08 describes cash/e-float operations and Tables 14a–14b identify agent cash and network problems in actual withdrawal delays.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Supports operational complement requirements; does not determine their separate shares in aggregate diffusion.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some services could operate through banks or physical transfers; agents were not the only conceivable transfer architecture.

**Inference limit:** Bounded to early M-PESA transfer/cash-conversion configuration, not all modern mobile-money products.

## DIG-K15

In Jack and Suri’s 2008/2009 survey rounds, 20% and 16% of user households reported withdrawal delays; agent cash shortages and network outages were among reported causes.

**Cases:** DIG-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [DIG-S08: Mobile Money: The Economics of M-PESA (NBER Working Paper 16721)](https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w16721/w16721.pdf), Tables 14a–14b, printed pp.25–26. Supports: Reported encounter rates and categories of delay.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Agent cash shortage or network outage -> inability to redeem immediately -> withdrawal delay.
- **strongest rival:** Personal documentation or deleted messages also generate delays; general dislike of technology does not predict the reported operational cause categories.
- **discriminating observation:** Cause-coded delays should include liquidity/network problems rather than only user preferences.
- **evidence sought found:** DIG-S08 Table 14a reports both liquidity and network causes, with other cause categories retained.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Self-attribution and nonconstant user composition limit quantitative causal apportionment.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Most reported delays resolved within a day; expanding use and changing user composition complicate comparisons.

**Inference limit:** Not transaction-level failure probabilities; delays are retrospective reports.

## DIG-K16

Suri and Jack estimated about 194, 000 Kenyan households escaped extreme poverty through increased mobile-money access, using 2008–2010 agent expansion to explain 2014 outcomes; this is conditional quasi-experimental estimation, not a direct beneficiary count.

**Cases:** DIG-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [DIG-S09: The long-run poverty and gender impacts of mobile money](https://www.findevgateway.org/sites/default/files/publications/files/new_jack_and_suri_paper_1.pdf), pp.1288–1292; equations 1–2; Table 1; attrition discussion. Supports: Design, authors’ national extrapolation and limits.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Early agent access growth -> easier financial transactions/saving/risk sharing -> occupational/consumption changes by 2014 -> lower estimated poverty.
- **strongest rival:** Agents may enter places with unobserved growth prospects; those prospects could independently improve outcomes.
- **discriminating observation:** Pre-rollout consumption/risk sharing and characteristics should not predict later access growth; estimates should survive plausible attrition selection.
- **evidence sought found:** Read DIG-S09 main-text description of pretests, location fixed effects and attrition bounds, plus Table 1 outcomes; supplementary tables and raw replication not acquired.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Reported falsification tests reduce some concerns but cannot eliminate unobserved trends or validate national extrapolation; data lineage overlaps DIG-S08.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Early agent placement could correlate with unobserved opportunity; 35% non-Nairobi attrition and later exclusion of Nairobi limit inference. Authors report no definitive evidence for all channels.

**Inference limit:** Supplementary tests were described in main paper but not separately inspected or replicated; later 2010–2014 rollout was intentionally not used as exogenous.

## DIG-K17

The 2024 FinAccess report gives current mobile-money use of 82.3% among Kenyan adults 18+, across providers, and identifies lack of a phone or identity documentation among non-use barriers.

**Cases:** DIG-C05. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S10: 2024 FinAccess Household Survey Main Report](https://www.centralbank.go.ke/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/2024-FINACCESS-HOUSEHOLD-SURVEY-MAIN-REPORT.pdf), Methodologypp.3–5; Figure 2.3p.12; usagep.26. Supports: Analysis age, current-use level and barrier narrative.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some adults remain outside the system; bank services and mobile money can coexist.

**Inference limit:** Not M-PESA market share, registered-account counts or a fixed-window active-use measure.

## DIG-K18

Ofcom’s 2023 Core Survey of UK adults 16+ reports gaming on VR devices 4%, phones 34%, consoles 22%, tablets 14%, laptops/netbooks 10%, desktops 8%; responses are non-exclusive and have no weekly/monthly threshold.

**Cases:** DIG-C06. **Evidence:** official-statistics. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S15: Adults’ Media Literacy Core Survey 2023 Data Tables](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/data/statistics/2024/adults-media-literacy-tracker/adults-media-literacy-core-survey-2023-data-tables?v=330749), Table 74 G 1 pp.307–308; fieldwork header. Supports: Matched device question, levels and 3, 643 base.
- [DIG-S16: Adults’ Media Literacy Research 2023 Technical Report](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/data/statistics/2024/adults-media-literacy-tracker/adults-media-literacy-tracker-2023-technical-report?v=330751), pp.7–8. Supports: 2, 121 face-to-face plus 1, 522 online quota interviews; adult weighting.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** VR use was 9% among 16–24-year-olds; non-gaming and non-UK use are outside this measure.

**Inference limit:** Rounded survey estimates, not headset ownership/sales, monthly-active users or retention. Fieldwork 27 Sep–12 Nov 2023.

## DIG-K19

A 2022 Quest 2 study found worse subjective ratings for prolonged VR desktop work than a matched physical setup among 16 university-worker completers; two additional participants withdrew on the first VR day.

**Cases:** DIG-C06. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [DIG-S12: Quantifying the Effects of Working in VR for One Week](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2206.03189), Sections 3.1–3.4, 3.6–3.7 and 4. Supports: Counterbalanced study, apparatus, sample, subjective results and withdrawals.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Prolonged use of tested headset/interface -> discomfort and interaction burden -> worse reported usability and productivity.
- **strongest rival:** Novelty, the chosen display/input setup and differing actual work across weeks could drive the same ratings.
- **discriminating observation:** Within-person, counterbalanced physical comparison with matched inputs should reveal a condition effect; persistence across optimised systems would distinguish general wearing costs.
- **evidence sought found:** DIG-S12 provided a counterbalanced matched-environment study and reported adverse ratings plus withdrawals; no matched alternative-headset experiment located within this source set.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Experiment identifies this treatment bundle more credibly than any individual ergonomic mechanism; cannot extrapolate to all VR.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some discomfort improved with time; the experiment did not optimise the spatial advantages of VR or test other tasks/hardware.

**Inference limit:** Small occupational sample; subjective productivity is not objective output; treatment includes headset/interface/remote-work bundle.

## DIG-K20

Meta reported FY 2023 Reality Labs revenue $1.896 billion and operating loss $16.120 billion for a segment including AR, mixed reality and VR hardware/software/content.

**Cases:** DIG-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S13: Meta Platforms, Inc. Form 10-K for year ended December 31, 2023](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1326801/000132680124000012/meta-20231231.htm), Note 16, pp.124–125. Supports: Segment definition, allocation description and accounting table.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Large investment can finance development of future products as well as current consumption.

**Inference limit:** Not number of VR users, per-device loss or retention; company has commercial interests.

## DIG-K21

The inspected Ofcom statistics establish minority UK VR gaming participation in 2023 relative to matched devices; they do not establish a global installed base, fixed-window use or retention rate.

Supports: Summary points to 2023 tracker but contains inconsistent comparison-year parenthesis.

Supports: Underlying question/denominator resolves level but has no fixed recency/frequency threshold.

Supports: Population and method scope.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

## DIG-K22

Microsoft reported 36.8 million Lumia and 126.8 million other non-Lumia phone sales in FY 2015 alongside $7.5 billion phone-business impairments and strategic retrenchment; FY 2014 sales covered only the post-acquisition period.

**Cases:** DIG-C07. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [DIG-S14: Microsoft 2015 Annual Report](https://www.microsoft.com/investor/reports/ar15/index.html), Shareholder letter; MD&A Phone Hardware; impairment discussion. Supports: Sales, charges, strategic scope and incomplete 2014 comparison.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded statistic or documentary statement is reported without assigning the cause of diffusion.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Substantial sales show deployment and users, so this is not a zero-adoption case.

**Inference limit:** Impairments reassess asset values; they do not identify current active users or the unique cause of strategic failure.

## DIG-K23

Software/content coordination is a plausible contributor to the Microsoft phone episode, but its 2015 stated unified-application strategy does not establish that app shortages caused the retrenchment.

**Cases:** DIG-C07. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** low. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [DIG-S14: Microsoft 2015 Annual Report](https://www.microsoft.com/investor/reports/ar15/index.html), Create more personal computing; MD&A Phone Hardware; impairment discussion. Supports: Company’s intended ecosystem strategy and business outcome, not causal proof.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Larger compatible app/user network -> developer/distributor participation -> buyer service availability -> sustainable phone demand.
- **strongest rival:** Execution/valuation/distribution problems could cause the impairment even with adequate apps.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare local uptake and retention when app availability changes but price, hardware and distribution are held similar.
- **evidence sought found:** Read DIG-S14 strategy and financial result; found management emphasis on common apps but no discriminating consumer/developer panel.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Outcome and management explanation cannot isolate network effects or switching costs.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Distribution, timing, acquisition valuation, margins and execution also fit the outcome.

**Inference limit:** No independent app availability, carrier incentives or buyer switching data inspected.

## DIG-K24

Pocket internet, performance, ergonomics and cost cannot be causally ranked from these observations; the claim that most laptops cost less than most smartphones is unestablished by this source set.

**Cases:** DIG-C03; DIG-C04; DIG-C06. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [DIG-S04: Communications Market Report 2015: Internet and online content](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/cmr/cmr15/uk_5.pdf?v=322209), Figure 5.20. Supports: Reported importance, without component intervention or price distributions.
- [DIG-S05: The Smartphone Difference / U.S. Smartphone Use in 2015](https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/wp-content/uploads/sites/9/2015/03/PI_Smartphones_0401151.pdf), Smartphone dependence and financial constraints. Supports: Constrained access is a rival to pure preference.
- [DIG-S06: Telecom Statistics India-2024](https://www.dot.gov.in/static/uploads/2025/07/f44e3a674c85aa98cd6fa881e4462b57.pdf), Table 6.2. Supports: Service-unit revenue is distinct from device purchase price.
- [DIG-S15: Adults’ Media Literacy Core Survey 2023 Data Tables](https://www.ofcom.org.uk/siteassets/resources/documents/research-and-data/data/statistics/2024/adults-media-literacy-tracker/adults-media-literacy-core-survey-2023-data-tables?v=330749), M 6A; G 1. Supports: Task perceptions and matched participation outcomes, not determinant coefficients.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Portable connected device -> more moments of available service -> higher reported importance for certain tasks.
- **strongest rival:** App/social coordination, constrained alternatives, status, habit and pricing can produce the same preference pattern.
- **discriminating observation:** Hold task, service availability, total cost and experience similar while varying portability/performance; independently measure prices and actual choices.
- **evidence sought found:** Sought matched official device-use measures: DIG-S04 and DIG-S15 found task-specific preferences/difficulty; DIG-S05 found financial dependence; no component-separating design or price-distribution dataset found in selected sources.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The findings fit multiple accounts; lack of price data establishes no ordering in either direction.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Phones can be useful despite document difficulty; low data unit cost can coexist with higher spending; VR participation differs by task.

**Inference limit:** No dated sales-weighted laptop/smartphone price distributions or matched causal choice experiment inspected.

## DIG-K25

The 2015 US household coexistence of computers and handhelds is consistent with task complementarity, but the inspected access data cannot distinguish it from wealth, different household members or unused equipment.

**Cases:** DIG-C02. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [DIG-S03: Computer and Internet Use in the United States: 2015 (ACS-37)](https://www.census.gov/content/dam/Census/library/publications/2017/acs/acs-37.pdf), p.2; Table 2 high-connectivity indicator. Supports: Co-access across device/subscription categories, without task logs.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Different device affordances -> households retain multiple devices for different tasks -> co-access.
- **strongest rival:** Wealth and household composition create multiple devices even without active task complementarity.
- **discriminating observation:** Within-person task logs and retained-device use, controlling resources, should show active complementary tasks.
- **evidence sought found:** Read DIG-S03 joint access and demographic tables; these establish coexistence but no task allocation.
- **remaining ambiguity:** All rival accounts can produce the observed 62% joint access.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Co-ownership need not imply one person actively divides tasks across devices.

**Inference limit:** No longitudinal task-use or equipment-disposal data inspected.

## ENE-K01

Nemet’s worldwide PV price reconstruction, interpreted alongside US market examples, documents a 1970s shift from space to terrestrial applications with changing quality requirements, standardization and market structure; falling module prices alone do not measure factory learning.

**Cases:** ENE-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S01: Beyond the learning curve: factors influencing cost reductions in photovoltaics](https://wikis.mit.edu/confluence/download/attachments/21139745/Nemet_BeyondtheLearningCurve_EP_06.pdf?api=v2), §§1.2–2.1 and 3.1.1–3.1.4, pp. 3221–3222 and 3224–3225. Supports: Worldwide price series from two world surveys, used as a cost proxy; partly US market evidence about quality, customers and standardization; no causal allocation of the early residual.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** New terrestrial tasks and customers -> less customized products and altered markups -> observed prices change with production practice.
- **strongest rival:** Repeated output alone lowers an unchanged product’s factory cost.
- **discriminating observation:** Do prices fall faster than the engineering model explains while customers/product requirements change?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S01 §§3.1–3.2: early residual and market changes found; later period better explained.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Quality, competition and manufacturing changed together; no matched unchanged-product counterfactual.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Manufacturing improvements also occurred; terrestrial government purchases remained substantial.

**Inference limit:** No causal allocation of the 1975–1979 residual to quality, markups or standardization; niche procurement is not evidence of grid competitiveness.

## ENE-K02

PV cost decomposition is model-sensitive: for 1980–2001 Nemet assigns 43% of decline to plant size, whereas Kavlak et al. assign 8%; the latter assigns 36% for 2001–2012 and separates efficiency, material use, prices and yield. Their reconstructions also identify early silicon supply/process benefits inherited from the semiconductor industry rather than generated entirely by PV output.

**Cases:** ENE-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ENE-S01: Beyond the learning curve: factors influencing cost reductions in photovoltaics](https://wikis.mit.edu/confluence/download/attachments/21139745/Nemet_BeyondtheLearningCurve_EP_06.pdf?api=v2), Table 3 and §4.3, p. 3227. Supports: 43% plant-size allocation and semiconductor silicon-supply spillover.
- [ENE-S02: Evaluating the causes of cost reduction in photovoltaic modules](https://static1.squarespace.com/static/56afb881e321406be6160228/t/5bff10822b6a2884d7267f17/1543442566890/Evaluating%2Bthe%2Bcauses%2Bof%2Bcost%2Breduction%2Bin%2Bphotovoltaic%2Bmodules.pdf), §§4.1–4.2, Table 3, pp. 705–707. Supports: 8% and 36% plant-size allocations; revised cost equation; semiconductor supply/know-how before the mid-2000s transition.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports and compares published model allocations, without treating them as identified causal effects.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Both studies support several improvement channels, but their shared historical evidence is not two independent estimates of the same causal policy effect.

**Inference limit:** These are allocated contributions under engineering models; neither percentage is a universal learning coefficient, electricity-price effect or welfare estimate. The comparison changes reconstructed inputs as well as model definitions (including the 1980 plant-size input); it does not isolate the cost equation as the cause of different shares.

## ENE-K03

China’s emergence as a PV producer in the late 2000s did not imply similarly large domestic deployment: the inspected 2010 study reports over one-third of world production in 2008 with over 95% exported, and about 2.2% of global demand in 2009.

**Cases:** ENE-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ENE-S03: Innovation and international technology transfer: The case of the Chinese photovoltaic industry (CERNA Working Paper 2010-12; HAL version 1)](https://minesparis-psl.hal.science/hal-00498578v1/document), Abstract and §1.1, working-paper pp. 1, 4–5. Supports: Production/export estimates and separate 2009 domestic-demand share.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Dated production/export and demand observations establish a stage/geographic mismatch, not its causal magnitude.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The production and demand numbers concern different years and inherited industry estimates; domestic installations were not zero.

**Inference limit:** No current market claim and no subtraction of nonmatching denominators; production capacity, annual output and deployment must remain distinct.

## ENE-K04

Interviews and supply-chain evidence from early Chinese PV entry support imported production equipment plus skilled personnel as important channels; upstream process secrecy and sparse equipment markets constrained entry despite downstream success.

**Cases:** ENE-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S03: Innovation and international technology transfer: The case of the Chinese photovoltaic industry (CERNA Working Paper 2010-12; HAL version 1)](https://minesparis-psl.hal.science/hal-00498578v1/document), §§1.2, 2.1–2.3, working-paper pp. 7–11; conclusion pp. 17–18. Supports: Turnkey lines, vendor training, diaspora recruitment and segment-specific entry barriers.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Equipment purchases plus recruited expertise -> process operation/adaptation -> cell/module entry; upstream tacit processes remain harder to acquire.
- **strongest rival:** Cheap factors alone explain entry uniformly across supply-chain stages.
- **discriminating observation:** Downstream/upstream entry should differ with accessible equipment and tacit knowledge even within the same country.
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S03 contrasts stages, documents vendor training and returning executives, and reports later FDI following pioneer firms.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Stages also differ in capital intensity, energy use and market concentration; no controlled isolation.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Low wages, energy prices, finance and foreign deployment incentives also mattered; firms’ retrospective accounts may exaggerate chosen channels.

**Inference limit:** Small producer-centered sample, rough equipment competition measure and imperfect patent proxies; no estimate of independent policy or labour contribution.

## ENE-K05

The 2016 seven-country reactor-cost compilation records differing within-country overnight-cost trajectories, including declines across Korean construction cohorts and escalation in later US/French cohorts; it does not identify a universal sign of nuclear learning.

**Cases:** ENE-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ENE-S04: Historical construction costs of global nuclear power reactors](https://core.ac.uk/download/pdf/82188133.pdf), §§3.1–3.2, §4.3, Table 1, pp. 373–379. Supports: Country/cohort construction-start comparisons and local-currency cost normalization.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes variation in the published construction-cost sample, without attributing it solely to standardization or state structure.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Countries adopted different designs in different eras; data quality and coverage vary, and some prototype routes are excluded.

**Inference limit:** The 349-reactor sample is not all proposed projects. Korean cohort-count inconsistency is not resolved here; no precise Korean cohort total is asserted. No international cost ranking is inferred.

## ENE-K06

France’s coordinated PWR programme combined bulk ordering, licensed designs, regulatory continuity and EDF engineering capacity with rapid deployment, yet its later construction cohorts still experienced real cost escalation.

**Cases:** ENE-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S05: The French Pressurized Water Reactor Program](https://pure.iiasa.ac.at/id/eprint/11077/1/The%20French%20Pressurised%20Water%20Reactor%20Programme.pdf), §2, Table 1 and §§3–4, pp. 2–8. Supports: Institutional organization, deployment timing, design changes and estimated escalation.
- [ENE-S04: Historical construction costs of global nuclear power reactors](https://core.ac.uk/download/pdf/82188133.pdf), §3.1 and French results, pp. 374–376. Supports: Later reactor-level series also shows escalation, with different magnitudes/coverage.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Concentrated ordering and engineering authority -> repeatable build programme -> rapid capacity deployment.
- **strongest rival:** Falling unit costs are the necessary cause of rapid deployment.
- **discriminating observation:** Can deployment expand under coordinated procurement while real construction costs rise?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S05 documents both rapid programme expansion and escalation; ENE-S04 supplies later, differently constructed French cost series.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Oil security, political priorities, financing and grid demand coincide; comparison does not identify each institutional contribution.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** French cost magnitudes differ by reconstruction and cohort; national coordination did not mean unchanged designs or citizen consent.

**Inference limit:** The coordinated setting supports an enabling account, not proof of necessity or sufficiency. Grubler’s universal negative-learning generalization is rejected here.

## ENE-K07

In Eash-Gates et al.’s US four-loop-plant reconstruction for 1976–1987, indirect accounts contribute 72% of the modeled cost increase, placing engineering, supervision and construction organization alongside hardware and regulatory requirements.

**Cases:** ENE-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S06: Sources of Cost Overrun in Nuclear Power Plant Construction Call for a New Approach to Engineering Design](https://dspace.mit.edu/bitstream/handle/1721.1/133049/Jacopo%27s%20and%20Jessika%27s%20paper%20on%20nuclear%20cost%20Sep%202020.pdf), §2.2, manuscript pp. 3–5; §4.4, pp. 10–11. Supports: 61 EEDB accounts; 72% indirect contribution; model/data limits.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Complex site activities and changing execution conditions -> more engineering/support and installation burden -> higher constructed cost.
- **strongest rival:** Cost escalation is entirely additional reactor hardware demanded by safety rules.
- **discriminating observation:** Do indirect service accounts account for large changes beyond direct hardware expenditure?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S06 EEDB decomposition found the majority in indirect accounts and acknowledges proportional-allocation assumptions.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Regulation, redesign and site interference can generate indirect costs jointly; ultimate causal shares remain underdetermined.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Indirect accounts can themselves include responses to regulation, changed scope or schedules; the allocation does not establish a separate regulation-free cause.

**Inference limit:** Account attribution is not a causal estimate that management alone explains 72%; it covers one modeled configuration and interval, not every reactor or worker productivity level.

## ENE-K08

Overnight reactor construction cost excludes interest during construction, and completed-project cost trajectories omit the outcome of cancellation; they cannot by themselves establish investor affordability or system welfare.

**Cases:** ENE-C02. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ENE-S04: Historical construction costs of global nuclear power reactors](https://core.ac.uk/download/pdf/82188133.pdf), §3.2, pp. 374–375 and §5.2, p. 381. Supports: Explicit exclusion of IDC; distinction from electricity cost and coverage frame.
- [ENE-S06: Sources of Cost Overrun in Nuclear Power Plant Construction Call for a New Approach to Engineering Design](https://dspace.mit.edu/bitstream/handle/1721.1/133049/Jacopo%27s%20and%20Jessika%27s%20paper%20on%20nuclear%20cost%20Sep%202020.pdf), Introduction, manuscript p. 1. Supports: Reports 2017 abandonment of VC Summer as a separate project outcome.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** States accounting and sampling boundaries, including an observed cancellation, without estimating their aggregate causal effect.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Overnight costs remain useful for an explicitly restricted question about construction resource cost.

**Inference limit:** No claim that every omitted cost reverses any national ranking; social damages and avoided pollution are not estimated.

## ENE-K09

Hoerni’s 1959 patent application specifies retaining a nonconducting surface coating over exposed junction edges; the museum history dates a working planar transistor to March 1959 and commercial introduction to April 1960.

**Cases:** ENE-C03. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S07: US3025589A: Method of manufacturing semiconductor devices](https://patents.google.com/patent/US3025589A/en), Specification and claim 1. Supports: Protected-junction process architecture.
- [ENE-S14: 1959: Invention of the “Planar” Manufacturing Process](https://www.computerhistory.org/siliconengine/invention-of-the-planar-manufacturing-process/), Main account, paragraphs 1–4. Supports: Demonstration and commercialization chronology; subsequent reliability problems.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Surface coating retained during fabrication -> junction protection -> reproducible planar-device architecture.
- **strongest rival:** A patent idea alone demonstrates commercially satisfactory production.
- **discriminating observation:** Is there separate evidence of functioning hardware and a later marketed device, as well as unresolved failure modes?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S07 describes process; ENE-S14 records demonstration, commercial device and subsequent reliability issues.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Factory qualification and independent yield series were not inspected; museum history inherits the patent record.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The process still needed complementary masking, diffusion, contacts and packaging; later bonding/interconnect failures continued.

**Inference limit:** Patent and retrospective chronology do not measure yield or prove that planar technology alone caused the semiconductor industry’s expansion.

## ENE-K10

The Apollo computer programme supported an early IC production niche through a large, standardized NOR-gate purchase, while its engineers valued size, power, timing and reliability rather than the lowest initial component price.

**Cases:** ENE-C03. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S08: Computers in Spaceflight: The NASA Experience — Chapter 2, Evolution of the hardware](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/static/history/computers/Ch2-4.html), Printed pp. 33–34; notes 30–35. Supports: Single-gate choice, initially higher cost, large 1963 production share and participant disagreement over perceived risk.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Mission constraints plus financed programme -> standardized purchase and qualification effort -> early IC production at volume.
- **strongest rival:** Unsubsidized component cheapness already made the choice automatic.
- **discriminating observation:** Did designers choose higher-priced components for nonprice capabilities and create a large early order?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S08 records higher initial IC cost, single NOR type and intensive 1963 purchasing.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Reliability choices and procurement size are jointly determined; no no-Apollo industry counterfactual.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Other military and commercial customers existed; NASA history alone cannot establish its share of total industry cost decline.

**Inference limit:** Procurement volume supports demand creation in a specific niche, not a claim that NASA invented ICs or alone financed semiconductor diffusion; historical 60% estimate is not extrapolated.

## ENE-K11

Irwin and Klenow’s 1974–1992 merchant-DRAM model estimates stronger own-firm than external learning contributions and finds no systematic faster learning rate for Japanese firms; it does not support a simple national-learning-superiority explanation.

**Cases:** ENE-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S09: Learning-by-Doing Spillovers in the Semiconductor Industry](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/experience-curve/1994-irwin.pdf), §IV, pp. 1210–1221, Tables 2–6. Supports: 32-firm shipment/price dataset; own/external and Japanese-rate tests; ambiguous Japanese within-country spillover difference.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Repeated firm production and some external knowledge -> lower inferred marginal cost -> product-generation price/output patterns.
- **strongest rival:** Japanese firms inherently learn faster, or domestic-only spillovers dominate.
- **discriminating observation:** Allow country-specific learning slopes and separate same-country from cross-country cumulative production.
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S09 estimates these alternatives, reporting similar country slopes and own-firm contribution about three times external contribution in baseline.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Unobserved investment, R&D and selection can covary with shipments; some spillover parameters are imprecise.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Tests of stronger within-Japan spillovers are inconclusive; international cooperation, investment and equipment differences may matter through other channels.

**Inference limit:** Costs and yields are not observed directly; structural competition assumptions and sample selection qualify the interpretation. Results concern merchant DRAM, not all chips or all eras.

## ENE-K12

The same DRAM study finds economically important spillovers between adjacent product generations in only two of seven tested transitions, so experience in one generation cannot be treated as a guaranteed permanent entry barrier.

**Cases:** ENE-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S09: Learning-by-Doing Spillovers in the Semiconductor Industry](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/experience-curve/1994-irwin.pdf), §IV.D and conclusion, pp. 1223–1224, Table 7. Supports: Weak cross-generation effects and entry/exit examples.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Design-generation change -> partial obsolescence of specific production knowledge -> limited transferable learning advantage.
- **strongest rival:** An early lead guarantees cumulatively increasing advantage across every generation.
- **discriminating observation:** Does previous-generation production strongly predict subsequent-generation cost, and do entrants appear despite little prior production?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S09 Table 7 identifies only two economically meaningful transitions and discusses contemporaneous entry.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Model imposes a particular knowledge-depreciation structure; entry is selected and requires other assets.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some spillovers are statistically detectable but economically tiny; capital, design skills, equipment and customer relationships can still deter entry.

**Inference limit:** Weak estimated spillovers are not evidence that experience or incumbency has no value; the underlying proprietary data were not re-estimated.

## ENE-K13

Yoshino’s account separates a rechargeable laboratory configuration from saleable batteries: polyacetylene’s heat/volume limitations motivated a carbon anode, and comparative impact testing supported continued development before Japanese commercialization in 1991.

**Cases:** ENE-C04. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S10: Brief History and Future of the Lithium-Ion Battery — Nobel Lecture](https://www.nobelprize.org/uploads/2019/10/yoshino-lecture.pdf), §§1.1, 1.4–1.5, pp. 207–217. Supports: PA limitations, carbon/lithiated-cathode combination, 1985 patent, impact comparison and 1991 commercialization.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Portable-device demand and metallic-lithium/PA limitations -> paired material changes and safety tests -> candidate suitable for commercialization.
- **strongest rival:** Electrochemical charge/discharge demonstration alone suffices for market entry.
- **discriminating observation:** Are working configurations rejected on heat, size or abuse performance, followed by separate commercialization steps?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S10 gives those rejected configurations and contrast tests, followed by commercialization date.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Narrative selection and retrospective credit cannot establish independent necessity of every contribution.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** This is a retrospective inventor narrative, not the whole industry’s development record or statistical safety certification.

**Inference limit:** A passed impact test does not establish general safety; commercial process yields, warranties, financing terms and all intervening qualification work remain unknown.

## ENE-K14

For representative high-energy-density 18650 cells, Ziegler et al. compare 1995–2000 with 2010–2015 and allocate 38% of modeled cost decline to charge-density improvements; reduced material prices and nonmaterial costs also contribute.

**Cases:** ENE-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S11: Determinants of lithium-ion battery technology cost decline](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlepdf/2021/ee/d1ee01313k), pp. 6076–6078, 6085–6087; Figures 5–6 and Table 4. Supports: Fixed-size cell definition, reconstructed costs and density/material decomposition.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** More active capacity in a fixed cell volume plus lower input cost -> overhead and components spread over more stored energy -> lower modeled USD/Wh.
- **strongest rival:** Raw-material cheapness or factory replication alone explains the decline.
- **discriminating observation:** Does a fixed-configuration component model require large energy-density changes as well as input-price changes?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S11 separates those variables and finds a substantial charge-density contribution.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Component interactions and representative-cell selection mean decomposition is not intervention identification.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Historical data are sparse and unequal across variables; some parameter values represent selected high-energy cells or prototypes, not a census.
