**Inference limit:** USD per energy capacity differs from price per cell, pack cost, cycle life and power capability. The result does not cover LFP vehicle packs or all battery uses.

## ENE-K15

The battery model’s baseline assigns about 54% of cost decline to broadly defined R&D, 30% to scale and 2% to learning-by-doing; these shares depend on classifications that include pilot-line and process work within R&D.

**Cases:** ENE-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [ENE-S11: Determinants of lithium-ion battery technology cost decline](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlepdf/2021/ee/d1ee01313k), pp. 6088–6091, Figures 7–9. Supports: Definitions, baseline shares, alternative-assignment sensitivity and proprietary information limits.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Recorded component/process changes -> literature-based assignment to research, routine learning or scale -> estimated mechanism shares.
- **strongest rival:** A different classification of intertwined process work changes apparent research versus learning contributions.
- **discriminating observation:** Does the ranking survive plausible reassignments, and are records available to separate overlapping activities?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S11 tests several extreme assignments and reports robust ranking but sparse/proprietary process evidence.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The method cannot identify a counterfactual public/private funding effect or labour’s social contribution.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The authors’ alternative assignments retain the broad ranking, but routine and nonroutine improvements can be complementary rather than mutually separable activities.

**Inference limit:** No inference that factory workers contributed only 2%, or that a public R&D programme caused 54%. Public/private R&D and endogenous demand are not independently identified.

## ENE-K16

In China’s mid-2000s electric-two-wheeler market, lead-acid remained the dominant battery despite commercially available lithium-ion alternatives; a contemporary comparison estimated roughly fourfold upfront Li-ion battery cost with a longer assumed life.

**Cases:** ENE-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [ENE-S12: The future of electric two-wheelers and electric vehicles in China](https://escholarship.org/content/qt0d05f8v9/qt0d05f8v9.pdf), §2.1.1, pp. 2547–2548. Supports: VRLA dominance and limited advanced-battery availability; export mix uncertainty.
- [ENE-S13: Comparative environmental impacts of electric bikes in China](https://itspubs.ucdavis.edu/download_pdf.php?id=1289), Conclusion, p. 289. Supports: Approximate power-equivalent fourfold purchase price and two-to-threefold lifespan comparison.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Establishes coexistence/continued older practice and reported price trade-off, leaving buyer-level causal attribution open.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Advanced-battery sales were increasing; estimated production shares include exports and are not domestic-use shares.

**Inference limit:** Does not prove low income or credit constraints caused individual choices. Historical price comparison is not a current specification, a universal lifetime ratio or a fully normalized lifecycle estimate.

## ENE-K17

E-bike adoption in the surveyed Chinese cities combined component-based assembly with a policy-shaped choice set: motorcycle restrictions favored some electric use, while other cities restricted e-bikes; available surveys mainly observed substitution from bicycles and public transport.

**Cases:** ENE-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S12: The future of electric two-wheelers and electric vehicles in China](https://escholarship.org/content/qt0d05f8v9/qt0d05f8v9.pdf), §1.2, pp. 2545–2546; §§2.1.2–2.4 and §3, pp. 2547–2551. Supports: Field methods, standardized components, six factory visits, motorcycle/e-bike bans, previous-mode observations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Standard components and permissive local classification amid motorcycle constraints -> accessible vehicle supply and usable road space -> adoption by some urban travellers.
- **strongest rival:** Uniform technical superiority or a uniform national mandate explains uptake.
- **discriminating observation:** Are rival modes and local restrictions heterogeneous, and are assemblers observed using standardized prefabricated parts?
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S12 documents opposing city rules, survey choice sets and six assembly-plant observations.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Policy adoption is endogenous to congestion and demand; force-field rankings do not identify effects.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Urban distance, incomes, transit quality and enforcement differed with policy; modular architecture did not eliminate entry/quality barriers.

**Inference limit:** Ten-city observations and three-city prior-mode surveys are not a national causal estimate. No inference that all Chinese adoption was voluntary or that bans alone explain it.

## ENE-K18

The 2009 partial lifecycle analysis finds that e-bike comparisons depend on regional electricity and the displaced mode; lead emissions arise upstream in battery production/recycling, so zero tailpipe emissions do not demonstrate zero pollution or a quantified health benefit.

**Cases:** ENE-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [ENE-S13: Comparative environmental impacts of electric bikes in China](https://itspubs.ucdavis.edu/download_pdf.php?id=1289), §§2.1–2.3, §§3.1–4, Tables 2 and 6, pp. 282–289. Supports: Production/grid inventories, lead pathways, excluded lifecycle processes, absence of direct population health measures.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Battery manufacture/recycling and electricity use -> geographically redistributed pollution -> different lifecycle comparisons than tailpipe accounting.
- **strongest rival:** A vehicle without exhaust has negligible pollution regardless of energy/material supply chain or displaced travel.
- **discriminating observation:** Include lead flows and region-specific electricity; compare bicycle, bus, motorcycle and car rather than one favorable alternative.
- **evidence sought found:** ENE-S13 performs a partial inventory and explicitly states missing health and lifecycle evidence.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Magnitude and exposure depend on process controls, region, service life and counterfactual travel; some inputs predate rapid e-bike expansion.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** E-bikes compare favorably with some motorized alternatives on several modeled emissions metrics; emissions relocation can reduce urban exposure while imposing burdens elsewhere.

**Inference limit:** Mass emissions are not absorbed dose, disease or money damages. Old lead-flow factors, borrowed vehicle factors, load/lifespan assumptions and omitted processes prevent a full welfare estimate.

## GHA-K01

African actors initiated or financed some southern roads as well as operating lorries: Heap records Kwahu contracting and the Begoro–Bosuso connection, while Austin identifies African demands, construction and ownership.

**Cases:** GHA-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [GHA-S01: Labour and land in Ghana, 1874–1939: a shifting ratio and an institutional revolution](https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/4392/1/Labour_and_land_in_Ghana_(LSERO_version).pdf), manuscript p. 7. Supports: African road initiative and motor-lorry ownership/operation.
- [GHA-S02: The Development of Motor Transport in the Gold Coast, 1900–39](https://www.academia.edu/80974990/The_Development_of_Motor_Transport_in_the_Gold_Coast_1900_39), pp. 29–31. Supports: Named projects, chiefs, local resources, contractors and district-commissioner participation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded report of inspected observations or evidence scope; no isolated causal effect is asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Colonial engineers, grants and contractors also contributed; official accounts celebrate cooperation.

**Inference limit:** Named initiative does not establish universal voluntariness, equal incidence of contributions, consent to land loss, or the proportion of roads independently initiated.

## GHA-K02

Southern lorries initially often fed railway long hauls and subsequently competed for cocoa freight; Austin reports rail export shares above 83% in 1922–23 and 58% in 1930–31, with head-loading commonly continuing from farms to local markets.

**Cases:** GHA-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S01: Labour and land in Ghana, 1874–1939: a shifting ratio and an institutional revolution](https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/4392/1/Labour_and_land_in_Ghana_(LSERO_version).pdf), manuscript pp. 5–7, especially pp. 6–7 and notes 28, 30, 35–36. Supports: Timing, freight relationships, quoted modal shares and continued first-stage head-loading.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Lorry access to feeder routes complements cheap rail long hauls; improved road service and rates permit some direct coastal haulage -> changing freight shares.
- **strongest rival:** Changing cocoa geography, road quality, vehicle productivity and fiscal treatment can jointly account for modal shares; a share decline need not mean absolute rail contraction.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched route/season traffic and complete delivered costs before and after road improvement, separating new production from diverted rail loads.
- **evidence sought found:** Read GHA-S01 pp. 5–7 for contemporary rates, feeder descriptions, export shares and retained farm-to-market head-loading; no matched route panel was available.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Aggregate temporal association does not isolate operating flexibility, physical improvements or institutional cost allocation.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Road and rail continued to coexist; changes in commodities, surfaces, vehicles, policy and route selection accompany modal changes.

**Inference limit:** No complete adoption denominator, independent reconstruction of Cardinall’s series, or road-versus-rail welfare comparison. Do not join these shares to MOB-S08’s broad pre-1970 summary without reconciliation.

## GHA-K03

African owner-driver entry used smaller enterprises and, in some accounts, hire-purchase; contemporary reports attributed competitive success to low overheads and long working days, but the independent contribution of that business organization is not identified.

**Cases:** GHA-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S01: Labour and land in Ghana, 1874–1939: a shifting ratio and an institutional revolution](https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/4392/1/Labour_and_land_in_Ghana_(LSERO_version).pdf), manuscript p. 7, notes 38–40. Supports: Chiefly fleets, smaller owners and 1927–28 administrative attribution of larger firms’ retreat.
- [GHA-S02: The Development of Motor Transport in the Gold Coast, 1900–39](https://www.academia.edu/80974990/The_Development_of_Motor_Transport_in_the_Gold_Coast_1900_39), pp. 34–35. Supports: Hire-purchase, owner-driver business, long workdays and contemporary profit descriptions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Smaller ownership/operation units and hire-purchase -> entry with different overheads and financing -> observed expansion of African owner-drivers.
- **strongest rival:** Technical changes, state-funded roads, crop demand or reporting bias could explain the same expansion and large-firm exit.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable operator accounts including vehicle, routes, debt, upkeep, hours, failed firms and family work.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S01 p. 7 and GHA-S02 pp. 34–35 document arrangements and contemporary attributions; they do not provide those matched accounts.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Low observed cash overhead could partly transfer costs into long or unpaid work and public road provision.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The same growth coincided with different vehicles, roads, public expenditure and demand; reported owner profitability omits failures and unpaid time.

**Inference limit:** No ownership census, financing-contract sample, repayment/default series, driver hourly income or causal decomposition. Chiefly fleets and small owner-drivers are distinct actors.

## GHA-K04

Entry into the southern cocoa economy was mediated by differing land institutions: group purchases with extended credit in Akyem Abuakwa coexisted with predominantly leased access in Ashanti, with political authority shaping charges and terms.

**Cases:** GHA-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S01: Labour and land in Ghana, 1874–1939: a shifting ratio and an institutional revolution](https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/4392/1/Labour_and_land_in_Ghana_(LSERO_version).pdf), manuscript pp. 15–17. Supports: Group finance and credit, sub-chief sales, Asante leases, stranger status and colonial intervention in rents.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cocoa demand raises value of access -> buyers organize finance while authorities set sale/lease rules -> unequal and regionally different routes into cultivation.
- **strongest rival:** Different land availability, locations and stages of settlement could account for contract forms alongside political authority.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable transactions with land quality, buyer status, dates, chief authority, prices and access refusals.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S01 pp. 15–17 supplies regional institutional contrasts, selected transactions and source limitations; no harmonized transaction dataset inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Historical political explanation is credible but not separated from regional economic differences.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Land suitability, scarcity, buyer migration, transaction size and timing also differ between the regions.

**Inference limit:** No matched causal estimate of political centralization; a farming-land credit arrangement is not evidence of a specific lorry-purchase loan.

## GHA-K05

Austin links cocoa-farm income to expansion of hired labour and farm pledging while retaining early coerced farm work, continued compulsory road work, different forest/savanna opportunities and unequal gains by gender; transport enterprise did not imply a uniformly free surrounding labour system.

**Cases:** GHA-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S01: Labour and land in Ghana, 1874–1939: a shifting ratio and an institutional revolution](https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/4392/1/Labour_and_land_in_Ghana_(LSERO_version).pdf), manuscript pp. 17–20, especially p. 18 n. 104 and p. 20 n. 115. Supports: Early bound labour in Ashanti cocoa planting, later hiring/pledging, road compulsion, migration and gender qualifications.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Mature cocoa farms generate purchasing power and collateral -> employers can hire and borrowers pledge farms -> some movement away from bound labour, uneven across people and tasks.
- **strongest rival:** Legal emancipation and enforcement could drive change independently, while coerced labour itself helped create the productive farms.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated individual contracts and exit choices across farm maturation, emancipation enforcement and wage alternatives, separated by sex/status.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S01 pp. 17–20 explicitly distinguishes ordinances, coerced planting, subsequent revenues, continuing road obligations and gender limits.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Reciprocal causation and heterogeneous enforcement prevent an isolated technological-emancipation explanation.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Legal abolition, colonial enforcement, crop revenues and workers’ alternatives changed together; the source describes gradual uneven transitions.

**Inference limit:** No claim that cocoa or lorries alone abolished slavery, that all later labour was free, or that wage employment gave every person better welfare. Much evidence inherits Austin’s book and earlier scholarship.

## GHA-K06

The 1925 Makongo wage/contract dispute and a June 1930 Savelugu instruction rejecting forced supply of PWD workers show local changes in paid recruitment before Britain’s 1931 convention ratification, without identifying the independent influence of international pressure or anticipatory policy change.

**Cases:** GHA-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S04: From Forced to Voluntary Labour in Rural Africa: The Transition to Paid Voluntary Labour on the Roads of the Northern Territories of the Gold Coast](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0020859022000657), “The Transition from Paid Forced Labour to Paid Voluntary Labour on PWD Roads”, notes 50–52. Supports: Protest, commissioners’ response and dated Savelugu diary on recruitment.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Chiefs contest terms and local officials change recruitment -> documented paid work offered without compulsory supply before formal ratification.
- **strongest rival:** Earlier international pressure or anticipated legal change influenced the same local officials; local adjustments need not have been independent of external debate.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated recruitment practice plus correspondence identifying officials’ prior information, pressure and reasons, comparing similarly exposed districts.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S04 cites 1925 complaints/responses and reproduces the June 1930 diary; these establish the reported sequence but do not isolate local from prior external influences.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Original diaries not independently reopened, and implementation breadth, anticipatory effects and residual tasks remain unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Earlier international pressure and policy debate remain possible influences; compulsory cleaning/weeding continued on some PWD roads.

**Inference limit:** Chronology does not quantify how many workers could refuse, date a universal end of compulsion, or show that law and international pressure were irrelevant.

## GHA-K07

Pont Cháfer connects changes in northern PWD recruitment to maintenance demands and workers’ alternatives; the post-1929 fall in southern wages could make northern offers attractive without a general improvement in workers’ outside options.

**Cases:** GHA-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S04: From Forced to Voluntary Labour in Rural Africa: The Transition to Paid Voluntary Labour on the Roads of the Northern Territories of the Gold Coast](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0020859022000657), “The Transition from Paid Forced Labour to Paid Voluntary Labour on PWD Roads”, notes 53–66. Supports: Motor traffic/maintenance pressures, regional wage comparisons, Depression timing, recruitment reports and residual obligatory cleaning.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Higher maintenance demands and resistance to compulsion -> local wage/recruitment changes; falling southern wages -> a stronger northern paid labour supply.
- **strongest rival:** Legal/political reform and new finance could explain recruitment changes even without relative-wage changes; source selection may overstate a smooth transition.
- **discriminating observation:** Recruitment, refusals, sanctions, wages including food, road conditions and budgets by site before/after policy and labour-market changes.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S04 provides dated disputes, wage evidence and recruitment reports; it also retains residual weeding obligations and PWD’s limited mileage.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The evidence supports several interacting contributions and no isolated causal share.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Legislation, local official convictions, budgets and technical organization also changed; wages and voluntary recruitment are separate variables.

**Inference limit:** No matched payroll/recruitment dataset or aggregate welfare estimate. Ending compulsory supply in one task does not establish that all work on the same road became voluntary.

## GHA-K08

Northern political-road labour changed unevenly: Kunkel identifies arrangements and mid-1930s budget assumptions preserving compulsion, while Pont Cháfer emphasizes earlier dissent, district payments and later grant-financed paid gangs; their evidence does not establish one uniform transition date or compulsory-labour rate.

**Cases:** GHA-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [GHA-S03: Forced Labour, Roads, and Chiefs: The Implementation of the ILO Forced Labour Convention in the Gold Coast](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0020859018000524), pp. 464–471, especially p. 469 n. 89 and pp. 470–471. Supports: Administrative powers, subsidy assumption, official dissent and local wage-payment initiatives.
- [GHA-S04: From Forced to Voluntary Labour in Rural Africa: The Transition to Paid Voluntary Labour on the Roads of the Northern Territories of the Gold Coast](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0020859022000657), “Indirect Rule, Taxation, and Unpaid Forced Labour”, notes 69–95. Supports: Explicit engagement with Kunkel, divergent district practices, budgets, grants and later legislation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Legal classifications and administrative powers can preserve coerced maintenance, while local opposition and finance can shift particular roads toward paid recruitment.
- **strongest rival:** A uniform strategy of preserving compulsion versus an earlier, locally differentiated transition; both can coexist on different roads or tasks.
- **discriminating observation:** Link the same road’s planned budget to actual payroll, recruitment order, complaints and sanctions across years; compare maintained and abandoned roads.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S03’s 1935 subsidy calculation and GHA-S04’s district expenditure/payroll accounts bear on different stages and places; both also describe dissent.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The available excerpts do not convert these heterogeneous observations into a comparable labour-days denominator.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Kunkel herself records dissent and local paid work; Pont Cháfer retains coercion and exceptions. They are not simple all-coerced versus all-free accounts.

**Inference limit:** Planned allocation, actual payment and voluntary acceptance are different measures. Exact archive overlap and the representativeness of selected districts are not audited; no countrywide adjudication is attempted.

## GHA-K09

In the Krachi–Salaga–Kpandai area, records of village requests in 1928 and later road initiatives and grants show farmers helping create feeder connections, while seeking materials, skilled work or government support they could not readily supply themselves.

**Cases:** GHA-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [GHA-S04: From Forced to Voluntary Labour in Rural Africa: The Transition to Paid Voluntary Labour on the Roads of the Northern Territories of the Gold Coast](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0020859022000657), “People’s Roads and Unpaid Voluntary Labour”, notes 100–109. Supports: Bajamse request, village-built connections, grants and requests for tools/bridges.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded report of inspected observations or evidence scope; no isolated causal effect is asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Officials and native authorities supplied assistance and administered funds; some earlier routes in the area had been made using forced labour.

**Inference limit:** Named initiatives do not establish all-household consent, universal access to grants, or that every local road was voluntary. Later oral testimony is not backdated to every interwar episode.

## GHA-K10

Pont Cháfer reports deterioration of the Bladjai feeder road after fuel shortages stopped exports in April 1945, consistent with maintenance responding to the usefulness of lorry access, but without isolating that mechanism.

**Cases:** GHA-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [GHA-S04: From Forced to Voluntary Labour in Rural Africa: The Transition to Paid Voluntary Labour on the Roads of the Northern Territories of the Gold Coast](https://doi.org/10.1017/S0020859022000657), “People’s Roads and Unpaid Voluntary Labour”, note 110 and surrounding discussion. Supports: Dated export interruption and reported road deterioration.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Fuel interruption stops lorry exports -> immediate practical return to maintenance falls -> reported deterioration of the road.
- **strongest rival:** Weather, wartime labour shortages or funding changes caused both the export interruption and reduced maintenance; official reporting may omit those factors.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated fuel, traffic, maintenance, weather and labour records for Bladjai and comparable roads unaffected by the fuel interruption.
- **evidence sought found:** GHA-S04 reports the April 1945 sequence from a contemporary district diary; no matched series inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Temporal order is informative but does not uniquely identify incentives or voluntariness.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Weather, labour availability, wartime conditions and funding may also have changed; absence of exports is not itself proof that all prior labour was voluntary.

**Inference limit:** Single qualitative sequence, no comparable control road, no traffic or labour-time denominator and no estimate of revenue or household welfare.

## GHA-K11

The GHA road/lorry history adds actor and institutional evidence to MOB-C04; it neither independently replicates nor by itself invalidates Jedwab–Moradi’s spatial railway estimates, whose historical discussion already draws on Austin and Hill.

**Cases:** GHA-C01. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [GHA-S05: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), January 2015 manuscript, §4.1 printed pp. 11–12 (southern cocoa-suitable sample), §5.1.3 printed p. 18 (Hill/Austin migration discussion), and references. Supports: Southern cocoa-suitable sample, spatial units and direct Austin/Hill references.
- [GHA-S01: Labour and land in Ghana, 1874–1939: a shifting ratio and an institutional revolution](https://eprints.lse.ac.uk/4392/1/Labour_and_land_in_Ghana_(LSERO_version).pdf), pp. 5–7 and references. Supports: Road/rail relationships and inherited historical source chain.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded report of inspected observations or evidence scope; no isolated causal effect is asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Historical summaries about early lorries and rail cocoa shares do not align transparently across the sources and warrant separate reconciliation.

**Inference limit:** This is a comparison of evidence scope and lineage, not an econometric reanalysis, an assertion of invalid identification, or a full welfare verdict.

## IND-K01

Agricola’s 1556 account presents human, animal and hydraulic mine-work choices together and includes repairable components and site access among practical considerations.

**Cases:** IND-C01. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S01: De re metallica, translated from the 1556 Latin edition](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/38015/38015-h/38015-h.htm), Book II pp. 31–33; Book VI pp. 171–185, especially replacement teeth p. 173 and staged pump pp. 184–185. Supports: Documents explicit technical choices and maintenance provisions in the treatise.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Texts and illustrations may describe exemplary arrangements rather than common practice.

**Inference limit:** Supports existence of described techniques and stated criteria, not measured prevalence, profitability or a universal choice rule.

## IND-K02

In sixteenth- and seventeenth-century central European mining, rod transmission extended the use of waterpower to pumps distant from the stream; non-steam engineering continued to change.

**Cases:** IND-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S02: Mine Pumping in Agricola’s Time and Later](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/31024/pg31024-images.html), pp. 117–120, especially pp. 118–119 and notes 13–20. Supports: Compares crank-driven pumps, remote rods, balanced transmission and later depictions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Remote hydraulic resource -> construct rod linkage -> mechanical power reaches distant pumps -> expanded site choice.
- **strongest rival:** Static post-Agricola mining would predict little relevant configuration change; a weaker rival accepts change but denies cost advantage.
- **discriminating observation:** Later mechanisms and installations absent from Agricola distinguish change; operating accounts are needed for cost advantage.
- **evidence sought found:** Read Agricola and Multhauf (IND-S01/S02); latter compares remote rod/ balanced transmission and later depictions.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Configuration change is supported; diffusion rate and net advantage remain unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Introduction dates vary; some depicted machinery is ambiguous. Some applications remained tied to local hydrology.

**Inference limit:** No region-wide diffusion denominator. Adequate hydraulics delaying German steam adoption is a further hypothesis, not established by this claim.

## IND-K03

Nuvolari’s Cornish comparison links early Newcomen use to tolerable fuel costs and the uptake of more fuel-efficient Watt engines to the expense of imported coal in Cornwall.

**Cases:** IND-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S03: Collective Invention during the British Industrial Revolution: The Case of the Cornish Pumping Engine](https://pure.tue.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/2343508/669221.pdf), working-paper pp. 10–12. Supports: Contrasts fuel-intensive atmospheric engines and Cornish demand for fuel economy; documents Boulton and Watt deployments.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** High fuel use plus local fuel price -> investor comparison -> fuel-saving design becomes more attractive -> Cornish deployment.
- **strongest rival:** Drainage demand or capital/engineering availability alone could yield deployment without a coal-price mechanism.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched engine choices and total costs across sites with comparable drainage but different delivered coal prices.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S03 pp. 10–12: coalfield/Cornish fuel contrast and condenser deployments; no matched-site experiment acquired.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Engine capability, finance and demand differ with geography.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Engine cost, drainage needs, labour, maintenance and royalties also entered investment decisions.

**Inference limit:** An historically supported pathway, not isolated identification of coal prices; cheap energy did not characterise every successful steam market.

## IND-K04

From 1811, Cornish engine reporting made performance and practices comparable across installations; Nuvolari interprets this information sharing and overlapping investment as supports for incremental improvement.

**Cases:** IND-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S03: Collective Invention during the British Industrial Revolution: The Case of the Cornish Pumping Engine](https://pure.tue.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/2343508/669221.pdf), pp. 14–20; figure 1 p. 32. Supports: Describes Lean’s reporting, engineering competition and cost-book ownership.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Comparable public engine reports -> engineer comparison and reputation -> incremental changes -> improved duty.
- **strongest rival:** New high-pressure designs and investment alone predict improving duty, even without disclosure.
- **discriminating observation:** Within comparable investment/design cohorts, reporting exposure predicts improvement; evidence of actual copying strengthens transmission.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S03 pp. 14–20: reporting intent, public comparisons, changing duty and shared ownership; original report series not audited.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Reporting, design, investment and district growth coincide.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** High-pressure designs and new investment arrived concurrently; a slack period followed patent expiry before reporting began.

**Inference limit:** Does not estimate the reporting effect separately from investment, engineers, fuel prices or design changes; original engine reports not directly audited.

## IND-K05

Watt’s fuel-saving royalties and patent enforcement generated documented conflict in Cornwall; Nuvolari argues that broad exclusion also obstructed rival steam development.

**Cases:** IND-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S03: Collective Invention during the British Industrial Revolution: The Case of the Cornish Pumping Engine](https://pure.tue.nl/ws/portalfiles/portal/2343508/669221.pdf), pp. 12–14. Supports: Describes royalty formula, disputes, Hornblower and post-expiry withdrawal of Cornish orders.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Broad patent enforcement -> restricted rival development -> delayed adoption of alternatives.
- **strongest rival:** Patent rents finance development and disclosure; litigation may protect appropriation rather than reduce total innovation.
- **discriminating observation:** Blocked independently workable designs and post-restriction improvement controlling for engineering capacity; also evidence of R&D financed by rents.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S03 pp. 12–14: Hornblower dispute, royalties, order cessation and subsequent slack period.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Dispute is stronger evidence than net innovation effect; expiry alone did not produce immediate progress.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Patents and royalties may also have financed development and dissemination; cessation of monopoly was not followed by immediate technical progress.

**Inference limit:** The direction and magnitude of net patent effects remain disputed. Distinguish the documented dispute from the author’s wider innovation judgement.

## IND-K06

In Crafts’s January 2002 reconstruction, steam contributed less than 0.01 percentage points per year to British labour-productivity growth before 1830, with a larger later contribution including railways.

**Cases:** IND-C02; IND-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S04: Productivity Growth in the Industrial Revolution: A New Growth Accounting Perspective](https://www.frbsf.org/wp-content/uploads/sites/4/crafts.pdf), pp. 13–19 and table 3 p. 23. Supports: Reports growth-accounting estimates and their stock/share assumptions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Early engine stocks are incomplete and unmeasured enabling effects may not be captured by aggregate accounting.

**Inference limit:** An estimate from the inspected version, not a timeless consensus or evidence that steam was unnecessary at individual sites. No cross-era forecast is made.

## IND-K07

Jonell and colleagues reconstruct unused waterpower potential across much of Britain alongside local constraints around Greater Manchester, and describe supplementary steam use in water-powered mills.

**Cases:** IND-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S05: Limited waterpower contributed to rise of steam power in British “Cottonopolis”](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11249955/), “Impact on the British textile industry”; hydropower and mill-census methods; abstract. Supports: Supports geographical heterogeneity and mixed-power operation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Local dependable water constraints -> mill seeks extra power -> steam supplement/replacement -> capacity expansion.
- **strongest rival:** Labour access/control or agglomeration could favour steam despite adequate water; national abundance predicts no shortage only if local access is ignored.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable mills/sites with usable flow versus scarce flow, plus seasonal output and expansion decisions.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S05 impact/method sections: local Manchester constraint alongside national potential and supplementary steam; compared IND-S06 coordination abstract.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Industrial clustering partly creates scarcity; reservoir costs, rights and labour conditions unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Reservoir construction, ownership, transmission and industrial location could alter usable supply; theoretical national potential is not economically deployable capacity.

**Inference limit:** Partial-text inspection only; underlying model and supplements not rerun. Local scarcity does not identify why factories originally clustered there.

## IND-K08

Malm argues that large proposed reservoir schemes in the 1820s–1830s failed partly because their collective financing and coordination requirements conflicted with manufacturers’ arrangements.

**Cases:** IND-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** low. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S06: Fleeing the Flowing Commons: Robert Thom, Water Reservoir Schemes, and the Shift to Steam Power in Early Nineteenth-Century Britain](https://portal.research.lu.se/en/publications/fleeing-the-flowing-commons-robert-thom-water-reservoir-schemes-a/), author-repository abstract. Supports: Explicitly attributes non-realisation of reservoir schemes to coordination and collective funding.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Shared reservoir requires funding and allocation -> manufacturers cannot coordinate -> proposed expansion not built.
- **strongest rival:** Projects may be physically unreliable or financially unattractive even under feasible collective governance.
- **discriminating observation:** Scheme correspondence and cost/flow evidence showing viable projects blocked specifically by coordination, compared with realised schemes.
- **evidence sought found:** Searched full article; only IND-S06 author abstract acquired. It explicitly makes the coordination claim; scheme-level evidence not found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Both rivals fit non-building. Weak-access claim retained only as attributed hypothesis.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Physical reliability, projected demand, land rights and cost assumptions are alternative or interacting explanations.

**Inference limit:** Abstract-only evidence: claim is about the author’s interpretation, not an independent finding that water was consistently cheaper or every proposed scheme feasible.

## IND-K09

Allen’s explanation assigns British mechanisation to labour being expensive relative to capital and energy, and subsequent wider diffusion to improvements that reduced other input requirements.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S07: The British Industrial Revolution in Global Perspective: chapter 1 excerpt](https://assets.cambridge.org/97805218/68273/excerpt/9780521868273_excerpt.pdf), chapter 1 excerpt pp. 1–3. Supports: States the factor-price and later engineering-diffusion thesis directly.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** High relevant labour cost relative to machinery/energy -> expected cost saving -> investment in labour-saving inventions/adoption.
- **strongest rival:** Low-paid spinning labour, quality requirements and skill/market complements could motivate or constrain adoption independently.
- **discriminating observation:** Task- and location-matched wages, output quality and machine payback comparisons before invention, including nonadopters.
- **evidence sought found:** Read Allen excerpt IND-S07 and archival-pay critique IND-S08; high-wage formulation is clear but spinner wage surge is challenged.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Allen’s underlying calculations and later responses not inspected; international and local wages use different comparisons.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Task-specific female wages, regional heterogeneity, skills, markets and political power complicate the aggregate comparison.

**Inference limit:** Attribution of a hypothesis; introductory excerpt does not validate Allen’s profitability calculations or universal foreign non-viability.

## IND-K10

Humphries and Schneider’s reconstructed British hand-spinning pay does not show a general wage surge immediately before the spinning inventions; it challenges including these workers in an undifferentiated high-wage economy.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S08: Spinning the Industrial Revolution](https://ora.ox.ac.uk/objects/uuid%3A4f321519-a0b7-4879-a521-139cbca81b25/download_file?safe_filename=Humphries_and_Schneider_Spinning_the_industrial_revolution.pdf&type_of_work=Journal+article), accepted-manuscript pp. 15–16, 25–32 and 37–41. Supports: Documents archival construction, source-type differences and absence of the proposed sharp wage increase.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Reconstructed pay by source type does not show the proposed general pre-invention surge -> weakens the proposed general wage-surge premise. This does not identify the marginal labour-cost return to a particular machine or exclude wage incentives.
- **strongest rival:** Samples overrepresent institutional/part-time low-output labour, concealing a high-wage commercial cotton margin.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable full-time commercial cotton spinning records with counts and hours, versus institutional and other fibres.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S08 source definitions, records and regressions: no general surge, source-type gaps, sparse cotton observations; explicit limits preserved.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Wage levels, wage changes, daily earnings, hourly labour costs and returns to a particular machine are different measures. Sparse commercial cotton records and working-time assumptions leave the relevant marginal incentive unresolved; alternative invention motives in IND-S08 remain speculative.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Cotton data are scarce, fibres and worker populations differ, and daily earnings involve time assumptions; Allen’s later rejoinders are not assessed here.

**Inference limit:** A challenge to a particular wage premise, not proof that factor prices never mattered or causal identification of quality control as the alternative motive.

## IND-K11

In 1835 Ure publicly advocated factory mechanisation partly as a means to substitute cheaper labour and reduce dependence on independent skilled workers.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S09: The Philosophy of Manufactures (selected passages)](https://sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu/mod/1835ure.asp), extract paragraphs beginning “The principle of the factory system”, “By the infirmity”, and “In the infancy of mechanical engineering”; compiler original-page ranges 20–23, 29–31. Supports: Records contemporary managerial advocacy for skill substitution and tractability.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Skilled mechanics and machine operatives remained necessary; Ure is a partisan advocate whose welfare claims are not corroborated by this extract.

**Inference limit:** Shows a stated aspiration, not universal employer intention, successful complete deskilling or demonstrated worker benefit.

## IND-K12

Kelly and Ó Gráda trace a pathway from instrument-making skill and precision measurement to complex textile machinery and the machine tools needed for repeated production and replacement parts.

**Cases:** IND-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S10: Connecting the Scientific and Industrial Revolutions: The Role of Practical Mathematics](https://www.econstor.eu/bitstream/10419/228197/1/1701742357.pdf), sections 2.1–2.3, printed pp. 13–18 (PDF pp. 15–20). Supports: Identifies skill transfer, gauges and exact tools, and the demand for reproducible wearing components.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Precision craft/measurement capabilities -> reproducible complicated components -> machinery supply and replacement -> sustained industrial use.
- **strongest rival:** Demand/capital expansion alone induces both tool skill and machinery; capabilities could be an outcome rather than prior constraint.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated personnel/tool transfers preceding new workable designs and matched sites with demand but missing skills.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S10 sections 2.1–2.3: named instrument/mechanic transfers, valve making and machine-tool/parts account; also simpler early artifacts acknowledged.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Demand and supply reinforce one another; broad national claims lack an exogenous comparison.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The jenny and atmospheric engine could use less elaborate capabilities; cross-country tool histories have other routes and political contingencies.

**Inference limit:** A historically supported pathway rather than a universal prerequisite; no independent causal effect of national skill endowment is estimated.

## IND-K13

Broadberry and Gupta’s reconstruction makes the timing of Lancashire’s competitive gains depend on raw-cotton and transport costs as well as wages and uneven productivity advances across spinning and weaving.

**Cases:** IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S11: Cotton Textiles and the Great Divergence: Lancashire, India and Shifting Competitive Advantage, 1600–1850](https://muslintrust.org/wp-content/uploads/2018/01/wrap_broadberry-cotton-textiles-and-the-great-diverge.pdf), printed pp. 16–25, 27, 33; tables 7–13. Supports: Reconstructs comparative wages, productivity proxies, raw cotton prices and market-specific delivery costs.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Productivity rises unevenly and cotton/freight costs change -> relative delivered cost shifts -> merchants source differently -> competitive reversal.
- **strongest rival:** Colonial policy and coercive market access could drive the same trade shifts without equivalent cost advantage.
- **discriminating observation:** Quality-matched delivered costs and shares across markets with different policy but similar transport, before and after price changes.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S11 pp. 16–25 and conclusion; compared IND-S13 policy testimony. The cost model includes cotton/freight but assumes some Indian productivity paths.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Policy shapes prices and markets; quality and labour-unit comparability remain incomplete.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Indian productivity stagnation is assumed for key intervals, and household earnings and labour units are not directly comparable; political market access remains a rival/complement.

**Inference limit:** Applies to the inspected 2005 model and reconstruction, not directly measured productivity for all Indian textiles or proof of tariffs’ irrelevance.

## IND-K14

Clingingsmith and Williamson propose that pre-1810 Indian textile decline involved domestic political/agricultural shocks, while later decline involved stronger international manufactured-price pressure.

**Cases:** IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S12: Mughal Decline, Climate Change, and Britain’s Industrial Ascent: An Integrated Perspective on India’s 18th and 19th Century Deindustrialization](https://www.nber.org/system/files/working_papers/w11730/w11730.pdf), sections 4–7, printed pp. 19–28. Supports: Uses a three-sector model and relative-price evidence to propose two phases.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Domestic agricultural/political shock -> dear grain and subsistence-linked nominal wages -> reduced textile competitiveness; later world textile prices add pressure.
- **strongest rival:** Colonial interventions or textile demand shifts could alter prices and employment without the posited agricultural cause.
- **discriminating observation:** District timing: independently measured harvest/drought/conflict shocks preceding wage pressure and textile contraction, including low-shock controls.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S12 model and relative-price/timing argument; no independently identified district treatment comparison found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Modelled labour mobility and wage linkage do causal work; price data alone fit multiple mechanisms.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Regional political histories and data are uneven; colonial intervention, quality changes and capital constraints are not independently isolated by these prices.

**Inference limit:** Model assumptions include mobile labour, subsistence-linked pay and initially non-traded grain. This is an interpretation, not exogenous identification of drought or Mughal decline.

## IND-K15

Inikori argues that Atlantic demand linked to slavery-based commodity production helped form the markets and regional expansion within which British cotton mechanisation developed.

**Cases:** IND-C04; IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S13: Atlantic Slavery and the Rise of the Capitalist Global Economy](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.1086/709818), sections I–III, especially section II paragraphs on Lancashire’s wages and Atlantic export sales. Supports: Connects regional protoindustry, Atlantic demand and later mechanisation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Slavery-linked Atlantic commodity trade -> expanding markets -> regional protoindustry growth -> investment and mechanisation incentives.
- **strongest rival:** Domestic markets, factor prices or engineering can explain innovation; Atlantic demand might follow rather than precede industrial advantage.
- **discriminating observation:** Regional/sectoral demand exposure preceding investment, with comparable low-exposure producers and separate supply/finance channels.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S13 regional wage/exports sequence and IND-S11 competition account; temporal market linkage found, no isolated necessity test.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Atlantic markets, shipping, wages and political power are reciprocally connected; export shares are not investment causality.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Factor prices, domestic demand, engineering and other trading regions also matter; regional wage rankings do not by themselves adjudicate international wage comparisons.

**Inference limit:** An attributed historical mechanism, not a quantified necessary contribution of slavery to each invention. Export demand is not the same variable as investment finance.

## IND-K16

Inikori’s discussion of Charles Grant’s 1821 testimony identifies asymmetric British and Indian tariff power as a contemporary concern alongside improvements in machinery.

**Cases:** IND-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S13: Atlantic Slavery and the Rise of the Capitalist Global Economy](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/10.1086/709818), section IV, Charles Grant paragraph immediately preceding table 3. Supports: Reproduces an explicit contemporary argument about protection at home and political power over Indian imports.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imperial tariff authority -> asymmetric protection/import permission -> altered producer choice set and market access.
- **strongest rival:** Productivity and freight advantages alone would produce the same import shift under symmetric sovereignty.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable products under differing tariff regimes and original legislative/merchant records on excluded choices.
- **evidence sought found:** Read Grant testimony as reproduced in IND-S13 section IV; sought original-record specificity but did not acquire parliamentary source.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Testimony establishes awareness and constrained sovereignty more firmly than the counterfactual trade magnitude.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The testimony does not establish a tariff counterfactual for each cloth quality or exclude cost-driven competitive shifts.

**Inference limit:** Primary testimony is accessed through Inikori’s quotation via Chaudhuri; original parliamentary record not independently inspected. No causal magnitude assigned.

## IND-K17

Japanese mills in the 1890s adapted ring spinning through a package involving cotton imports, mixed fibres, more preparation labour and a changed labour force, rather than merely copying American operating conditions.

**Cases:** IND-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S14: National Leadership and Competing Technological Paradigms: The Globalization of Cotton Spinning, 1878–1933](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1T5GYPrNlqQ3QoJPo_vzEwlDRF7LhR7IG/view), pp. 549–551, table 2, and conclusion p. 561. Supports: Combines order specifications with historical accounts of cotton preparation and workforce change.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imported fibre variety plus labour-intensive mixing -> reduced breakage/broader fibre control -> viable rings under Japanese conditions.
- **strongest rival:** Changes in machine design or yarn demand alone could improve performance without preparation reorganisation.
- **discriminating observation:** Within comparable machinery and yarn counts, mixing adoption precedes improved throughput/breakage; costs and work hours measured.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S14 pp. 549–551, order table and contemporary observations: timing of mixed-cotton orders and package change support the pathway.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Order proxies do not independently identify labour, fibres and design; exact realised productivity not observed.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Cotton prices, yarn markets and suppliers changed concurrently; order specifications are not direct measurements of realised mill productivity.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish the separate effect of each component, or that the labour arrangements were freely chosen or beneficial to workers.

## IND-K18

Between 1878 and 1914 mule and ring spinning both continued to improve; Saxonhouse and Wright interpret British mule persistence as a choice supported by skills and product flexibility, not simply stagnant technology.

**Cases:** IND-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [IND-S14: National Leadership and Competing Technological Paradigms: The Globalization of Cotton Spinning, 1878–1933](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1T5GYPrNlqQ3QoJPo_vzEwlDRF7LhR7IG/view), pp. 537–542 and 552–554. Supports: Documents coexisting technologies, skill requirements and adoption models using changing technical frontiers.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Mule-specific skills and continuing machine improvement -> sustained capability -> continued purchases where advantageous.
- **strongest rival:** Entrenched employers/unions or sunk capital produce persistence despite inferior returns.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare realised quality-adjusted profits and best-practice improvements in new mule purchases versus feasible rings, not only old stocks.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S14 performance specifications and choice model: both technologies improve before 1914 and multiple countries buy mules.
- **remaining ambiguity:** New orders weaken a pure obsolete-stock story but may still reflect expectations, omitted costs or institutional constraints.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Rings later gained capabilities and mule performance growth slowed; rationality at one time does not demonstrate optimal long-run investment.

**Inference limit:** Technical specification proxies and nonexperimental firm-choice models do not isolate learning, and the authors’ strong claim about unavoidable British decline is not adopted here.

## IND-K19

India supplied the largest number of high-draft machinery orders in Saxonhouse and Wright’s 1929–1933 dataset, a bounded counterexample to equating low wages with non-adoption of labour-saving equipment.

**Cases:** IND-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S14: National Leadership and Competing Technological Paradigms: The Globalization of Cotton Spinning, 1878–1933](https://drive.google.com/file/d/1T5GYPrNlqQ3QoJPo_vzEwlDRF7LhR7IG/view), pp. 558–559, high-draft discussion. Supports: Reports destination ordering and qualifications to the labour-relations explanation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Order count is not an installed-stock share or necessarily a comparable fraction of each country’s capacity.

**Inference limit:** Restricted to the supplier dataset and interval; the paper’s proposed male-workforce/labour-relations mechanism remains tentative.

## IND-K20

In late Qing and Republican China, some handweavers combined machine-spun warp with hand-spun weft, and cheaper factory yarn also supported handloom weaving in new districts.

**Cases:** IND-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S15: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/div-class-title-womenandapos-s-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980-div.pdf), pp. 373–374, notes 23–27. Supports: Describes mixed yarn use, Dingxian and geographical expansion of hand weaving.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cheap machine yarn -> replace selected spinning inputs -> handloom production retains product functions and expands accessible supply.
- **strongest rival:** General market growth alone may sustain hand weaving; poor households may simply be unable to buy cloth.
- **discriminating observation:** Yarn-price/access changes preceding weaving expansion, with product-level evidence of mixed warp/weft and comparison to inaccessible districts.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S15 pp. 373–374: explicit mixed yarn and named new weaving districts; original local studies not independently acquired.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Price, population, cash and fabric differences interact; mechanism stronger than national quantitative effect.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Hand spinning declined and factory cloth increased its share; hybrid persistence differed by region and product.

**Inference limit:** Earlier studies underlying Eyferth’s historical background were not independently acquired; no nationwide adoption rate is inferred from the examples.

## IND-K21

Eyferth reports that handloom cloth accounted for about 70 percent of China’s cotton textile output in 1934–1936, despite the expansion of factory production.

**Cases:** IND-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S15: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/div-class-title-womenandapos-s-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980-div.pdf), p. 374, estimate and note 28. Supports: Provides the bounded period and output denominator for the reported estimate.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Handloom market share was declining and aggregation may mask regional variation; the underlying estimation method was not audited.

**Inference limit:** An attributed estimate from earlier research, not a newly verified census; cannot equate output share with employment share or household welfare.

## IND-K22

Eyferth documents moral and gendered classifications of textile work in late imperial and Republican China, including reform rhetoric that rendered household female production economically invisible.

**Cases:** IND-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S15: Women’s Work and the Politics of Homespun in Socialist China, 1949–1980](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/1B4DC63727682F1EEC958A62A4126757/S0020859012000521a.pdf/div-class-title-womenandapos-s-work-and-the-politics-of-homespun-in-socialist-china-1949-1980-div.pdf), pp. 369–373, “Imagining a new national economy”. Supports: Contrasts gendered household ideals, diverse practice and later reformist definitions of productive work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Actual household divisions were more varied than prescriptive discourse; beliefs alone need not explain persistence when cash, prices and employment differ.

**Inference limit:** Documents classifications and interpretation, not freely expressed worker preferences. Post-1949 rationing mechanisms are excluded from this pre-1950 case.

## IND-K23

American factory electrification included distinct line-shaft, group-drive and unit-drive arrangements; replacing a prime mover with a motor did not automatically reorganise factory power distribution.

**Cases:** IND-C08. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S16: From Shafts to Wires: Historical Perspective on Electrification](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/automation/1983-devine.pdf), pp. 353–363, figure 2 and line-shaft/group-drive sections. Supports: Documents the configurations, early retrofits and the functional differences.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Group drive remained useful in some applications, and older configurations survived beyond the main transition.

**Inference limit:** The engineering distinction is well documented; it supplies neither an exact date for universal replacement nor proof that all firms benefited from unit drive.

## IND-K24

Devine links effective unit-drive use to compatible machinery, production redesign, utility expansion and application services, with sunk onsite equipment differentiating retrofits from new plants.

**Cases:** IND-C08. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [IND-S16: From Shafts to Wires: Historical Perspective on Electrification](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/automation/1983-devine.pdf), pp. 363, 368–371; machine redesign and Detroit Edison paragraphs. Supports: Describes indirect production benefits, compatibility constraints and services offered with electricity.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Compatible motors/tools plus redesign and service -> flexible local power -> productive reorganisation -> adoption benefits.
- **strongest rival:** Demand-led expansion or new capital generally causes both redesign and productivity, with motors only accompanying it.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched retrofits/new plants measuring workflow and output before/after, with comparable non-electric new equipment.
- **evidence sought found:** Read IND-S16 configuration history, redesign comments and utility installation services; evidence for needed complements found, no clean experiment.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Selective engineering cases and firm growth can bias productivity interpretation; unit drive not always superior.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Firms varied in load, process and existing capital; some group-drive choices remained justified. Expanding firms may have selected electrification.

**Inference limit:** Not causal identification of all postwar productivity gains; worker pace, discretion and welfare are not assessed by the engineering evidence.

## IND-K25

Devine reports that electric motors represented roughly one-quarter of US manufacturing mechanical-drive horsepower in 1909 and over half in 1919; he separately infers unit-drive diffusion because comprehensive configuration statistics were unavailable.

**Cases:** IND-C08. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [IND-S16: From Shafts to Wires: Historical Perspective on Electrification](https://gwern.net/doc/economics/automation/1983-devine.pdf), pp. 368–370, “Market penetration”. Supports: Provides the capacity denominator and explains indirect evidence for unit-drive timing.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** The bounded claim identifies a text, configuration, attributed estimate or observed dataset measure; it does not estimate the causes of the wider outcome. See inference_limits for the distinction.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Installed capacity is not utilisation, output, establishment adoption or the source of electricity generation.

**Inference limit:** Reuses the bounded historical series, not a reconstructed census; motor adoption must not be counted as complete factory redesign.

## LBR-K01

The AT&T historical study attributes delayed switching automation to organisational interdependencies and market-scale economics, rather than treating invention as immediate deployability.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [LBR-S01: Organizational and Economic Obstacles to Automation: A Cautionary Tale from AT&T in the Twentieth Century](https://pubsonline.informs.org/doi/10.1287/mnsc.2022.01760), Abstract and author-page summary. Supports: States adaptation and larger-market profitability mechanisms.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Interdependent manual service -> firm/technology adaptation -> viable switching at suitable scale -> staged adoption
- **strongest rival:** Purely technical improvement or wage-cost changes could drive timing without independent organisational causation.
- **discriminating observation:** Internal plans and comparable exchanges with different organisational readiness or market size.
- **evidence sought found:** LBR-S01 abstract reports archival interpretation; full mechanism evidence was not acquired, so no adjudication claimed.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The contribution cannot rank organisational adaptation versus technical and economic change.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Technology improvement and market growth were intertwined.

**Inference limit:** Abstract-level support; relative causal weights not audited.

## LBR-K02

In the telephone-automation study, incumbent operators and later young female cohorts experienced different labour-market outcomes; stable later-cohort employment did not mean incumbent workers were unharmed.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), §VI, §VII and conclusion p.1934. Supports: Linked incumbent outcomes differ from repeated-cohort employment findings.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Dial cutover removes switching jobs -> incumbents lose specialised job opportunity -> adverse decade-later employment/occupational outcomes, while future entrants reallocate
- **strongest rival:** Different local demand trends or non-random linkage/selection could explain cutover timing and measured incumbent outcomes. Such trends should also appear in comparable local controls or pre-cutover outcomes; selection should be sensitive to linkage and weighting checks.
- **discriminating observation:** Compare linked incumbents with local matched controls separately from future cohorts around cutover timing; inspect pre-trends, alternative controls and linkage/weighting robustness. These checks constrain but do not eliminate unobserved selection.
- **evidence sought found:** LBR-S02 §§III,VI,VII inspected; study uses these distinct designs and reports different outcomes.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Unobserved local changes, linked-sample selection and incomplete wages remain possible limitations.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Genealogical linkage is non-random; the authors report robustness and weighting rather than eliminating every possible selection process.

**Inference limit:** Inference is conditional on comparison/selection assumptions; not a universal automation result.

## LBR-K03

The main later-cohort comparison focuses on white American-born women aged 16–25, while incumbent operators are followed through a separate linked census sample; neither sample directly represents all affected workers.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), §III; Figures III/IV; Table VIII. Supports: Defines populations and separate linkage strategy.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The paper includes additional robustness populations, but the main population remains bounded.

**Inference limit:** Do not generalise to all women, racial groups, immigrants or displaced workers.

## LBR-K04

The authors find no clear overall wage effect for later young female cohorts, but the relevant tests cannot exclude wage changes around plus or minus 10 percent; no detected effect is not proof of unchanged pay.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), §VII.B, pp.1927–1928. Supports: Explicitly discusses power and wide bounds in smaller wage-analysis sample.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Heterogeneous effects and measurement noise could mask changes.

**Inference limit:** Applies to these wage tests, not an exact confidence interval for every subgroup.

## LBR-K05

The roughly 60 percent estimate of 2018 US employment in job titles added since 1940 is an imputation from new-title shares within occupations, not a direct count of workers in new micro-titles.

**Cases:** LBR-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S03: New Frontiers: The Origins and Content of New Work, 1940–2018](https://economics.mit.edu/sites/default/files/2026-04/New-Frontiers-QJE-v139%283%29-p1399-1465-Aug-2024.pdf), §II.B, Figure I and accompanying pp.1410–1412. Supports: Explains the estimate and unavailable micro-title employment counts.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Index revisions and within-occupation title weighting affect measurement.

**Inference limit:** Do not restate as 60 percent of occupations invented since 1940, or as identified jobs caused by technology.

## LBR-K06

The new-work study links title emergence to both output-complementing innovation and demand shifts; its patent-based categories may overlap and cannot exhaust technological change.

**Cases:** LBR-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [LBR-S03: New Frontiers: The Origins and Content of New Work, 1940–2018](https://economics.mit.edu/sites/default/files/2026-04/New-Frontiers-QJE-v139%283%29-p1399-1465-Aug-2024.pdf), §II.C pp.1413–1419; Tables IV/V discussion. Supports: Defines exposure limitations and estimates augmentation/demand relationships.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Changed productive possibilities or market demand -> changed tasks/output -> new job specialties and employment allocation
- **strongest rival:** Relabelling and classification changes, or shared demand trends, could create apparent new work without substantive new tasks.
- **discriminating observation:** Check source-title consistency and compare innovation/demand exposures with placebo timing and alternative specifications.
- **evidence sought found:** LBR-S03 §II and Tables IV/V discuss construction, IV and placebo strategies; raw data not replicated.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Classification and identification assumptions remain; title addition does not directly measure worker entry costs or bargaining.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Automation and augmentation exposures are positively correlated; classification itself may contribute to overlap.

**Inference limit:** A US constructed-data study; neither title novelty nor patent similarity alone proves an accessible job opportunity.

## LBR-K07

A task-allocation model distinguishes labour displacement, productivity effects and creation of new labour tasks; positive productivity alone need not imply increased labour demand.

**Cases:** method/synthesis. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S04: Automation and New Tasks: How Technology Displaces and Reinstates Labor](https://economics.mit.edu/sites/default/files/publications/Automation%20and%20New%20Tasks%20-%20How%20Technology%20Displace.pdf), Introduction pp.3–5 and abstract. Supports: Sets out the model channels and their distinct implications.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a bounded measurement/design or author argument; no new causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Aggregate demand, ownership, institutions and adjustment alter realised outcomes.

**Inference limit:** This is a model distinction, not an unconditional causal claim about every technology.

## LBR-K08
