Feigenbaum and Gross note that mechanical-switching technicians were nearly all male; their cohort estimates show no effect of cutovers on young white American-born women’s employment in non-operator telephone jobs.

**Cases:** LBR-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [LBR-S02: Answering the Call of Automation: How the Labor Market Adjusted to Mechanizing Telephone Operation](https://www.dpgross.com/papers/answering-call-automation.pdf), pp.1928–1929, direct labour-demand discussion and FigureV. Supports: Gender composition of technicians and distinct successive-cohort test of complementary telephone employment.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports the study’s occupation composition and scoped result; not a new causal estimate of exclusion or an opportunity forecast.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some linked incumbent operators moved to other telephone jobs; this is a different population and does not contradict the cohort result.

**Inference limit:** A maintenance task does not establish an accessible transition for displaced women. The study does not isolate the causes of gendered entry or prove that no individual entered such work.

## MAT-K01

Xianrendong pottery-bearing contexts were dated to approximately 20,000–19,000 calibrated years BP, long before farming; this dates surviving use, not the invention of pottery.

**Cases:** MAT-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S01: Early Pottery at 20,000 Years Ago in Xianrendong Cave, China](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/228089260_Early_Pottery_at_20000_Years_Ago_in_Xianrendong_Cave_China), pp. 1697–1699; Tables 1–2. Supports: Associated bone/charcoal dates and micromorphological checks of the deposits.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports contextual dating, not a causal explanation of adoption.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The dated materials are associated deposits; some strata were affected by small-scale bioturbation.

**Inference limit:** No first inventor, unique origin, or regional prevalence follows from this site.

## MAT-K02

Xianrendong fragments document vessel-making among foragers, while exterior burning makes cooking plausible rather than identifying particular foods.

**Cases:** MAT-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S01: Early Pottery at 20,000 Years Ago in Xianrendong Cave, China](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/228089260_Early_Pottery_at_20000_Years_Ago_in_Xianrendong_Cave_China), pp. 1696–1697. Supports: Forager context, temper, forming methods, and burned exterior surfaces.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes vessel evidence and a qualified use inference, not why people adopted pottery.

**Counterevidence/rival:** No reconstructed complete vessels or direct residue identification in this study.

**Inference limit:** Multiple sherds do not establish routine household deployment or mass production.

## MAT-K03

The Japanese sample maintained a strong aquatic-processing association across the Pleistocene–Holocene transition, challenging a simple account in which pottery expanded principally to process newly abundant terrestrial foods.

**Cases:** MAT-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S02: The impact of environmental change on the use of early pottery by East Asian hunter-gatherers](https://www.pnas.org/doi/full/10.1073/pnas.1803782115), Results and Discussion; Table 1; Conclusion. Supports: 801 samples across 46 sites; conservative aquatic assignments of 73/156 and 256/566 lipid-bearing samples in the two periods.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Warming changes available foods -> users adopt/use more pots for new terrestrial processing -> pottery expands and contents diversify. The evidence tests the predicted functional change.
- **strongest rival:** Continued aquatic processing with changing fishing practices or social conventions also permits ceramic expansion.
- **discriminating observation:** Time-matched, preservation-controlled changes in plant/terrestrial versus aquatic residues, combined with independent food availability and vessel production measures.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S02 explicitly tested environmental/period expectations and found strong aquatic continuity; it did not identify every pot’s contents or measure all household use.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Poorly detected plants and lipid mixing remain possible; residue continuity cannot by itself identify a psychological cultural norm.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Plant foods and low-lipid contents are imperfectly detectable; functional continuity need not imply unchanged diet.

**Inference limit:** The sample concerns surviving sampled ceramics, not shares of all food or all households; motives remain interpretive.

## MAT-K04

In the surveyed southern Levant contexts, utilitarian iron appears in Iron I but becomes dominant among classified metal tools/weapons only in Iron IIA, while bronze manufacture continues.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 286–294; abstract; Iron Production during the Iron IIA. Supports: Contextual counts distinguish initial use from tenth–ninth-century BCE dominance and continuing bronze work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports chronology and assemblage composition rather than a cause.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Recovery, corrosion and recycling differ by metal and context.

**Inference limit:** Archaeological assemblage dominance is not a regional household adoption percentage.

## MAT-K05

The southern Levant evidence weakens an explanation that a general bronze-input shortage forced the transition: copper production continued and the authors report persistent tin-bronze compositions.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 298–299, Copper Production and Bronzeworking. Supports: Arabah copper chronology and 95 copper-alloy objects with reported average tin contents maintained around 5–6 wt%.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Bronze inputs become generally scarce -> producers/users substitute iron -> iron gains use. The claim tests the broad scarcity premise.
- **strongest rival:** Continued bronze supply plus changed iron production organisation can yield the same transition without general shortage.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated procurement interruptions or price/access deterioration preceding substitution, contrasted with areas retaining supply.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S03 pp. 298–299 reports copper-production continuity and maintained alloy tin levels; prices and distribution were not found, and the tin dataset is unpublished there.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Stable composition can coexist with scarcity, higher prices or selective allocation; geographically local shocks remain possible.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The 95-object chemical dataset was unpublished; scarcity could affect prices or places without changing surviving alloy percentages.

**Inference limit:** Does not disprove every local or short-lived supply disruption; neither prices nor access distributions are observed.

## MAT-K06

The inspected Canaan study does not find a heat-treatment breakthrough that accounts for iron dominance; carbon content alone is insufficient evidence of deliberate superior steelmaking.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 295–297; p. 300. Supports: Metallography, corrosion limits, spontaneous carburisation, and absence of a clear chronological technical leap.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Improved heat treatment -> reliably superior iron objects -> users replace bronze. The claim tests a chronological breakthrough.
- **strongest rival:** More reliable/available iron supply or institutional demand can expand use without a new heat-treatment threshold.
- **discriminating observation:** A well-dated step change in treatment and task-specific performance preceding adoption, with comparable preservation and alloy alternatives.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S03 pp. 295–300 reports no clear technical leap in sampled material and explains carbon/corrosion ambiguities.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Corrosion, unpublished detail and missing exceptional workshops may hide changes; hardness alone is not complete performance.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Preservation restricts metallography; exceptional objects may escape the sample.

**Inference limit:** Failure to detect a breakthrough does not prove equal cost, durability, repairability or battlefield performance.

## MAT-K07

Iron work associated with older bronze-working areas and public architecture makes local craft adaptation and administrative support plausible contributors to systematic production.

**Cases:** MAT-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S03: The Transition from Bronze to Iron in Canaan: Chronology, Technology, and Context](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/275250407_The_Transition_from_Bronze_to_Iron_in_Canaan_Chronology_Technology_and_Context), pp. 291–293, 299–300. Supports: Spatial/chronological association of metalworking and evidence for urban smelting or forging.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Existing metal skills plus administrative support -> local smelting/forging capacity -> sustained iron production and wider use.
- **strongest rival:** Co-location may reflect ordinary urban facilities or market access, while iron demand draws administrations to existing work.
- **discriminating observation:** Sequenced production investment, inventories, institutional orders or controlled access preceding expansion; comparison with independent workshops.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S03 documents metalworking continuity and public-architecture association, but no contracts or direct ownership evidence.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Reverse causation and shared urban location fit the record; early production remains are sparse.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Iron I production remains are lacking; imported iron or mobile specialists remain possible.

**Inference limit:** Workshop location alone neither identifies ownership nor demonstrates compulsion, funding or a state monopoly.

## MAT-K08

Chinese iron history requires separate chronologies for meteoritic objects, bloomery production, casting and practical cast-iron implements; later finds revised earlier accounts of a wholly southern, cast-iron beginning.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S04: The earliest use of iron in China](https://wuecampus.uni-wuerzburg.de/moodle/pluginfile.php/4671985/mod_resource/content/1/Wagner%2C%20Donald%20B.%20The%20earliest%20use%20of%20iron%20in%20China.pdf), sections 1, 3.4 and 4. Supports: Explicit revision of the author’s previous reconstruction.
- [MAT-S15: Questioning Diversity (of Iron) in the Workplace: Bloomery Iron, Cast Iron, China and the West](https://intarch.ac.uk/journal/issue69/14/full-text.html), Table 1; sections 2–3. Supports: Differentiates first appearance, production and functional use, with disputed dates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes distinctions in evidence and changes in scholarship; no transmission mechanism asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Early object dating and production identification remain contested; surviving objects do not locate first smelters.

**Inference limit:** Supports branching stages, not a definite single transmission route or inventor.

## MAT-K09

Chinese cast-iron production plausibly combined iron smelting with bronze-casting and ceramic/kiln expertise, but the claim that political suppression explains the absence of alternative iron routes remains a hypothesis.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MAT-S15: Questioning Diversity (of Iron) in the Workplace: Bloomery Iron, Cast Iron, China and the West](https://intarch.ac.uk/journal/issue69/14/full-text.html), sections on cast-iron annealing and Access to technology; concluding discussion. Supports: Proposes cross-craft integration and discusses moulds, kilns, fuel, labour and state power.
- [MAT-S04: The earliest use of iron in China](https://wuecampus.uni-wuerzburg.de/moodle/pluginfile.php/4671985/mod_resource/content/1/Wagner%2C%20Donald%20B.%20The%20earliest%20use%20of%20iron%20in%20China.pdf), section 4. Supports: Earlier hypothesis connecting agricultural implement forms with iron casting.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Links among smelting, casting and ceramic expertise -> controlled casting/treatment -> practical cast-iron output; state support may organise this bundle.
- **strongest rival:** Local ore/task economics and independent craft exchange could create the same bundle without coercive political suppression.
- **discriminating observation:** Securely phased co-located processes and independent evidence of organisation; competing production under/away from claimed restrictions.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S15 proposes the bundle and stronger political account; MAT-S04 documents revised early histories. No enforcement archive was inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Facilities identify technical contributions more securely than ownership, suppression or counterfactual western failure.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The 2025 comparison aggregates diverse regions and infers institutions partly from outcomes.

**Inference limit:** No comparative cost accounts or direct policy enforcement evidence here identify the causal contribution of suppression.

## MAT-K10

Song–Yuan mineral fuel in iron manufacture is supported by texts and anomalously old carbon dates in castings, but a universal shift to coke, or one exact production route, is not established.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S05: Blast Furnaces in Song–Yuan China](https://donwagner.dk/arch-iron/resources/Wagner2001.pdf), pp. 51–53 and 58–61. Supports: Su Shi text, sulphur evidence, radiocarbon results, and distinction between smelting and remelting.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes support for mineral-fuel use and its observational limits.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Charcoal remained possible in other furnaces; carbon can enter during remelting as well as smelting.

**Inference limit:** Neither national adoption share nor coal/coke ratio follows from the few dated objects.

## MAT-K11

The often-repeated large estimate of Song iron output is not a secure observation: Wagner explicitly rejects the reliability of the extrapolation from state quota figures.

**Cases:** MAT-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S05: Blast Furnaces in Song–Yuan China](https://donwagner.dk/arch-iron/resources/Wagner2001.pdf), p. 42 n. 6. Supports: Discusses the roughly 115,000 metric tonne estimate and its flawed underlying assumptions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports an explicit methodological criticism of an estimate.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The author considers the magnitude plausible; criticism does not establish low output.

**Inference limit:** Do not use disputed output as a denominator for productivity or an adoption rate.

## MAT-K12

Meroe replica smelts improved after changes in ore, air delivery and operation, demonstrating coupled operating constraints rather than the sufficiency of a copied furnace shape.

**Cases:** MAT-C04. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S06: Iron Smelting in Sudan: Experimental Archaeology at The Royal City of Meroe](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/58594bce-8624-430b-822e-bdec34bf76e6/content), Tables 2–4; Lessons Learned, pp. 412–415. Supports: Failed/low-yield trials, changes to ores and bellows, viable later operation and explicit experimental limitations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Suitable ore plus adjusted blast/charging and operator coordination -> better reduction/slag behaviour -> more workable products in replica trials.
- **strongest rival:** Different modern operating choices could also succeed; apparent improvement could result from one unisolated variable rather than the whole historical reconstruction.
- **discriminating observation:** Controlled replication changing one parameter at a time, matching archaeological residues and retaining detailed mass balances.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S06 Tables 2–4 and Lessons Learned record failed/low-yield runs and improved combinations; authors flag anecdotal later data.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Joint changes prevent attribution to each input; viability is demonstrated only for tested modern configurations, not a unique ancient procedure.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Parameters changed together; some later successes lacked detailed records.

**Inference limit:** Modern reconstructed feasibility does not identify the unique ancient process or provide an ancient output series.

## MAT-K13

Charcoal analysis at Meroe and Hamadab shows persistent, selective use of Acacia type nilotica in metallurgical contexts across a long production history.

**Cases:** MAT-C04. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S07: Fuel selection during long-term ancient iron production in Sudan](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/332221733_Fuel_selection_during_long-term_ancient_iron_production_in_Sudan), Tables 1–2; pp. 34–39 and 45–47. Supports: 1,754 analysed charcoal fragments overall; strong metallurgical dominance contrasted with limited non-metallurgical assemblages.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes observed charcoal selection over dated contexts.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Comparison contexts are small and not always contemporaneous; taxonomic identification sometimes tentative.

**Inference limit:** Persistent fuel selection does not by itself prove woodland management, local sourcing or unchanged production intensity.

## MAT-K14

Meroe’s charcoal assemblages do not establish smelting-induced deforestation; stable fuel access is compatible with management, lower intensity, efficient operation or imports.

**Cases:** MAT-C04. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MAT-S07: Fuel selection during long-term ancient iron production in Sudan](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/332221733_Fuel_selection_during_long-term_ancient_iron_production_in_Sudan), pp. 47–50, especially Conclusions. Supports: Authors explicitly enumerate alternative supply explanations and unknown exchange range.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Sustained iron demand -> heavy local wood removal -> depletion/changed fuel use or production decline; charcoal should register some consequences if local sourcing persists.
- **strongest rival:** Managed regeneration, smaller output, efficient operation or fuel imports maintain selected charcoal despite different local ecological conditions.
- **discriminating observation:** Independent vegetation history and charcoal provenance/procurement radius alongside dated production intensity.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S07 finds persistent selection and explicitly enumerates alternative supply explanations, with no demonstrated shortage signal.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Imports or expanding catchments can hide local degradation; charcoal selectivity is not a woodland census.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Ecological degradation could coexist with continued access through a widening collection catchment.

**Inference limit:** No landscape-scale vegetation reconstruction or procurement-cost series is supplied; absence of a charcoal signal is not proof of ecological harmlessness.

## MAT-K15

Controlled comparisons show that tin bronze’s greater possible hardness does not make it universally superior to arsenical bronze; ductility and sheet-forming performance can favour the latter.

**Cases:** MAT-C05. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 500–508; Tables 4–6; Figs. 25–27. Supports: Mechanical tests and comparison with thin-sheet fabrication traditions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Alloy composition and working treatment -> different hardness/ductility/toughness -> different suitability for specified sheet-forming tasks.
- **strongest rival:** A universal tin-bronze superiority account predicts better performance across relevant tasks, rather than a trade-off.
- **discriminating observation:** Controlled processing of both alloys followed by relevant mechanical tests and task failure assessment.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S08 directly tests alloys and identifies different advantages; pp. 500–508 discuss limits of elongation data.
- **remaining ambiguity:** This tests material behaviour, not whether historical smiths selected it for the inferred reason or whether costs favoured it.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some tests need more reproducible elongation measurements; alloy concentration and processing strongly affect results.

**Inference limit:** Modern mechanical advantage is not a complete explanation of archaeological selection or user value.

## MAT-K16

Experimental cosmelting of Peruvian copper oxides with arsenic-bearing sulphide ores produced usable copper–arsenic alloys across multiple mixtures, supplying a feasible alternative to a separate roasting sequence.

**Cases:** MAT-C05. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S09: The Production of Copper–Arsenic Alloys (Arsenic Bronze) by Cosmelting: Modern Experiment, Ancient Practice](https://www.scribd.com/document/374679205/Lechtman-H-The-Production-of-Copper-Arsenic-Alloys-by-Co-Smelting), pp. 497–499, 523–526; Table 11. Supports: Crucible/furnace tests without pre-roasting or added flux and discussion of naturally mixed versus deliberately mixed ores.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes achieved modern experimental feasibility, not a causal historical adoption account.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Feasible experiments do not show which ancient workshops used this process or how often.

**Inference limit:** No universal origin sequence, automatic intention, ancient occupational-exposure level or cost advantage can be inferred.

## MAT-K17

The Andean history summarized by Lechtman includes continued arsenical-bronze use alongside Inka tin bronze, making coexistence and political distribution more appropriate than a complete superior-alloy replacement story.

**Cases:** MAT-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 477–479. Supports: Regional ore geography, coexistence and interpretation of Inka alloy distribution.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imperial access/control over tin and distribution -> supply of tin bronze across political space -> partial substitution with local continuities.
- **strongest rival:** Ore geography, transport costs and task performance can generate regional compositions without imperial standardisation as the primary cause.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable sites before/after incorporation, official versus local products, and independently established control of mines/flows.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S08 pp. 477–479 reports coexistence and interprets political distribution; underlying local-control studies not independently inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Political and resource effects likely interact; no identified causal effect or complete displacement.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Underlying mine-control and closure evidence was not independently audited in this contribution.

**Inference limit:** Political explanation is plausible but not identified against transport costs, ore supply and task-specific demand.

## MAT-K18

Hosler’s West Mexican studies describe tools as well as objects whose sound and colour mattered, so technological performance cannot be reduced to cutting hardness or military lethality.

**Cases:** MAT-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S10: Sound, color and meaning in the metallurgy of Ancient West Mexico](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/00438243.1995.9980295), abstract. Supports: Distinguishes tools/status items and relates bells and metallic colours to ritual and war symbolism.
- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 506–508. Supports: Mechanical/physical properties and American object selection.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Particular sonic/visual values -> patrons/users select bells and colours -> smiths develop relevant material properties.
- **strongest rival:** Functional availability, general display demand or analyst selection may produce similar assemblages without shared specific beliefs.
- **discriminating observation:** Object-level material choices combined with independently contextualised historical/linguistic evidence and non-elite comparisons.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S10 abstract states laboratory and ethnohistoric interpretation; MAT-S08 discusses American physical properties. Full symbolic argument unavailable.
- **remaining ambiguity:** No independently audited belief evidence here; not every consumer or object need share the proposed meanings.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The 1995 full text was not acquired; frequency and direct historical passages have not been audited.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish that every object was ritual, that all consumers shared values, or that productive utility was absent.

## MAT-K19

Hosler proposed South/Central American transmission into West Mexico combined with local technical transformation; this is a claim about selective recombination, not passive copying.

**Cases:** MAT-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MAT-S11: Ancient West Mexican Metallurgy: South and Central American Origins and West Mexican Transformations](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1525/aa.1988.90.4.02a00040), abstract. Supports: Two proposed phases of technical links and explicit local reinterpretation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Interregional contact -> movement of know-how/components -> local recombination -> distinctive West Mexican production.
- **strongest rival:** Convergence from similar resources/tasks can create similarities without the proposed transfer; object movement alone may not transmit skills.
- **discriminating observation:** Diagnostic production sequences, intermediate chronology, source/provenance and contact evidence evaluated together.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S11 abstract proposes two transmission phases and transformations; detailed supporting catalogue unavailable.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Neither routes, carriers nor first dates independently verified; similarity alone fits several accounts.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Only abstract read; dates differ between the 1988 and 1995 formulations. Similarity alone cannot exclude convergence.

**Inference limit:** Transfer route and carriers remain unverified here; neither isolation nor a complete imported package is assumed.

## MAT-K20

Chemical study of 52 Igbo Olokun beads supports locally distinctive glass groups; related beads elsewhere support exchange, while imported glass at other Ile-Ife sites rules out a wholly isolated setting.

**Cases:** MAT-C07. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S13: Chemical analysis of glass beads from Igbo Olokun, Ile-Ife (SW Nigeria): New light on raw materials, production, and interregional interactions](https://www.academia.edu/35714341/Chemical_analysis_of_glass_beads_from_Igbo_Olokun_Ile_Ife_SW_Nigeria_New_light_on_raw_materials_production_and_interregional_interactions_Babalola_et_al_2018_JAS_90_), sections 2, 3.4, 4.1–4.3 and 5; Table 3. Supports: Selected-sample chemistry, local raw-material compatibility, regional distribution and other-site soda-lime glass.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Local glass production -> beads circulate through exchange -> related chemical groups occur elsewhere.
- **strongest rival:** Multiple makers using similar raw materials or redistribution from an unlocated production centre could share chemical signatures.
- **discriminating observation:** Distinctive trace patterns tied to production debris and secure regional chronology; tests of other potential sources.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S13 reports local bead chemistry, production debris and regional matches, with imported glass in other city contexts.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Local production is well supported, but origin of every distant bead and direction/organisation of exchange are not uniquely identified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** 52 beads are a small selected fraction; deposits disturbed; compatible raw materials are not a uniquely sourced quarry.

**Inference limit:** No import share for the entire city, market size, annual output or proven transmission of production skills follows.

## MAT-K21

Ile-Ife glass crucibles were specialised ceramic complements; reported usable capacities are evidence of batch capability, not a measurement of sustained industrial throughput.

**Cases:** MAT-C07. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S12: The Glass Making Crucibles from Ile-Ife, SW Nigeria](https://repository.cyi.ac.cy/bitstream/CyI/1939/1/Babalola%20et%20al%202018%20Glassmaking%20crucibles%20Ile-Ife%20JAA%2016.pdf), pp. 31–32, abstract. Supports: Ten analysed samples, refractory clay, estimated 1–7 L usable capacities.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes reported crucible capacities and measurement scope.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Body tables not acquired; vessel reconstruction and actual fill levels carry uncertainty.

**Inference limit:** Do not multiply capacity by an invented number of firings, workshops or years.

## MAT-K22

Incompletely reacted glass batch on Ile-Ife crucibles directly strengthens the case for local primary glassmaking, while recipe uniqueness alone cannot establish an origin independent of all external ideas.

**Cases:** MAT-C07. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MAT-S14: Semi-finished glass from Ile-Ife, Nigeria: implications for the archaeology of glass in sub-Saharan Africa](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10104101/3/Rehren_Babalola%20et%20al%20Antiquity%202020%20final%20version.pdf), accepted manuscript pp. 2–4, Laboratory analysis and Implications. Supports: Residual quartz/feldspar, new reaction phases and heterogeneous glass distinguish raw-batch melting from only remelting finished imports.
- [MAT-S13: Chemical analysis of glass beads from Igbo Olokun, Ile-Ife (SW Nigeria): New light on raw materials, production, and interregional interactions](https://www.academia.edu/35714341/Chemical_analysis_of_glass_beads_from_Igbo_Olokun_Ile_Ife_SW_Nigeria_New_light_on_raw_materials_production_and_interregional_interactions_Babalola_et_al_2018_JAS_90_), sections 4.2–4.3. Supports: Locally compatible recipe and participation in exchange.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Identifies material evidence of a production operation; does not claim an adoption cause or independent-invention mechanism.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Museum-store fragments lack newly excavated sealed contexts; authors advance a stronger autonomous-origin interpretation.

**Inference limit:** Local primary production is better established than the exact invention date, transmission history or subsequent disappearance.

## MAT-K23

Recycling and composite manufacture complicate inferences from surviving iron objects to initial production, replacement rates or material scarcity.

**Cases:** MAT-C02; MAT-C03. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MAT-S16: The sword that was broken …: The detection of recycled iron in the archaeological record](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/arcm.12892), Introduction; Iron production and the forms of iron recycling; Conclusions. Supports: Detection problems and selected examples of reused iron; distinction between manufacture and reuse.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Reuse/composite fabrication -> multiple material histories within surviving objects -> object count/chemistry does not uniquely identify new production.
- **strongest rival:** New composite manufacture without old scrap can produce some similar structures; differential preservation also changes counts.
- **discriminating observation:** Diagnostic reused fragments or incompatible material histories, compared with production structures of known new composites.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S16 supplies selected examples and explicitly warns that common manufacturing features can mimic recycling.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Methodological non-identifiability is supported; no recycling rate or actual local pathway is measured for MAT-C02/C03.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some structural evidence is consistent with new composite manufacture as well as recycling.

**Inference limit:** A methodological warning, not a recycling percentage for either case; selective later parallels are not proof of earlier local practice.

## MAT-K24

Across these purposively selected cases, repeatable processes, complementary crafts and task-specific demand are better-supported comparison variables than a universal ranking of materials.

**Cases:** MAT-C01; MAT-C02; MAT-C03; MAT-C04; MAT-C05; MAT-C06; MAT-C07. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MAT-S02: The impact of environmental change on the use of early pottery by East Asian hunter-gatherers](https://www.pnas.org/doi/full/10.1073/pnas.1803782115), Results and Conclusion. Supports: Functional persistence despite changes in availability.
- [MAT-S06: Iron Smelting in Sudan: Experimental Archaeology at The Royal City of Meroe](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/58594bce-8624-430b-822e-bdec34bf76e6/content), Lessons Learned and Conclusion. Supports: Coupled operating variables.
- [MAT-S08: Arsenic Bronze: Dirty Copper or Chosen Alloy? A View from the Americas](https://www.academia.edu/31356952/Lechtman_Arsenic_Bronze_Dirty_Copper_1996), pp. 500–508. Supports: Task-dependent alloy properties.
- [MAT-S14: Semi-finished glass from Ile-Ife, Nigeria: implications for the archaeology of glass in sub-Saharan Africa](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10104101/3/Rehren_Babalola%20et%20al%20Antiquity%202020%20final%20version.pdf), Laboratory analysis. Supports: Local raw-material transformation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Task requirements and accessible complementary operations -> workable production/adoption choices -> branching and coexisting technologies.
- **strongest rival:** Different episodes may instead be dominated by independent resource shocks or political power; purposive selection may overstate recurrence.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched task/region contrasts with failed production, available alternatives and independent measures of demand/institutions.
- **evidence sought found:** MAT-S02, S06, S08 and S14 supply different kinds of bounded evidence; they do not constitute a representative comparative design.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Cross-case recurrence is not causal identification; outcomes and source survival affect selection.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Selection emphasises contrasting outcomes and cannot estimate how frequently any mechanism dominated.

**Inference limit:** A research framework rather than causal identification, universal law or forecast.

## MED-K01

Early eighteenth-century reports connect British variolation discussions to Ottoman practitioners, including women, and African expertise conveyed through Onesimus; this is evidence of transmission rather than a British origin.

**Cases:** MED-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S01: The origins of inoculation](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1258/jrsm.2012.12k044), Inoculation in parts of the Ottoman Empire and Europe. Supports: Reproduces the relevant reports and correspondence.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about documented knowledge channels, not their causal share in uptake.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Surviving elite intermediaries may obscure earlier local transmission.

**Inference limit:** No continental prevalence, precise birthplace or unique transmission route is inferred.

## MED-K02

The inspected variolation evidence does not establish a unique origin or secure ancient chronology.

**Cases:** MED-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S01: The origins of inoculation](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1258/jrsm.2012.12k044), Inoculation in China and India; Conclusions. Supports: Contradictory testimony, different methods and uncertain early dates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Sparse surviving accounts -> reconstruction of routes -> uncertain origin attribution
- **strongest rival:** Independent development or earlier undocumented diffusion also fits surviving accounts
- **discriminating observation:** Contemporaneous dated descriptions with identifiable technique and transmission contacts
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S01 checked early-origin claims against its source criticism; found uncertainty and conjecture
- **remaining ambiguity:** Original local-language records not independently inspected.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Boylston proposes a common route, but labels parts speculative.

**Inference limit:** Neither silence in surviving texts nor similarity of methods proves absence or common descent.

## MED-K03

Ottoman vaccination policy combined free provision commitments, multilingual instruction and religious endorsement before and alongside later compulsory rules.

**Cases:** MED-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S02: Mandating immunity in the Ottoman Empire: A history of public health education and compulsory vaccination](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7689169/), §2 Normalizing and facilitating vaccination. Supports: 1840 free-provision decree, 1845 endorsement and 1846 multilingual treatise.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Describes documented policy instruments, not their effectiveness.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Policy texts describe intended provision, not universal performance.

**Inference limit:** No effect size for price, education or religious messaging is identified.

## MED-K04

Ottoman universal-vaccination ambitions were constrained by vaccine production, personnel and rural delivery capacity; legal scope exceeded documented implementation.

**Cases:** MED-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S02: Mandating immunity in the Ottoman Empire: A history of public health education and compulsory vaccination](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7689169/), §§2–5. Supports: Contrasts legislative expansion with production and staffing limitations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Wide mandate plus limited supplies/staff -> inability to deliver repeatedly -> incomplete coverage
- **strongest rival:** Adequate provision with refusal could also yield low coverage
- **discriminating observation:** Local records of stock availability, staff visits and refusal under the same rule
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S02 explicitly contrasts mandate with resource limits and easier institutional reach; no comparable district data supplied
- **remaining ambiguity:** Capacity constraints supported, relative contribution against distrust unmeasured.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Fear and refusal, instability and war could also depress uptake.

**Inference limit:** No district panel separates supply, trust and coercion effects.

## MED-K05

The 1885 Ottoman rules conditioned school entry and state-linked roles on vaccination evidence; later rules enlarged obligations and penalties, so legally induced uptake cannot be equated with consent.

**Cases:** MED-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S02: Mandating immunity in the Ottoman Empire: A history of public health education and compulsory vaccination](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7689169/), §3 1885 and 1894 regulations; §4 1903 and 1915 expansions. Supports: Specific conditions, fines and certification rules.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Documents compulsion instruments and the logical limit of inferring consent from compliance.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The same source reports incomplete enforcement and implementation.

**Inference limit:** Legal exposure is not evidence of actual penalties or every household's inability to evade.

## MED-K06

Lister’s 1867 address presented an evolving wound-treatment practice through cases and claimed ward improvements, rather than a controlled trial of a fixed protocol.

**Cases:** MED-C03. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S03: On the Antiseptic Principle in the Practice of Surgery (1867; 2010 reprint)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2895849/), Opening clinical discussion; paragraph beginning If the severest forms; concluding ward discussion. Supports: Procedural adaptations, case reports and asserted outcomes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Characterizes the evidence form and treatment specification in the historical document.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Observations can be informative despite lacking controlled identification.

**Inference limit:** Does not deny antisepsis efficacy or validate every claim in Lister's address.

## MED-K07

Contemporary objections to Lister’s aggregate outcome comparisons included small samples, case comparability and hospital epidemic cycles, making simple before/after attribution contestable.

**Cases:** MED-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S05: Statistics and the British controversy about the effects of Joseph Lister’s system of antisepsis for surgery, 1867–1890](https://www.jameslindlibrary.org/articles/statistics-and-the-british-controversy-about-the-effects-of-joseph-listers-system-of-antisepsis-for-surgery-1867-1890/), Statistics; Glasgow Royal Infirmary; Longer-term perspective. Supports: Examines selection periods, small numbers and changing background mortality.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Chosen comparison periods -> apparent mortality reduction -> efficacy inference
- **strongest rival:** Epidemic cycles, selection and case mix can produce a similar comparison
- **discriminating observation:** Longer ward series, comparable operations and independent comparison wards
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S05 reports wide prior fluctuation and very small operation counts, weakening the simple inference
- **remaining ambiguity:** Does not separate all genuine treatment effects from confounding; critiques are not proof of no benefit.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Improvement in fracture management and other outcomes may still support antiseptic benefits.

**Inference limit:** Hamilton reconstruction is mediated through Tröhler; no independent raw-data reanalysis.

## MED-K08

Demonstration, detailed instruction and embodied operative skills contributed to communicating Listerian practice; adoption was not reducible to assent to germ theory.

**Cases:** MED-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S04: Joseph Lister and the performance of antiseptic surgery](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3744349/), Antiseptic performance; Professional performance. Supports: Traces demonstrations, hands-on details, students and adaptation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** New multi-step regimen -> demonstration and practice -> ability to reproduce treatment
- **strongest rival:** Prestige and self-selection of adherents may explain following the same teachers
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable surgeons with and without direct training, measured performance rather than declared allegiance
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S04 documents channels and adaptations but does not supply such matched outcomes
- **remaining ambiguity:** Contribution is plausible and documented; necessity or magnitude not identified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Reputation, materials and institutional differences could explain the same diffusion pattern.

**Inference limit:** No controlled comparison isolates training from selection of motivated trainees.

## MED-K09

Alsan and Goldin estimate that combined safe water and effective sewerage account for about one-third of the decline in log under-five mortality in their jointly treated Massachusetts municipalities, 1880–1920; the interventions are complementary in their preferred model.

**Cases:** MED-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MED-S06: Watersheds in Child Mortality: The Role of Effective Water and Sewerage Infrastructure, 1880–1920](https://bpb-us-e1.wpmucdn.com/sites.harvard.edu/dist/e/935/files/2025/11/alsangoldin_jpe.pdf), pp.589–590,597,604–607; Table2 column5 (rendered table independently inspected during R1 repair). Supports: Staggered completion design and reported decomposition of 26.6 out of 79.2 log points.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Combined exposure-route interventions -> reduced pathogen exposure -> lower child mortality
- **strongest rival:** Other changes or selective migration timed with construction could mimic the effect
- **discriminating observation:** Separate water/sewerage terms and their interaction on log under-five mortality, with pre-trends, age/cause contrasts and composition checks; this is a health-production contrast, not an adoption decision estimate.
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S06 reports pre-trend checks, age/cause contrasts, Table 4 composition checks and robustness
- **remaining ambiguity:** Observational identification remains conditional; engineering rollout not random assignment.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Other sanitary improvements, spillovers and population selection could threaten the design.

**Inference limit:** Local estimate under model assumptions; neither a universal effect nor the national mortality contribution.

## MED-K10

Regional compulsory participation and payment formed part of the Massachusetts sewerage response to pollution crossing municipal boundaries.

**Cases:** MED-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S06: Watersheds in Child Mortality: The Role of Effective Water and Sewerage Infrastructure, 1880–1920](https://bpb-us-e1.wpmucdn.com/sites.harvard.edu/dist/e/935/files/2025/11/alsangoldin_jpe.pdf), p. 589 and §II.B pp. 592–593. Supports: Upstream/downstream problem and legally coordinated regional system.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Cross-boundary sewage costs -> regional obligations -> coordinated investment
- **strongest rival:** Political influence or voluntary coordination could deliver similar investment
- **discriminating observation:** Evidence of failed voluntary arrangements and subsequent contribution changes under mandate
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S06 documents boundary problem and mandate; not a counterfactual comparison of governance regimes
- **remaining ambiguity:** Observed institutional route does not prove necessity.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Voluntary bargains or private coordination might have supplied a viable alternative.

**Inference limit:** No claim that compulsion was uniquely necessary, consensual or costlessly beneficial.

## MED-K11

The size of clean-water contributions to aggregate US mortality decline remains disputed across analyses using overlapping records and different coding choices.

**Cases:** MED-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S07: Re-Examining the Contribution of Public Health Efforts to the Decline in Urban Mortality (July 2019 manuscript)](https://www.aeaweb.org/conference/2020/preliminary/paper/s855Kd6r), §3 pp. 8–9; §6 pp. 17–18; conclusion. Supports: Smaller estimates and sensitivity to transcription, dates and denominators.
- [MED-S08: Reexamining the Contribution of Public Health Efforts to the Decline in Urban Mortality: Comment](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/app.20190711), Abstract. Supports: Defends substantial total effect while acknowledging reduced infant estimate and coding differences.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Record coding and intervention timing -> different panel estimates -> different historical attribution
- **strongest rival:** Real treatment heterogeneity across cities could also explain differences
- **discriminating observation:** Reestimate with harmonized raw counts, population and partial-year rules
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S07 reports within-data corrections; MED-S08 attributes remaining difference to coding and denominators
- **remaining ambiguity:** No independent replication here; shared data means source count is not independent corroboration.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The disagreement does not erase agreement that filtration reduced typhoid mortality.

**Inference limit:** Final-paper data and reply not audited; MED-S08 abstract cannot adjudicate calculations.

## MED-K12

Wartime penicillin supply required solving culture-yield and downstream-processing problems in addition to establishing clinical promise.

**Cases:** MED-C05. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S09: Penicillin Production through Deep-tank Fermentation](https://www.acs.org/education/whatischemistry/landmarks/penicillin.html), Penicillin Production by Deep-tank Fermentation. Supports: Scale-up, extraction, purification and sterile production.
- [MED-S10: The Rescue of Penicillin](https://www.ars.usda.gov/oc/timeline/penicillin), 1941–1942 collaboration paragraphs. Supports: Culture medium and strain work.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Low usable yield -> strain/media/process work -> more recoverable output
- **strongest rival:** Expansion of vessel numbers or different extraction routes might explain part of output growth
- **discriminating observation:** Measured yields and losses before/after controlled process changes
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S09/S10 describe process improvements and production transition; no primary batch logs inspected
- **remaining ambiguity:** Causal weights and alternative industrial routes remain unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Institutional accounts may overstate their own component's contribution.

**Inference limit:** Process-specific contribution, not proof that this exact route was the only possible route.

## MED-K13

Pfizer’s prior submerged-fermentation experience was an identified contributor to its penicillin production route.

**Cases:** MED-C05. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Prior fermentation know-how -> adaptation of tanks/sterility controls -> production scale-up
- **strongest rival:** Capital and purchasing incentives could account for success despite different prior experience
- **discriminating observation:** Compare firms with similar finance and different inherited fermentation capabilities
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S09 names a concrete technical transfer; no matched firm histories examined
- **remaining ambiguity:** Source may organize history retrospectively around eventual success.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Capital, procurement access and new staff might matter as much as inherited skills.

## MED-K14

Governmental coordination and firms’ investment choices accompanied penicillin scale-up, while committing plant capacity involved opportunity costs.

- **mechanism chain:** Wartime demand and public coordination -> commitments to risky capacity -> enlarged supply

## MED-K15

Sri Lankan records reported six indigenous and eleven imported malaria cases in 1963, followed by reduced control activity and a major late-1960s resurgence.

Supports: Counts and programme sequence.
- [MED-S12: Malaria resurgence: a systematic review and assessment of its causes](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/1475-2875-11-122), Weakening of the malaria control programme, Sri Lanka paragraph. Supports: Same sequence with surveillance and population-movement qualifications.

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Records temporal pattern and reported classifications without allocating causal shares.

## MED-K16

The inspected chronology does not justify attributing Sri Lanka’s initial late-1960s reversal solely to DDT resistance: relaxation began earlier, while the account reports resistance in 1969, which is not a demonstrated date of biological onset. The account reports replacement of DDT by malathion in 1977.

**Cases:** MED-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MED-S11: Analysis of Historical Trends and Recent Elimination of Malaria from Sri Lanka and Its Applicability for Malaria Control in Other Countries](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/public-health/articles/10.3389/fpubh.2017.00212/full), Second Half of the Twentieth Century. Supports: 1964 consolidation, later resurgence, 1969 resistance and 1977 replacement.
- [MED-S12: Malaria resurgence: a systematic review and assessment of its causes](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/1475-2875-11-122), Sri Lanka example and Technical problems. Supports: Multiple operational and ecological causes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Hypothesized: operational relaxation contributes to renewed transmission; undetected resistance and ecological change remain possible interacting causes.
- **strongest rival:** Undetected earlier resistance could also contribute
- **discriminating observation:** Contemporaneous susceptibility assays and local coverage preceding resurgence
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S11 supplies reported dates and MED-S12 a multi-factor account. Detection dates do not establish biological onset, so these records do not isolate a sole cause.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Detection lag and intertwined ecological changes remain unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Resistance may have been present before detection and can interact with weakened operations.

**Inference limit:** The inspected chronology leaves a sole-cause resistance account unestablished; it does not exclude earlier undetected resistance or establish a unique alternative cause.

The 1971 refugee-camp implementation led by Mahalanabis demonstrated family-administered oral rehydration in an emergency, extending evidence beyond tightly staffed research settings.

BRAC’s 1980s programme used household demonstration, monitoring and wider community engagement; its 1993 evaluation sampled 90 villages from three programme phases. Teaching coexisted with expanding packaged-ORS distribution.

## MED-K21

Chowdhury et al. Table 2 reports 51.8%, 54.5% and 53.0% ORT use for all reported diarrhoeal episodes across cohorts; the abstract’s 60% conflicts with that denominator, while the discussion obtains over 60% after excluding dysentery.

**Cases:** MED-C07. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MED-S14: The status of ORT in Bangladesh: how widely is it used?](https://doi.org/10.1093/heapol/12.1.58), p. 58 abstract; p. 62 Table 2 footnote; pp. 64–65 Discussion. Supports: Direct denominator comparison; broad ORT definition includes home fluids.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Source-internal measurement audit, verified against table and discussion rather than abstract alone.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The abstract could reflect an unstated restriction rather than a transcription error.

**Inference limit:** Not strict packaged-ORS coverage or population-weighted national rate; no new pooled estimate calculated.

## MED-K22

In experiment 1 of the 2011–2012 one-city Chinese audit, expected purchase outside the hospital reduced antibiotic prescribing from 55% to 10% without a request; with a request, the corresponding rates were 85% and 14%.

**Cases:** MED-C08. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MED-S15: Addressing Antibiotic Abuse in China: An Experimental Audit Study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4776334/), §I results; §III experiment 1 protocol. Supports: Randomly assigned standardized-patient roles and reported encounter rates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Changed expected dispensing location -> changed provider incentives/signals -> prescribing response
- **strongest rival:** Outside-purchase script may signal offense, distrust or counterfeit risk
- **discriminating observation:** Demand held explicit while purchase location changes; further scripts varying alternative signals
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S15 paired physician design and second-experiment scripts inspected; demand effect largely disappears with outside purchase
- **remaining ambiguity:** Mechanism decomposition less secure than treatment-script effect.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Scripts may signal factors besides lost revenue; auditors were not blinded.

**Inference limit:** Identifies response to scripts in this sample, not national policy effects or patient health effects.

## MED-K23

The audit’s additional scripts support financial incentives as a major explanation for prescribing in its sampled encounters, while leaving general-equilibrium reform effects unresolved.

**Cases:** MED-C08. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MED-S15: Addressing Antibiotic Abuse in China: An Experimental Audit Study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4776334/), §III experiment 2 and stated results; limitations. Supports: Relative-pharmacy, knowledge and gift treatments test rival interpretations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Revenue expectation -> unnecessary prescription incentive -> observed excess use
- **strongest rival:** Etiquette, perceived demand and drug-quality concern predict some similar responses
- **discriminating observation:** Changing those signals while maintaining outside-purchase condition
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S15 reports persistence under relative-pharmacy script and smaller knowledge effects; original experiment examined
- **remaining ambiguity:** Alternative signals remain bundled and actual reforms change hospital budgets.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Social signaling cannot be removed perfectly; providers may substitute other services after reform.

**Inference limit:** Not an estimate of nationwide incentive removal or a complete explanation of prescribing everywhere.

## MED-K24

This prescribing experiment illustrates a possible divergence between provider revenue and appropriate use, but does not quantify resulting antimicrobial resistance or total social harm.

**Cases:** MED-C08. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MED-S15: Addressing Antibiotic Abuse in China: An Experimental Audit Study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4776334/), §I motivation; §§II–III methods. Supports: Revenue mechanism, concern about resistance and measured prescribing outcome.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Revenue incentive -> unnecessary antibiotic exposure if prescriptions used -> possible wider resistance cost
- **strongest rival:** Prescriptions may not be used or marginal ecological effects may be small in a given setting
- **discriminating observation:** Link actual dispensed use to resistance outcomes with appropriate controls
- **evidence sought found:** MED-S15 establishes encounter prescribing responses but collected no resistance outcomes
- **remaining ambiguity:** Private incentive divergence supported; ecological magnitude and welfare balance unmeasured.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Needed treatment can also be underprovided, and prescription does not prove ingestion.

**Inference limit:** Externality is a mechanism-level interpretation, not a measured outcome of these visits.

## MOB-K01

ORBIS models Roman connectivity as a seasonally variable combination of road, river and sea routes; its route outputs are modeled opportunities rather than observed adoption or actual journeys.

**Cases:** MOB-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S01: ORBIS: The Stanford Geospatial Network Model of the Roman World, version dated 2 May 2012](https://orbis.stanford.edu/orbis2012/ORBIS_v1paper_20120501.pdf), pp. 7–11 and 19. Supports: Authors define average structural simulations, seasonal constraints and non-contemporaneous road coverage.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Local knowledge, secondary roads and route-specific hazards can change actual decisions.

**Inference limit:** A network model cannot establish traffic shares, willingness to adopt, welfare or the causal contribution of Roman state road building.

## MOB-K02

For pottery movement in lowland Britain c. 250–400 CE, Wiseman and colleagues infer best-fit road costs roughly three times river and four times sea costs, with wide uncertainty and regional river variation.

**Cases:** MOB-C01. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MOB-S02: The costs of transporting goods by different modes: A case study of pottery movement in late Roman Britain](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/7291f9ad-f581-44cb-9e21-a63889ffd40b/content), abstract; Table 3 p. 8; §5 p. 10. Supports: Estimated ratios are derived from excavated pottery distributions and a transport network.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Transport/transfer costs vary by mode -> buyers face different delivered costs -> pottery demand declines with generalized distance -> observed distribution fits moderate modal ratios.
- **strongest rival:** Institutional supply, product preference or distribution hubs generate the same spatial decay without the assumed cost-demand relationship.
- **discriminating observation:** Independent consignment/freight accounts or repeated fits across commodities and regions with different social distributions would separate general costs from pottery-specific institutions.
- **evidence sought found:** Read MOB-S02 §1.1, Table 3 and §§4.4–5: fits, river variation and rural/military exclusions support a bounded inference, not direct traffic measurement.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Cost is inferred from the outcome to be explained; network and demand assumptions leave multiple explanations possible.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Text-based Mediterranean estimates can imply much greater water advantages; the authors argue these often compare unlike segments and omit handling costs.

**Inference limit:** Rural pottery distribution, not all goods or military movement; the cost-demand model, sample and inferred routes matter. Abstract and conclusion summarize river ranges differently; no precise interval is generalized here.

## MOB-K03

Segment-level freight prices can misstate door-to-door modal advantages when transshipment, storage, port charges and access legs are omitted.

**Cases:** MOB-C01. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S02: The costs of transporting goods by different modes: A case study of pottery movement in late Roman Britain](https://api.repository.cam.ac.uk/server/api/core/bitstreams/7291f9ad-f581-44cb-9e21-a63889ffd40b/content), §1.1 pp. 1–2 and §4.4 pp. 8–9. Supports: Explicit chain of transport costs and critique of comparisons omitting transfers.
- [MOB-S01: ORBIS: The Stanford Geospatial Network Model of the Roman World, version dated 2 May 2012](https://orbis.stanford.edu/orbis2012/ORBIS_v1paper_20120501.pdf), p. 7 and p. 17. Supports: ORBIS warns expense data are schematic and uses a price-edict sailing-time calibration.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Additional transfers/access legs incur resources -> total delivery cost differs from line-haul price -> ranking inferred from a segment may change.
- **strongest rival:** Sea advantages remain dominant because transfer costs are negligible relative to long line-haul savings.
- **discriminating observation:** Observe or reconstruct full chains at different distances; whether adding transfers changes relative delivered cost distinguishes claims for a particular route.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S02 explicitly enumerates omitted costs and derives different end-to-end ratios; MOB-S01 acknowledges schematic price calibration.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The existence of omitted terms is established, but no universal ranking reversal is demonstrated.
- **hypothesis timing:** retrospective synthesis

**Counterevidence/rival:** Long bulk sea voyages can retain a large advantage despite transfer costs.

**Inference limit:** This is a measurement/mechanism proposition; it does not imply Roman land transport was universally cheaper or universally preferred.

## MOB-K04

Inka expansion incorporated earlier Andean routes and added or maintained roads, stores and wayside facilities, so imperial scaling was partly the integration of inherited infrastructure.

**Cases:** MOB-C02. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S03: Qhapaq Ñan, Andean Road System (No. 1459): ICOMOS advisory body evaluation](https://whc.unesco.org/document/152728), printed pp. 321 and 324–325, Description and History. Supports: Lists road engineering and supply structures and states that much of the network preceded Inka occupation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The evaluation also describes new secondary roads; inheritance does not mean no technical innovation or expansion.

**Inference limit:** Heritage synthesis, not a dated census of every route. No single inventor or uniform date of network completion is assigned.

## MOB-K05

For Garrido’s Cachiyuyo de Llampos road segment in the Atacama, water and forage scarcity make predominantly pedestrian traffic more plausible than regular llama caravans.

**Cases:** MOB-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** hypothesis.

- [MOB-S04: Rethinking imperial infrastructure: A bottom-up perspective on the Inca Road](https://www.academia.edu/26536064/Rethinking_Imperial_Infrastructure_A_bottom_up_perspective_on_the_Inca_Road), §4.1, pp. 98–99. Supports: Survey geography, scarce water, absent vegetation and alternative highland pasture routes motivate the author’s inference.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Sparse water/forage -> higher logistical burden for llama caravans -> travelers favor pedestrian traffic on this segment.
- **strongest rival:** Organized supply or small occasional caravans allowed substantial pack-animal traffic despite local scarcity.
- **discriminating observation:** Road-associated corrals, dung, feed provision or transport-specific evidence would discriminate better than generic camelid food bones.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S04 §4.1 reports ecology, limited water and absent ethnographic caravan precedent for this locality; no transport census was found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Ecological feasibility and alternative-route reasoning do not establish actual traffic shares; preservation/recognition of animal evidence must be tested.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Llama transport occurred elsewhere in the Andes; occasional camelid use on this segment is not excluded.

**Inference limit:** Traffic composition is inferred, not counted. This supports ecological task differentiation rather than a claim that Inka roads generally lacked pack-animal traffic.

## MOB-K06

At Cachiyuyo de Llampos, settlement, craft and roadside assemblages support Garrido’s interpretation that some local miners used the Inka Road for exchange beyond directly administered state production.

**Cases:** MOB-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S04: Rethinking imperial infrastructure: A bottom-up perspective on the Inca Road](https://www.academia.edu/26536064/Rethinking_Imperial_Infrastructure_A_bottom_up_perspective_on_the_Inca_Road), §§4–6, especially pp. 100–106. Supports: Mining camps concentrated nearer the road; non-Inka roadside craft activity and contrast with official Tambo Medanoso inform the interpretation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Imperial road creates traffic/supply opportunities -> local miners concentrate nearer it and build craft/exchange spaces -> production connects to wider circulation.
- **strongest rival:** State directly organized miners or supervised exchange informally while leaving little formal architecture.
- **discriminating observation:** Uniform official storage/consumption and elite production supervision at mining camps would favor direct control; contrasting domestic organization and nonofficial roadside craft activity favor autonomy.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S04 §§4–6 compares camp layout, ceramic/food assemblages and craft traces with Tambo Medanoso; found meaningful contrasts and roadside non-Inka craft sites.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Indirect taxation, permissions or local intermediaries can coexist with the observed household organization.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Substantial state-controlled mining and corvée systems existed in other locations discussed by the same author.

**Inference limit:** Absence of elite architecture is not proof of wholly autonomous exchange; site chronology, informal supervision and local intermediaries remain alternatives.

## MOB-K07

Early private Indian railway expansion combined British capital with colonial guarantees, while government line-placement priorities included military control; observed construction was not simply a market vote by passengers or farmers.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S06: Regulation, Ownership and Costs: A Historical Perspective from Indian Railways, April 2011 version](https://idei.fr/sites/default/files/medias/doc/conf/icied/papers_2011/bogartbis.pdf), §2.1, pp. 6–7. Supports: Concession contracts and 5% capital guarantee at a fixed exchange rate; payments when returns fell short.
- [MOB-S05: Railroads of the Raj: Estimating the Impact of Transportation Infrastructure](https://dave-donaldson.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/Donaldson_RRRaj_AER.pdf), §I.C, pp. 905–907. Supports: Government line placement and military priorities using historical planning documents.
- [MOB-S07: Extractive institutions? Investor returns to Indian railway companies in the age of high imperialism, April 2019 accepted manuscript](https://sites.socsci.uci.edu/~dbogart/extractive_copyacceptedJIE-March2019.pdf), p. 2. Supports: Guarantee shortfalls were funded from Government of India tax revenues.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Risky capital project and colonial strategic priorities -> guaranteed concessions and state routing -> financed trunk-line deployment.
- **strongest rival:** Comparable rail expansion would have occurred without guarantees because expected commercial demand alone attracted capital.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable unguaranteed proposals, financing failures and decision correspondence predating construction would help separate risk support from redundant subsidy.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S06 describes contract terms and contemporaries disputing necessity; MOB-S05 reconstructs military priorities; MOB-S07 confirms taxpayer-funded shortfalls.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Documents establish instruments and objectives, not the counterfactual size/timing of a fully unguaranteed network.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Trade and humanitarian motives also mattered; private capital and local demand were consequential.

**Inference limit:** Historians’ reconstructions of planning and contracts, not independent inspection here of every concession or Dalhousie minute. No claim that all lines had identical motives.

## MOB-K08

Donaldson’s published study finds lower trade costs and higher trade, and estimates a 0.164 log-point rise in real agricultural income per unit area following rail access in its district panel; the paper summarizes the income effect as about 16%.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MOB-S05: Railroads of the Raj: Estimating the Impact of Transportation Infrastructure](https://dave-donaldson.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/Donaldson_RRRaj_AER.pdf), §§III–V; §V.B–D pp. 923–926 and Table 4. Supports: Price/trade analysis and within-district panel of 192 districts, 1870–1930; unbuilt-line placebo checks address selection.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Rail access reduces trade costs -> price gaps narrow and trade expands -> districts specialize/access cheaper goods -> measured real agricultural income rises.
- **strongest rival:** Railroads were built where income/trade would already have grown, or unrelated investment caused both access and growth.
- **discriminating observation:** If unbuilt but seriously planned lines predict similar income growth, route-selection bias would undermine the rail mechanism; commodity-price and network-cost responses provide additional pathway evidence.
- **evidence sought found:** Read MOB-S05 §§III–V and Table 4: salt-price/network and trade analyses plus over 40,000 km of planned-unbuilt routes show no analogous placebo effects in the reported tests.
- **remaining ambiguity:** No randomized assignment; plans need not have identical latent trends, and distributional/nonagricultural effects are outside the main outcome.
