- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Placement was nonrandom; model assumptions and residual unobserved local trends can affect causal interpretation.

**Inference limit:** Agricultural output valued at local prices and deflated, divided by land area: not household disposable income or a measure of distribution. 0.164 log points is the reported coefficient, not an exact 16% transformation.

## MOB-K09

Bogart and Chaudhary find lower working expenses after colonial state takeovers of Indian railways, with employment reductions supporting a labour-cost channel; they do not directly observe railway wages.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MOB-S06: Regulation, Ownership and Costs: A Historical Perspective from Indian Railways, April 2011 version](https://idei.fr/sites/default/files/medias/doc/conf/icied/papers_2011/bogartbis.pdf), §§4–6, especially pp. 22–25 and Tables 7–9. Supports: Predetermined contractual purchase dates and within-railway estimates; employment and expense-category evidence; direct wage-data limitation.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Contractual state purchase strengthens treasury incentive -> labour/input reorganization -> lower employment/expenses.
- **strongest rival:** Different reporting, anticipated retrenchment or deteriorating service generates an apparent cost reduction.
- **discriminating observation:** Expense falls after rather than before scheduled purchases, across consistently reported categories, with employment changes and no measured accident deterioration.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S06 checks timing/accounting and selected quality measures; Tables 7–9 show expense and employment patterns; authors explicitly lack wage data.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Unmeasured service quality and other organizational changes may matter; worker welfare cannot be inferred from firm costs.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Their measured safety/quality checks do not cover all service dimensions; institutional differences limit transfer to other nationalizations.

**Inference limit:** April 2011 version used; no mixed-version numerical effect. Fiscal savings cannot be equated with workers’ welfare, and reduced wage rates are not established.

## MOB-K10

The April 2019 accepted manuscript by Bogart and Chaudhary estimates average annual total returns of 3.7% on Indian guaranteed railway securities for 1880–1908 and finds no average abnormal returns in the three years before state purchases.

**Cases:** MOB-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S07: Extractive institutions? Investor returns to Indian railway companies in the age of high imperialism, April 2019 accepted manuscript](https://sites.socsci.uci.edu/~dbogart/extractive_copyacceptedJIE-March2019.pdf), p. 3; Table 2a and takeover event-study discussion. Supports: Clearly defines guaranteed securities, period, return comparison and event-study result.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Guaranteed payments still exposed Indian tax revenues; ordinary returns at secondary-market prices can coexist with other transfers, coercion or opportunity costs.

**Inference limit:** This challenges a particular excess-investor-return mechanism, not the broader possibility of colonial extraction or unequal infrastructure benefits.

## MOB-K11

The Ghanaian western railway was routed toward gold mines and Kumasi for commercial and military purposes, while the eastern route had different political and commercial origins and reached Kumasi only in 1923 after wartime delays.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §2.1, printed pp. 5–6. Supports: Reconstructs western line Sekondi–Tarkwa–Obuasi–Kumasi and eastern Accra route, lobbying, governors and construction dates.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Alternative surveyed routes were judged attractive for agriculture; cocoa potential was already relevant to the eastern line.

**Inference limit:** Mainly the authors’ historical reconstruction; colonial political interests and technical transport benefits are not mutually exclusive.

## MOB-K12

Jedwab and Moradi find that railway access encouraged cocoa production and settlement growth in Ghana, using geographic controls, planned-but-unbuilt lines and instruments to address route selection.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §§3–5, printed pp. 9–20. Supports: Spatial data and identification exercises compare rail areas with controls and placebo routes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Rail access lowers inland shipment costs -> farmers expand cocoa -> rural production supports trading towns.
- **strongest rival:** Planners selected inherently promising cocoa areas and pre-existing settlement growth rather than causing expansion.
- **discriminating observation:** Effects absent near comparable planned-but-unbuilt lines, stronger with actual access, and robust to alternative alignments would count against simple selection.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S08 §4 and Table 3 compare planned routes, geographic controls and straight-line instruments; paper reports rail effects and weak placebo effects for Ghana.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Instrument exclusion is substantive; political endpoint choice and cocoa suitability can still correlate with unobserved potential.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** The eastern line could partly respond to cocoa prospects; instruments rely on exclusion assumptions and local growth can include relocation.

**Inference limit:** This is the January 2015 forthcoming version. Effects on cells and towns do not by themselves identify household gains or a full national cost-benefit balance.

## MOB-K13

Ghanaian railway traffic declined sharply after 1974, yet towns near former railway routes retained relative economic importance through the study’s 2000 endpoint as roads expanded.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §2.2 pp. 7–8 and §6 pp. 20–26. Supports: Rail decline, near-route road replacement, and persistence results; authors discuss sunk investments and coordination.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Early rail connection concentrates settlement/investment -> subsequent actors coordinate around established towns -> relative concentration persists after rail traffic declines.
- **strongest rival:** Persistent geography, road replacement or unmeasured durable amenities explain persistence without expectation-based coordination.
- **discriminating observation:** Persistence after equalized access and controls for inherited assets supports coordination; direct evidence of investors following prior settlement rather than amenities would be stronger.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S08 §6 controls for roads and historical facilities and discusses population persistence. Authors explicitly note possible mismeasurement of sunk assets and instrument caveats.
- **remaining ambiguity:** The fall of rail does not remove roads or all place advantages; evidence supports persistence more strongly than a uniquely identified coordination mechanism.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Roads preserved absolute transport access; persistence does not show towns could flourish with no replacement transport.

**Inference limit:** Not a claim about current Ghanaian railways. Sunk capital, continuing amenities and expectation-based coordination are difficult to fully disentangle.

## MOB-K14

The same Ghana study describes a feedback between poor service, lower freight revenue and deferred maintenance, alongside cocoa cultivation’s movement into new forest areas, as context for rail decline.

**Cases:** MOB-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S08: The Permanent Effects of Transportation Revolutions in Poor Countries: Evidence from Africa, January 2015 forthcoming version](https://alexandermoradi.org/research/rail.pdf), §2.2, printed pp. 7–8. Supports: Historical description links deficits/service/maintenance and changing cocoa geography; forests converted to cocoa and later food crops.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Service/financial deterioration and shifting crop geography -> lower rail demand/revenue -> deferred repair -> further deterioration.
- **strongest rival:** Road competition or political mismanagement alone caused decline, with maintenance and crop shifts mainly consequences.
- **discriminating observation:** Dated line-level service, finance, repair and commodity-flow series could establish ordering and distinguish shocks.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S08 §2.2 provides historical sequence and mechanisms, including forest-to-cocoa shifts, but no separate causal decomposition was inspected.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Feedback makes direction reciprocal; environmental net costs are not identified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Political instability, roads, changing labour costs and management also mattered.

**Inference limit:** The paper does not separately identify the causal contribution of each decline mechanism or quantify net deforestation, deaths, dispossession or environmental welfare.

## MOB-K15

The 1897 New York electric-cab venture deliberately sold transport service while retaining vehicles and specialist control, rather than merely selling cars to inexperienced owners; operation was demonstrated before profitability was established.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S09: The Electric Car and the Burden of History: Studies in Automotive Systems Rivalry in America, 1890–1996](https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v026n2/p0304-p0310.pdf), pp. 306–307. Supports: Morris and Salom’s service model, operating experience and initial-cost assessment.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The first six months were probably loss-making on Kirsch’s reconstruction, and reported operation is not proof of scalable profitability.

**Inference limit:** Operational results were originally self-reported and the profitability inference uses later cost studies; this contribution does not reproduce the implied cost calculation.

## MOB-K16

Kirsch’s history attributes the EVC’s breakdown to a bundle of production, financial and organizational problems, and distinguishes early operating-company failures from the parent’s default in December 1907.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S09: The Electric Car and the Burden of History: Studies in Automotive Systems Rivalry in America, 1890–1996](https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v026n2/p0304-p0310.pdf), p. 307. Supports: Production delays, fires, shareholder suits, public attacks, regional failures and later parent default.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Ambitious system expansion encounters operational/financial problems -> operating firms fail -> organizational collapse damages the service model.
- **strongest rival:** Battery shortcomings alone made profitable service impossible, so financial trouble was simply a downstream symptom.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable fleets with sound finance but similar batteries, plus separated operating/financing accounts, would test sufficiency of the battery explanation.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S09 p. 307 documents several failure modes; MOB-S10 shows later task-specific viable fleets, weakening a timeless impossibility claim but not isolating EVC causes.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Later fleets had improved batteries and different tasks; sources share some intellectual lineage; no clean comparison quantifies causal weights.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Battery limitations and unreliable vehicles remain part of the contemporary feasibility problem; business failure does not prove a viable alternative would otherwise have won.

**Inference limit:** Author dissertation summary; relative weights of factors are not independently identified. A successful EVC counterfactual is not an observed outcome.

## MOB-K17

Mom’s comparative history distinguishes early battery-related failures from later electric fleets that worked under specific duty cycles and disciplined maintenance; it rejects the electric starter as a sufficient explanation for all electric-vehicle displacement.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MOB-S10: Das Scheitern des frühen Elektromobils (1895–1925): Versuch einer Neubewertung](https://zeithistorische-forschungen.de/sites/default/files/medien/material/2017-3/Mom_1997.pdf), pp. 281–283, conclusions. Supports: Discusses sulphation/maintenance, commercial and specialized fleets, universal gasoline use and starter timing.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Duty cycle and disciplined maintenance mitigate battery weaknesses -> specified fleet work remains viable -> general private-car displacement coexists with electric niches.
- **strongest rival:** One universally inferior propulsion technology or the 1912 starter alone explains all electric decline.
- **discriminating observation:** Successful electric tasks after the starter and differing decline by application contradict a universal single-trigger prediction.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S10 pp. 281–283 identifies successful defined fleets and notes the Model T starter appeared in 1919; source explicitly disaggregates generations/tasks.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Fleet success does not establish equal suitability for touring or lower costs for every owner; the broad interpretation is historical, not an experiment.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The gasoline vehicle’s range, autonomy and purchase cost were real advantages for general private use; fleet successes were conditional.

**Inference limit:** US-focused case with European task contrasts. No all-vehicle market share or claim of equivalent generalized performance; interpretation translated from German.

## MOB-K18

The Electric Vehicle Association of America, established in 1909, promoted utility fleets, charging facilities, off-peak rates and a common charging plug; this was an organized attempt to build complements after early electric-car setbacks.

**Cases:** MOB-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S09: The Electric Car and the Burden of History: Studies in Automotive Systems Rivalry in America, 1890–1996](https://thebhc.org/sites/default/files/beh/BEHprint/v026n2/p0304-p0310.pdf), pp. 308–309. Supports: Formation, membership, marketing and standard-plug committee, then incorporation into NELA in 1916.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Central stations were not uniformly supportive, and coordination alone did not remove battery and vehicle costs.

**Inference limit:** Earlier coordination changing the dominant technology is Kirsch’s counterfactual hypothesis, not a causal result established here.

## MOB-K19

Postwar container scaling required compatible handling equipment and intermodal facilities; the published trade study distinguishes 1956 US commercial deployment from the start of its international diffusion period in 1966.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S11: Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade](https://qn1-next.xuetangonline.com/16800654811573.pdf), §§2.1–2.3, pp. 38–40. Supports: Specialized cranes, road/rail/ship integration, Ideal-X deployment and international standardization narrative.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Compatible boxes and equipment reduce repeated handling -> ship/rail/truck interfaces work together -> deployments can scale beyond one proprietary route.
- **strongest rival:** A better ship or cheaper box alone explains diffusion without complementary investment.
- **discriminating observation:** Early experiments that failed with weak handling complements, followed by adoption tied to cranes/terminals/standards, discriminate against artifact-only explanations.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S11 §§2.1–2.3 reports prewar experiments, specialized-capital constraints, early port reluctance and negotiated compatibility.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Narrative is inherited from earlier histories; it does not prove any one exact standard or institutional arrangement was necessary.
- **hypothesis timing:** pre-search candidate

**Counterevidence/rival:** Earlier containers and domestic experiments existed; some goods and routes remained outside containerization.

**Inference limit:** Dates describe this study’s modern container-system convention, not invention of boxes or the first transport container anywhere.

## MOB-K20

US construction subsidies tied to approved container dimensions, international standardization and military logistics demand in the Vietnam War helped create conditions for container diffusion; these were distinct from intrinsic box efficiency.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S11: Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade](https://qn1-next.xuetangonline.com/16800654811573.pdf), §2.3, p. 40. Supports: Federal Maritime Board policy, ISO compromises and military logistics described as complementary mechanisms.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Subsidy specifications/ISO coordination and military demand -> reduce compatibility/investment uncertainty and add traffic -> support diffusion.
- **strongest rival:** Commercial demand and private integrated shipping would have produced the same diffusion trajectory without these policies.
- **discriminating observation:** Route- and firm-level investment responses to policy changes, or comparable commercial routes without military demand, would separate contribution from coincidence.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S11 p. 40 documents the historical policy and procurement channels; no dedicated causal estimate of their incremental effect is provided.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Military demand, private innovation and standardization interacted; narrative timing cannot identify necessity.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The historical narrative also starts with private shipping investment; it does not identify military demand as necessary for every route.

**Inference limit:** Historical interpretation in an econometric paper, based on prior histories. No causal estimate of each policy or claim that war was necessary for global containerization.

## MOB-K21

Bernhofen and colleagues warn that very large estimates using adoption dates alone do not convincingly establish causal trade effects; their product-containerizability comparisons are smaller and provide stronger but still qualified support.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** contested.

- [MOB-S11: Estimating the effects of the container revolution on world trade](https://qn1-next.xuetangonline.com/16800654811573.pdf), p. 37; Table 2 pp. 43–44; §3.4 pp. 45–46. Supports: Pre-trend failures and endogeneity warning; benchmark 17.4% North–North product differential and approximately 14% world differential with delayed accrual.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Container systems lower relevant handling costs -> suitable products should gain more trade than unsuitable products after adoption.
- **strongest rival:** Countries adopt while trade is already growing, inflating aggregate adoption-timing effects.
- **discriminating observation:** Pre-adoption trends among future adopters and suitable/unsuitable products test timing confounding; comparative product responses add a discriminating dimension.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S11 p. 37 explicitly reports failed parallel-trend robustness for adoption-only models. Tables 2–3 and pp. 44–46 give smaller product differentials and classification sensitivity.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Suitability definitions and correlated product shocks remain; product effects are not equivalent to all general-equilibrium benefits.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Classification and robustness choices alter estimated magnitudes; product-level effects do not capture every network spillover.

**Inference limit:** Do not quote old working-paper 700% figures as established causal effects. Denominators are bilateral product trade comparisons over specified periods, not total global GDP or a universal return to a container port.

## MOB-K22

The 1960 West Coast mechanization agreement exchanged union acceptance of labour-saving changes for a $29 million fund over 1961–1966, and its financing imposed uneven charges contested by Volkswagen.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S12: Volkswagenwerk Aktiengesellschaft v. Federal Maritime Commission, 390 U.S. 261](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/390/261), opening paragraphs 2–4, 390 U.S. 261. Supports: Agreement, fund, and weight-versus-volume assessment dispute.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** A negotiated fund does not establish that every worker or shipper was compensated, nor that all members regarded the terms as fair.

**Inference limit:** Court record of specific contracts and litigated incidence; not an aggregate estimate of gains or proof that containers alone caused every negotiated change.

## MOB-K23

Containerization shifted much stuffing and unstuffing away from docks, creating conflict between longshore and Teamster jurisdictions after the initial West Coast mechanization bargain.

**Cases:** MOB-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [MOB-S13: California Cartage Co. v. NLRB, 822 F.2d 1203](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F2/822/1203/373792/), part I, paragraphs describing break-bulk, 1960 agreement and off-dock container work. Supports: Court recounts Board findings of greater-than-expected work loss and the dispute over a fifty-mile work-preservation agreement.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Sealed intermodal boxes bypass repeated dock handling -> stuffing/unstuffing moves inland -> occupational jurisdiction is contested.
- **strongest rival:** Work loss consists only of machine substitution, with no material relocation across employers or unions.
- **discriminating observation:** Observed off-dock stuffing by Teamsters and disputes over those tasks distinguish relocation from pure disappearance.
- **evidence sought found:** MOB-S13 part I recounts off-dock work and the fifty-mile supplement litigation, alongside new dockside equipment duties.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Court evidence cannot count net jobs or infer conditions at every freight station.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some longshore workers acquired new container-handling roles; moving tasks is not equivalent to eliminating all employment.

**Inference limit:** Judicial retrospective of a particular dispute, not a comprehensive labour census. Legal characterization of work restrictions is not adopted as a neutral welfare assessment.

## MOB-K24

Goh Chok Tong’s 2000 terminal-opening speech records Singapore’s 1972 container berths and presents further port expansion as a coordinated choice involving scarce waterfront land, long planning horizons, workforce upgrading and information services.

**Cases:** MOB-C07. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [MOB-S14: Speech by Prime Minister Goh Chok Tong at the opening of the Pasir Panjang Terminal, 30 March 2000](https://www.nas.gov.sg/archivesonline/data/pdfdoc/2000033003/gct20000330l.pdf), paragraphs 3–6, PDF pp. 1–2. Supports: 1972 chronology and official explanation of Pasir Panjang land, capacity and service choices.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded documentary/model/measurement statement; does not itself estimate a causal contribution. Associated causal propositions are tested separately.

**Counterevidence/rival:** The official account celebrates success and omits a full account of displaced uses, residents and environmental costs.

**Inference limit:** Strong evidence of publicly stated state rationale, weaker evidence of exclusive causes of success; no independent causal estimate and no use of the speech’s forecasts.

## NET-K01

Bloom's historical synthesis places papermaking in Baghdad by the late eighth century and its transmission to Mediterranean Christians by the twelfth; this is a broad chronology of manufacture, not a unique invention event.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S01: Papermaking: The Historical Diffusion of an Ancient Technique](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-319-44654-7_3), pp. 51–54, The Early Diffusion of Papermaking and The Replacement of Papyrus and Parchment. Supports: Manufacturing and transfer chronology; paper use predates European typography.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Regional practice and surviving evidence are uneven; exact first mills and routes are less secure than the broad sequence.

**Inference limit:** Does not establish a single battle-mediated transfer, rates of uptake, literacy effects, or indigenous manufacturing wherever paper was used.

## NET-K02

For the high-quality European book/writing-paper workflow reconstructed for 1300–1800, water quality, fibre preparation and gelatin sizing affected usable sheet properties; a generic recipe for pulp did not capture the complete craft.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S02: European Papermaking Techniques 1300–1800](https://paper.lib.uiowa.edu/european.php), Introduction; Raw Materials/Water; Sizing, paragraphs beginning As a result and Gelatin size allowed. Supports: Production constraints, sizing function, practical and historical evidential limits.
- [NET-S03: Material properties of Islamic paper](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/1527462/1/2016%20Mahgoub%20HS.pdf), pp. 2–3 and 9. Supports: Storage, provenance and material-classification limits prevent a simple cross-regional superiority inference.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Controlled inputs and finishing -> workable, ink-resistant sheets -> suitability for specific writing/printing tasks
- **strongest rival:** Observed surviving-paper quality could instead reflect storage and preservation selection; cheaper rough paper could satisfy other tasks.
- **discriminating observation:** Matched historical papers or workshop records separating manufacture from storage and end-use requirements.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S02 was read for process evidence; NET-S03 explicitly warns that unknown storage prevents general causal claims about stability.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Technical functionality is supported; contribution to historical diffusion and cross-regional cost differences remains unidentified.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Barrett explicitly calls aspects reconstruction/hypothesis; climatic storage and survival also affect present condition.

**Inference limit:** No quantified contribution to historical costs, output or regional adoption; high-quality European paper is not all paper.

## NET-K03

Bloom cites Italian watermarked paper used in a Tunisian royal letter in 1350 and a 1409 Ibn Marzuq ruling permitting writing on Christian-made paper with objectionable watermark imagery; imported paper could support local writing without local manufacture.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S01: Papermaking: The Historical Diffusion of an Ancient Technique](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-319-44654-7_3), pp. 63–64, Trade Relations and the Decline of Arab Papermaking. Supports: Specific imported-paper examples and the permissive ruling.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Imported sheets encounter religious objections -> jurist authorises their use -> a permissible route to continued local writing
- **strongest rival:** Material availability or price alone determines purchases; the ruling retrospectively validates an already established practice.
- **discriminating observation:** Purchasing changes before/after the ruling or correspondence showing objections prevented use until resolved.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S01 reports both actual imported-paper use and the later ruling; no before/after purchase series was found.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Chronological sequence demonstrates compatibility of religion and adoption, but not the ruling's marginal effect on demand.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The ruling records a normative solution, not the subsequent number of purchasers; imports and local production could coexist.

**Inference limit:** Underlying letter and fatwa not directly inspected. Does not prove imports were always cheaper or that ruling caused a measured increase.

## NET-K04

Mahgoub et al.'s 228-paper collection includes 133 undated samples and mostly eighteenth-/nineteenth-century dated examples; text language and finishing cannot by themselves establish paper's place of manufacture.

**Cases:** NET-C01. **Evidence:** experiment. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S03: Material properties of Islamic paper](https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/1527462/1/2016%20Mahgoub%20HS.pdf), pp. 2–3, discussion before Methods and Reference collection. Supports: Sample composition, uncertain provenance, trade and finishing complications.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Some watermarks, fibres and morphology can inform provenance when combined with other evidence.

**Inference limit:** The study supplies no medieval adoption curve and no representative comparison of Islamic and European production efficiency.

## NET-K05

The surviving 868 Diamond Sutra's translated colophon names Wang Jie and a distribution dedication for his parents; it documents sponsored woodblock production and declared purpose, not achieved circulation volume.

**Cases:** NET-C02. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S06: Buddhist texts: The Diamond Sutra](https://idp.bl.uk/discover/learning/buddhism-on-the-silk-roads/articles/buddhism-on-the-ground/buddhist-texts-the-diamond-sutra/), The Diamond Sutra; Colophon from the Diamond Sutra (object Or.8210/P.2). Supports: Date, printing method, sponsorship and declared distribution purpose.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** This is a surviving dated object rather than the earliest-ever experiment; no run count is preserved in the cited description.

**Inference limit:** Institutionally translated colophon, not independent original-language collation. Purpose does not demonstrate that universal distribution occurred.

## NET-K06

Shen Gua describes Bi Sheng's clay-type process in 1041–1048, including type levelling, multiple common characters and alternating boards; he reports greater convenience for many copies than a few.

**Cases:** NET-C02. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S04: A Selection of Shen Gua's Writings in Translation, appendix 3 to Shen Gua's Empiricism](https://asiacenter.harvard.edu/sites/default/files/2023-06/shengua_app3.pdf), appendix 3, pp. 22–24, movable-type passage and nn. 69–71. Supports: Operational description and the author's conditional efficiency assessment.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Clay composition and later interpretations are debated; the passage does not supply timed trials or a surviving print-run account.

**Inference limit:** High confidence is in what the text reports, not an independently verified cost advantage or mass adoption.

## NET-K07

McDermott and Burke identify spatially and temporally separable craft stages, reusable blocks and thin-paper use as advantages of Chinese woodblock production relative to European press-room organisation.

**Cases:** NET-C02. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S05: Introduction, The Book Worlds of East Asia and Europe, 1450–1850: Connections and Comparisons](https://hkupress.hku.hk/image/catalog/pdf-preview/9789888208081.pdf), pp. 11–14, Technology Transfer. Supports: Division of labour, later reprinting from blocks, paper/premises and financing comparisons.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Separable stages and retained blocks -> flexible labour scheduling and later reprints -> viable decentralised publishing
- **strongest rival:** Woodblock persistence mainly follows script size, restrictions or lack of capital, even when workflows are inefficient.
- **discriminating observation:** Same-script publishers switching methods with demand volatility, reprint interval or relative labour prices.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S05 documents workflow differences and movable-type niches, while NET-S04 already distinguishes print-run scales.
- **remaining ambiguity:** These observations weaken technological-incapacity stories but do not isolate cost from institutions or preferences.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Blocks required skilled cutting and storage; costs differed by quality, wage and place. No matched workshop price panel is supplied.

**Inference limit:** Conditional production affordances do not prove universal woodblock cheapness or economic optimality.

## NET-K08

Chinese movable-type use in particular genealogical and imperial projects coexisted with predominant woodblock printing in 1450–1850; a writing system alone is insufficient to specify which method was used for every task.

**Cases:** NET-C02. **Evidence:** synthesis-inference. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [NET-S05: Introduction, The Book Worlds of East Asia and Europe, 1450–1850: Connections and Comparisons](https://hkupress.hku.hk/image/catalog/pdf-preview/9789888208081.pdf), pp. 14–15, especially discussion and n. 29. Supports: Woodblock predominance alongside explicitly identified movable-type niches.
- [NET-S04: A Selection of Shen Gua's Writings in Translation, appendix 3 to Shen Gua's Empiricism](https://asiacenter.harvard.edu/sites/default/files/2023-06/shengua_app3.pdf), pp. 23–24 and n. 71. Supports: Early process testimony and later predominance distinction.
- [NET-S16: The Great Divergence and the Economics of Printing](https://eprints.gla.ac.uk/120520/13/120520.pdf), January 2016 manuscript pp.17–22. Supports: Cost-model compatibility with the same genealogical and imperial niches.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Different production tasks and input costs within a script -> differing method choices -> persistent woodblock/type coexistence
- **strongest rival:** Script-sensitive fixed and operating costs, combined with print runs and pages, predict the observed niches without an independently identified institutional effect.
- **discriminating observation:** Comparable projects with measured composition, storage and reprint costs, or a type-cost change within one market, could separate predictions. Niche existence alone cannot.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S05 describes niches; NET-S16 explicitly models them. Thus the alleged counterexample does not discriminate against this stronger rival.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Historical calibration, demand and project selection remain uncertain. Categorical impossibility is excluded; explanation of relative use is unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Angeles’s script-sensitive cost model predicts block predominance and type niches using pages, print runs and type material; fitting those inherited observations does not identify script’s causal weight.

**Inference limit:** Niches reject practical impossibility and a uniform all-tasks rule, not a script-sensitive cost explanation of limited relative uptake. Relative causal weights remain unestablished.

## NET-K09

The BnF identifies its surviving Jikji volume as metal-type printing from Heungdeok-sa in 1377 and records a complete woodblock version from 1378; the evidence shows adjacent uses, not replacement or mass readership.

**Cases:** NET-C03. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S07: Jikji, a treasure of the world of printing](https://www.bnf.fr/en/jikji-treasure-world-printing), What is Jikji?; A treasure of humanity; Preservation and access. Supports: Techniques, dates and partial survival of BnF volume.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Only part of the metal-type edition survives in this holding; run sizes and reader counts are unknown.

**Inference limit:** Do not equate oldest known surviving object with first invention, profitable reproduction or economy-wide diffusion.

## NET-K10

Park's synthesis places elite female/palace manuscript circulation of Korean vernacular novels in the late seventeenth century and commercial rental manuscripts from the mid-eighteenth, with continued coexistence of later woodblock versions.

**Cases:** NET-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S08: Manuscript, Not Print, in the Book World of Chosŏn Korea (1392–1910)](https://api.pageplace.de/preview/DT0400.9781000539578_A42450661/preview-9781000539578_A42450661.pdf), pp. 22–24, The Chosŏn Book World Beyond Print. Supports: Chronology, audiences, rental circulation and warning against a replacement sequence.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Vernacular genres and audiences differed; surviving evidence is not a population-wide readership sample.

**Inference limit:** Commercial circulation is not necessarily print. No inference of national literacy growth, reader numbers or universal female access.

## NET-K11

Late Chosŏn copying served access to imported books and literary collections, including selected or variant texts; manuscript publication cannot be treated solely as a preliminary stage awaiting print.

**Cases:** NET-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S08: Manuscript, Not Print, in the Book World of Chosŏn Korea (1392–1910)](https://api.pageplace.de/preview/DT0400.9781000539578_A42450661/preview-9781000539578_A42450661.pdf), pp. 25–28, imported Chinese books, literary collections and Toward More Descriptions. Supports: Copying practices, selective texts, publication without expected printing and variable reading contexts.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Demand for newly obtained or tailored texts -> scribal duplication/selection -> circulation without waiting for a print edition
- **strongest rival:** Copying persists only because otherwise identical printed books are expensive or unavailable.
- **discriminating observation:** Manuscripts that selectively rework printed sources, circulate for specific readers or are prepared without expectation of printing.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S08 pp. 25–28 documents such practices and contrasts them with print-centred generalisations.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Tailoring, affordability and shortage can operate together; relative importance remains unmeasured.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** High prices or limited printed supply may still explain particular copied texts; textual change need not be deliberate preference.

**Inference limit:** No aggregate cost advantage or mental preference inferred from manuscript survival alone.

## NET-K12

Park cites private publishing drawing on official editing experience or state-owned type, challenging a strict separation in which Chosŏn state printing only obstructed nonstate publication.

**Cases:** NET-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** contested.

- [NET-S08: Manuscript, Not Print, in the Book World of Chosŏn Korea (1392–1910)](https://api.pageplace.de/preview/DT0400.9781000539578_A42450661/preview-9781000539578_A42450661.pdf), p. 27 and n. 16; p. 28. Supports: Examples of Chang Hon's career and elite private use of official type; critique of state/private binary.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Official production develops skills/resources -> some actors reuse them privately -> state capacity sometimes complements private publication
- **strongest rival:** A comprehensive state monopoly prevents nonstate production using public skills or equipment.
- **discriminating observation:** Documented private deployment of official expertise or type contradicts the comprehensive form; denied applications would test limits.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S08 supplies counterexamples with references, but the underlying archives were not acquired.
- **remaining ambiguity:** State support and state exclusion can coexist; net effect and who could access resources remain uncertain.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** These examples do not show unrestricted access to equipment or negate censorship and unequal access.

**Inference limit:** Underlying studies Walraven (2007) and No (2013) not independently inspected; a few cases cannot measure net institutional effect.

## NET-K13

Dittmar identifies 205 adopting cities for 1450–1500; Table I compares them with 1,811 historical cities in its listed polities, selected from places reaching 5,000 residents sometime in 1000–1800.

**Cases:** NET-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S09: Information Technology and Economic Change: The Impact of the Printing Press](https://people.bu.edu/chamley/764-23/Dittmar.pdf), pp. 1143–44, Table I and nn. 20–23. Supports: Adoption-source compilation and denominator definition.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Counts depend on survival and catalogue geography; monasteries and neighbouring centres are handled separately.

**Inference limit:** Not all European settlements, not a contemporaneous urban census, and not literacy; regression sample sizes differ.

## NET-K14

For European presses in 1450–1500, Dittmar's history connects diffusion to mobile trained printers and entry to financing and sufficiently stable demand; the existence of large cities alone did not determine adoption.

**Cases:** NET-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S09: Information Technology and Economic Change: The Impact of the Printing Press](https://people.bu.edu/chamley/764-23/Dittmar.pdf), pp. 1153–56; n. 38 on casting uncertainty and n. 40 on equipment valuations. Supports: Apprenticeship transmission, financing, customer base and forty of one hundred largest cities without presses in 1500.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Trained operators and capital reach a place with customers -> workshop established -> local printing deployment
- **strongest rival:** Demand or city size alone attracts presses; apparent skill networks merely follow profitable locations.
- **discriminating observation:** Large nonadopting cities and adoption varying with technical-network proximity after demand controls; workshop correspondence would be stronger.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S09 records forty of the hundred largest cities without a press and discusses printer mobility, financial requirements and distance.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Demand, skills and funding are jointly selected; historical sequencing and IV assumptions do not fully separate them.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The history is partly inherited synthesis; mature casting details are uncertain and demand also shaped location.

**Inference limit:** No claim that every European press was unregulated, that all operators used identical equipment, or that finance alone explains every nonadopter.

## NET-K15

In Dittmar and Seabold's European printing-city analysis, local competition is associated with lower book prices and changes in publication output; transport costs make location consequential even after technical feasibility.

**Cases:** NET-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [NET-S10: New Media and Competition: Printing and Europe's Transformation after Gutenberg](https://cep.lse.ac.uk/pubs/download/dp1600.pdf), pp. 3, 6–7; §3.1 prices/edition measures; §5 competition analysis. Supports: Book-price evidence, printer entry/death strategy, transport and local market structure.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Entry or disrupted collusion -> increased local competition -> changed prices and content
- **strongest rival:** Growing demand drives entry and output, or printer death changes scarce expertise directly rather than competition.
- **discriminating observation:** Prices/output shifting with death-induced competitive changes rather than only growing markets; direct skill-channel tests.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S10 compares entries/exits and printer deaths, uses Colón purchase prices and finds lower prices with more local printers; §3 notes different death measures and selection.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Exclusion restriction is substantive; a death can alter more than market structure. This is evidence from the authors' design, not an independent replication.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Competition, customer demand and content can influence one another; death also disrupts expertise and relationships.

**Inference limit:** Effects conditional on identification assumptions. Prices are from a selected purchasing record; editions are not copies sold or readers.

## NET-K16

Studies of early European printing estimate city-growth and content effects using distance from Mainz and printer deaths as instruments; interpretation as causal depends on excluding other geography, expertise and network channels.

**Cases:** NET-C04. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** causal-identification.

- [NET-S09: Information Technology and Economic Change: The Impact of the Printing Press](https://people.bu.edu/chamley/764-23/Dittmar.pdf), §V.E, pp. 1157–63, IV design and comparison with OLS. Supports: Distance instrument and tests, outcome is city population growth.
- [NET-S10: New Media and Competition: Printing and Europe's Transformation after Gutenberg](https://cep.lse.ac.uk/pubs/download/dp1600.pdf), §§3 and 5–7; introduction pp. 3–4. Supports: Printer-death designs for competition/content and outcomes.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Local printing/content access -> useful skills or ideological transmission -> observed city or institutional outcome
- **strongest rival:** Underlying commercial growth or institutions cause both printing and outcomes; geography/deaths affect outcomes through other channels.
- **discriminating observation:** Adoption/output shocks unrelated to pre-trends, sector-specific effects, and falsification of direct channels.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S09 checks prior growth and uses Mainz distance; NET-S10 uses content-specific printer-death variation. Historical production and controls were inspected; estimates not re-estimated.
- **remaining ambiguity:** No independent replication; validity remains conditional and external validity does not extend automatically to other book cultures.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Pre-trend and control checks support the studies, but exclusion cannot be established by chronology alone; city growth can involve redistribution by migration.

**Inference limit:** Do not treat city population as national productivity, welfare or a universal print effect. Designs share scholarship and some data foundations.

## NET-K17

Schwartz finds no inspected original of the alleged Bayezid II/Selim I general printing bans, while the extant 1588 firman protects merchants selling printed Arabic books; a blanket ban is not established evidence.

**Cases:** NET-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** contested.

- [NET-S11: Did Ottoman Sultans Ban Print?](https://religionculturesociety.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/05/s1__schwartz_did-ottoman-sultans-ban-print.pdf), pp. 6–7, The Non-Extant Firmans and The First Extant Firman; pp. 19–26 on inherited accounts. Supports: Documentary contrast and lineage of repeated ban claims.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Proposed general ban -> suppression of printing/trade -> limited uptake
- **strongest rival:** Later writers conflated specific restrictions or preferences into a general ban; permission varied by actor and text.
- **discriminating observation:** An authentic general prohibitory decree or consistent enforcement records versus extant permissions predating 1727.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S11 traces repeated assertions without an original decree and closely reads the 1588 protective firman.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Archival loss remains possible; extant permission defeats a timeless blanket claim but not all narrower prohibitions.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Absence of these documents does not establish universal permission; narrower restrictions and enforcement could exist.

**Inference limit:** Claim concerns evidential support for alleged blanket bans, not proof that no Ottoman restrictions ever occurred.

## NET-K18

The 1727 grant to Müteferrika permitted particular printing subjects and required learned correctors through the associated fatwa; it both enabled a project and delimited its scope.

**Cases:** NET-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S11: Did Ottoman Sultans Ban Print?](https://religionculturesociety.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/05/s1__schwartz_did-ottoman-sultans-ban-print.pdf), pp. 9–11, Ahmed III firman and quoted fatwa. Supports: Named permission, subject exclusions and appointment of correctors.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Petition and expertise claim -> religious/legal approval with correction and subject conditions -> authorised press project
- **strongest rival:** Approval merely formalises an independently viable venture; exclusions reflect broader suppression rather than quality or requested scope.
- **discriminating observation:** Text of the grant/fatwa and actual correcting practices; counterfactual applications denied without conditions.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S11 pp. 9–11 reproduces the permitted subjects and correction condition, and notes the request's own exclusions.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Permit text supports formal enabling/limitation, but cannot identify motives, compliance or marginal commercial effect.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The documents do not state every motive for excluding religious subjects or prove a previous universal prohibition.

**Inference limit:** Regulatory design establishes permitted scope, not the effectiveness of correction or its impact on sales.

## NET-K19

Sabev reports 8,200 initial copies across thirteen editions with known runs, estimates three other runs, and records 2,981 remaining copies in Müteferrika's estate; stock subtraction is not a directly observed sales measure.

**Cases:** NET-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S12: Formation of Ottoman Print Culture (1726–1746): Some General Remarks](https://nec.ro/wp-content/uploads/2019/05/ORLIN_SABEV.pdf), pp. 299–303, especially pp. 302–303. Supports: Known/assumed runs, estate stock, gifts, different finishing and market exposure.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Large residual stock is consistent with slow sales; earlier titles had more years available and gifts removed copies without sales.

**Inference limit:** Author's edition grouping and reconstructed figures retained; original register not recounted. No exact profit, sell-through or readership rate.

## NET-K20

Sabev finds Müteferrika prints in sixteen of 335 selected Istanbul askeri probate inventories with more than three books, dated 1724–1748; some owners were religious functionaries.

**Cases:** NET-C05. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S12: Formation of Ottoman Print Culture (1726–1746): Some General Remarks](https://nec.ro/wp-content/uploads/2019/05/ORLIN_SABEV.pdf), pp. 305–307. Supports: Selection rules, 335 inventories, sixteen containing prints, religious-functionary owners and stated caveats.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Probates omit living ownership, gifts, donations and readers who borrowed; surviving registers and askeri status select the sample.

**Inference limit:** High confidence in author's stated result, not independent archive replication. Not a population adoption or literacy denominator.

## NET-K21

In the northern Indian Ocean routes described by fifteenth-/sixteenth-century navigation texts, knowledge of regional and transitional winds mattered within the broad monsoon pattern; season was part of route selection.

**Cases:** NET-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S13: Indian Ocean navigation in Islamic sources 850–1560 CE](https://www.academia.edu/111549511/Indian_Ocean_navigation_in_Islamic_sources_850_1560_CE), §2 pp. 2–3 and §3 pp. 4–5. Supports: Regional wind windows, departure times and route instructions.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Route-specific seasonal knowledge -> chosen departure/bearing -> use of favourable winds and avoidance of hazards
- **strongest rival:** The general monsoon cycle alone suffices; detailed pilot knowledge adds little.
- **discriminating observation:** Instructions distinguishing routes and transitional windows within the same broad season; voyage outcomes would test magnitude.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S13 identifies countervailing winds and an eastward Bay of Bengal window, and reports route-specific dates and hazards.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Detailed prescriptions show relevant distinctions but not actual compliance or a quantified safety benefit.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Different routes had different windows; general environment does not by itself determine mariners' choices or voyage outcomes.

**Inference limit:** No fleet-level timing distribution, failure rate or comparison of trained/untrained crews is available.

## NET-K22

Staples describes wooden star-altitude aids and magnetic compasses using stellar direction names in late medieval Indian Ocean navigation; Tolmacheva calls magnetic use rare, leaving carriage prevalence unmeasured.

**Cases:** NET-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [NET-S13: Indian Ocean navigation in Islamic sources 850–1560 CE](https://www.academia.edu/111549511/Indian_Ocean_navigation_in_Islamic_sources_850_1560_CE), §3 p. 4, fingers, wooden instruments and stellar rhumb names. Supports: Layering of standardising instruments and established direction conventions.
- [NET-S14: Reflections on the Knowledge of Shallow Waters](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/full/10.1086/713561), paragraph beginning Where the Chinese compass had 28 rhumbs. Supports: Stellar compass scheme and assertion of rare magnetic-compass use.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Familiar stellar conventions plus measuring aids -> integration with existing navigation skills -> hybrid operating system
- **strongest rival:** An imported magnetic device substitutes for stellar/local knowledge and becomes a universal prerequisite.
- **discriminating observation:** Documents retaining stellar nomenclature and methods alongside devices; actual equipment inventories for prevalence.
- **evidence sought found:** NET-S13 records integration of stellar names into magnetic directions and wooden measurement; NET-S14 reports rare compass carriage.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Textual prescription is not fleet practice; both scholars rely heavily on the same navigational corpus. Prevalence remains unresolved.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** The two sources share underlying texts and differ in emphasis on prevalence; a stellar compass is a directional scheme, not necessarily a magnetic device.

**Inference limit:** No claim that all ships used compasses, that wooden instruments had a quantified accuracy, or that later kamal descriptions reproduce all earlier instruments.

## NET-K23

Ibn Majid's surviving instructions include verse suitable for memorisation and lack associated maps, while Portuguese reports describe some Indian Ocean chart use; absence in one corpus does not establish universal chartlessness.

**Cases:** NET-C06. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S14: Reflections on the Knowledge of Shallow Waters](https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/full/10.1086/713561), paragraphs beginning It appears that early Portuguese sailors, Curiously, and Moreover. Supports: Contrasting chart evidence and verse sailing instructions.
- [NET-S13: Indian Ocean navigation in Islamic sources 850–1560 CE](https://www.academia.edu/111549511/Indian_Ocean_navigation_in_Islamic_sources_850_1560_CE), §3 p. 4; §4 pp. 5–7. Supports: Difference between Arabic literary references and Portuguese testimony; limitations of editions/translations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Charts may have existed but not survived; verse need not prove every navigator memorised it.

**Inference limit:** No chart adoption rate or causal effect of oral transmission; inherited corpus is not an exhaustive census of practice.

## NET-K24

The anonymous 1498 voyage narrative reports da Gama seizing a Malindi court servant while demanding pilots, then receiving a pilot and departing with him on 24 April; this documents local expertise and coercion in one crossing episode.

**Cases:** NET-C06. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [NET-S15: A Journal of the First Voyage of Vasco da Gama, 1497–1499](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/46440/old/46440-h/46440-h.htm), pp. 45–47, Malindi entry for 22 April and Across the Gulf entry for 24 April 1498. Supports: Hostage seizure, pilot provision and departure with that pilot.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded statement about records, material observations or reported counts; no independent causal effect asserted.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Narrator's religious identification is unreliable; this single episode does not show the pilot's full role or whether the coercion was necessary.

**Inference limit:** Do not identify the pilot as Ibn Majid, infer all local recruitment was coerced, or use one voyage as a safety/accuracy comparison.

## OCE-K01

Kyselka’s contemporaneous account records Hōkūleʻa’s 1980 Hawaiʻi–Tahiti return voyage under Nainoa Thompson’s non-instrument navigation, with planetarium training, Mau Piailug’s teaching and escort-based documentation explicitly present.

**Cases:** OCE-C01. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S01: Hokule‘a 1980 (reprinted in “A Journey by the Stars”)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GPO-CRECB-1980-pt20/pdf/GPO-CRECB-1980-pt20-1-3.pdf), p.25908, Hokule‘a 1980. Supports: Training, successful landfall and return, twice-daily position reports and instrument comparison.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Reports a specific documented voyage and its stated supports, not their separate causal effect.

**Counterevidence/rival:** This was a supported modern voyage, not a controlled recreation of every ancient condition.

**Inference limit:** Demonstrates this configuration’s operation; no proof of a particular ancient migration, population uptake or cargo economics.

## OCE-K02

The 1979–1980 apprenticeship and March 2007 Satawal initiation support an intergenerational, inter-island knowledge-transmission pathway in voyaging renewal; they do not establish that one navigator alone caused a Pacific-wide cultural revival.

**Cases:** OCE-C01. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [OCE-S01: Hokule‘a 1980 (reprinted in “A Journey by the Stars”)](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GPO-CRECB-1980-pt20/pdf/GPO-CRECB-1980-pt20-1-3.pdf), p.25908. Supports: Direct learning from Mau and planetarium practice.
- [OCE-S03: Hōkūleʻa Mo‘olelo: The Pwo Ceremony](https://worldwidevoyage.hokulea.com/moolelo-pwo-ceremony/), The Pwo Ceremony, 2007 initiation paragraphs. Supports: Eleven Satawal and five Hawaiʻi initiates and responsibility to perpetuate knowledge.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Experienced navigator agrees to teach -> repeated sea and classroom practice -> new navigators operate and receive recognition
- **strongest rival:** Museum science, organizational support and a wider cultural movement independently sustain revival; these may complement teaching
- **discriminating observation:** Training sequence and autonomous voyages by multiple pupils, with explicit instructional histories rather than just shared symbols
- **evidence sought found:** S01 traces instruction before the 1980 voyage; S03 identifies later pupils and ceremony
- **remaining ambiguity:** No comparison of equally supported trainees without this teaching; many inputs co-varied
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** PVS accounts also celebrate many canoe builders and participants; institution and eyewitness source share a network.

**Inference limit:** Formal initiation is neither a census of practicing navigators nor a standardized competence test.

## OCE-K03

PVS records a 1978 capsize and loss of Eddie Aikau, followed by renewed voyages including 1980; revival included severe operational failure, not an uninterrupted sequence of successful demonstrations.

**Cases:** OCE-C01. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S02: History: The Story of Hōkūle‘a](https://hokulea.com/history/), Tragedy and A Generation of Renewal sections. Supports: 1978 capsize, rescue/loss, resumption in 1980.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Bounded chronology of failure and resumption.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Retrospective narrative emphasizes perseverance.

**Inference limit:** Does not identify the capsize’s technical cause or quantify safety changes or a voyage failure rate.

## OCE-K04

Wilson-Hokowhitu’s interviews with women voyagers document ancestral responsibility and identity as expressed reasons for participation, alongside sacrifices of volunteer time; not all interviewees knew or chose to share personal genealogies.

**Cases:** OCE-C01. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S14: He Pukoa Kani ‘Āina: Kanaka Maoli approaches to mo‘okū‘auhau as methodology](https://manoa.hawaii.edu/nhpol/wp-content/uploads/2022/05/8_Wilson-Hokowhitu-2012-p.-137-143.pdf), pp.143–145. Supports: Interview method, participants’ choice about genealogy, volunteering and expressed motivations.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Direct self-reports about purpose and participation barriers; no causal productivity claim.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Interview framing explicitly invited genealogical and spiritual reflection.

**Inference limit:** Purposive qualitative evidence cannot establish prevalence, an exclusive motive, equal entry or wage effects; spiritual accounts are recorded as participants’ interpretations.

## OCE-K05

At Muldoons Trap Complex, sediment stratigraphy and AMS charcoal dates support an early excavated channel around 6,600 cal BP and a later wall-building episode around 600–800 cal BP, with an intervening hiatus.

**Cases:** OCE-C02. **Evidence:** archaeological. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S04: Dating Aboriginal stone-walled fishtraps at Lake Condah, southeast Australia](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jas.2011.09.007), pp.279–284, section 3.9, Table 1 and Discussion. Supports: Dated fill, mixed horizon, proposed construction sequence.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Summarizes authors’ site-specific chronological interpretation, not a first-invention or continuous-use claim.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Fluvial charcoal can be redeposited or have inherited age; investigators explicitly address these problems.

**Inference limit:** One excavated feature does not date every trap, establish continuous operation, or measure the regional share of food supplied.

## OCE-K06

Tae Rak’s enlarged drainage works, completed in 1954 despite recorded opposition, sharply curtailed the water conditions for lake-margin trapping; this was institutional and hydrological disabling rather than a demonstrated voluntary choice of a more productive fishery.

**Cases:** OCE-C02. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [OCE-S05: Budj Bim Cultural Landscape: World Heritage Nomination](https://whc.unesco.org/document/166283), pp.68–69. Supports: Opposition, drainage, short water retention and traps rarely functioning outside floods.
- [OCE-S06: Lake Condah Restoration Project](https://www.gunditjmirring.com/lake-condah-restoration-project), opening project-history paragraphs. Supports: Drain completion in 1954 and later restoration attempts.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** evaluated
- **mechanism chain:** Pastoral drainage authorized over objections -> rapid loss of retained lake water -> trap operating windows shrink
- **strongest rival:** Fishing fell because tastes, labor allocation or alternative livelihoods changed, independently of drainage
- **discriminating observation:** Operation varies with retained water and flood episodes; objections show fishing decline was not simply revealed preference
- **evidence sought found:** S05 describes rapid recession and flood-only functioning; S06 records failed/restored water-control attempts
- **remaining ambiguity:** No catch time series; labor and ecological change can operate alongside water loss
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Drainage served pastoral flood-control and grazing objectives; those benefits are not evaluated here.

**Inference limit:** Underlying engineering measurements and original opposition letters not acquired. Sources share project lineage.

## OCE-K07

The 2010 cultural weir restored retained water under a negotiated 52.4 m AHD setting intended to limit flooding of upstream farms; the nomination says this could reactivate most trap complexes but excluded Muldoons at that level.

**Cases:** OCE-C02. **Evidence:** institutional-synthesis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [OCE-S05: Budj Bim Cultural Landscape: World Heritage Nomination](https://whc.unesco.org/document/166283), pp.69–70. Supports: 2010 restoration, selected water level and differing trap reach.
- [OCE-S06: Lake Condah Restoration Project](https://www.gunditjmirring.com/lake-condah-restoration-project), 2004–2010 project sequence. Supports: Failed early trial, planning, hydrological studies, land return and construction.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Land return and negotiated engineering -> water retention compatible with neighboring property constraints -> uneven hydraulic reactivation
- **strongest rival:** Rainfall alone restored the lake, with formal restoration incidental
- **discriminating observation:** Retention duration before/after at comparable inflow, and trap elevation relative to negotiated water setting
- **evidence sought found:** S05 explains selection of level and excluded complex; S06 documents failed trial and successive studies
- **remaining ambiguity:** Matched hydrological time series not acquired; rainfall and gate management interact
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Restoration also relied on heavy rainfall; the weir was not sufficient by itself.

**Inference limit:** Not proof that all potentially wetted traps were operated or that precolonial output returned.

## OCE-K08

Gunditj Mirring reports supported Aboriginal recruitment, training and supervision during January–May 2010 weir construction, including construction and some administrative work.

**Cases:** OCE-C02. **Evidence:** historical-primary. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S06: Lake Condah Restoration Project](https://www.gunditjmirring.com/lake-condah-restoration-project), 2010 construction and award criteria paragraphs. Supports: Government employment support and specific task opportunities.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Records a specified employment/training programme without inferring household prosperity.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Implementer account may foreground programme successes.

**Inference limit:** No employee count, wage, duration beyond construction, retention or distributional impact is reported.

## OCE-K09

The 2000s Martu studies observe hunting-related burning within a contemporary mixed economy that includes vehicle travel and purchased food; they do not observe an unchanged precolonial regime.

**Cases:** OCE-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S07: The “fire stick farming” hypothesis: Australian Aboriginal foraging strategies, biodiversity, and anthropogenic fire mosaics](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0804757105), p.14797, Ethnoecological Context. Supports: Outstation return, vehicle-supported foraging and stores.
- [OCE-S08: Aboriginal hunting buffers climate-driven fire-size variability in Australia’s spinifex grasslands](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3387077/), Materials and Methods, fire-regime classification. Supports: 347 sample days, 1,811 bouts and 104 foragers, 2000–2010.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** Defines actual observed users and configuration.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Site use is spatially selective and does not represent all Martu territory.

**Inference limit:** No estimate of the share of all households practicing burning or its ancient form.

## OCE-K10

The 2008 study reports higher mean winter small-game hunting returns with burning than without (656 versus 478 edible kcal/hour), with 152 burning and 89 non-burning hunts; assignment was observational.

**Cases:** OCE-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** high. **Causal status:** descriptive.

- [OCE-S07: The “fire stick farming” hypothesis: Australian Aboriginal foraging strategies, biodiversity, and anthropogenic fire mosaics](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0804757105), p.14798, second Results test. Supports: Hunt sample sizes, harvest, return-rate and failure comparisons.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** descriptive-only
- **reason:** A within-study association with a stated outcome and comparator; no unqualified claim that burning caused the difference.

**Counterevidence/rival:** Hunters choose when and where to burn; hunting skill and patch condition can differ across groups.

**Inference limit:** These are foraging-return observations, not wages or a randomized treatment effect.

## OCE-K11

In the 1999–2010 study region, zones classified as Martu-burning dominated had smaller and more clustered fires and weaker rainfall-related fire-size expansion than lightning-dominated zones; this challenges a climate-only account for these landscapes.

**Cases:** OCE-C03. **Evidence:** scholarly-analysis. **Confidence:** medium. **Causal status:** mechanism-supported.

- [OCE-S08: Aboriginal hunting buffers climate-driven fire-size variability in Australia’s spinifex grasslands](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3387077/), Results, Tables 1–2; Materials and Methods. Supports: Fire-size and clustering comparison, reported within-regime rainfall associations, proxy regime definition, and burn-age (not ignition-source) ground truth.

**Diagnostic test:**

- **status:** underdetermined
- **mechanism chain:** Hypothesised: repeated small hunting fires -> patches with different fuel ages -> reduced spread under rainfall-driven fuel accumulation; selection into tracked/settled environments remains unresolved.
- **strongest rival:** Climate and substrate alone explain fire patterns; settled/tracked areas occupy different environments
- **discriminating observation:** A direct between-regime interaction contrast would test whether rainfall–fire-size slopes differ. Separating a fuel-mosaic mechanism from environmental selection additionally requires environmental comparability or a defensible causal design; separate significant/nonsignificant slopes establish neither.
- **evidence sought found:** S08 reports size/clustering and within-regime rainfall associations. Regimes are proxied by community/track distance and fires assigned by centroid; rainfall comes from one station. The 90% ground-truth result checks estimated burn age at 50 points within 500 m of a road/track, not human-versus-lightning ignition classification.
- **remaining ambiguity:** Nonrandom environment, settlement/track selection and ignition proxies remain confounds; no matched intervention or identified between-regime causal slope difference.
- **hypothesis timing:** emerged during research

**Counterevidence/rival:** Ignitions are proxied by tracks/community distance, and landscape selection is nonrandom. The seasonal fraction burnt did not differ significantly.

**Inference limit:** No automatic generalization to forests, other Indigenous burning systems, extinction rates or total ecological welfare.

## OCE-K12
